Degenerating complex variations of Hodge structure in dimension one
Claude Sabbah, Christian Schnell

Abstract




1
Introduction

1.1
Summary of the paper

1
.

In this paper, we analyze the behavior of polarized complex variations
of Hodge structure on the punctured unit disk. For
integral
variations of
Hodge structure, this analysis was carried out by Wilfried Schmid
[
Schmid
]
in
his famous article
Variation of Hodge structure: the singularities of the period
mapping
, one of the central works in modern Hodge theory. The restriction to
integral variations of Hodge structure is natural from the point of view of geometry;
it also implies that the eigenvalues of the monodromy transformation
are roots of unity, and this fact makes things technically easier.
Nevertheless, there are two reasons why we want to remove this restriction now. First, we
need the results for complex variations of Hodge structure to set up the theory of
complex mixed Hodge modules, the topic of our “Mixed Hodge Module Project”. Second,
it turns out that working with complex variations clarifies many aspects of the
theory that were somewhat obscured by the presence of an integral structure,
especially the central role played by the Hodge metric.

2
.

Analytically, the behavior of a polarized variation of Hodge structure on the
punctured disk turns out to be surprisingly simple. Before getting into any details,
let us therefore quickly highlight the two results that are, conceptually, the most important.
A complex variation of Hodge structure is a smooth vector bundle
E
with a flat
connection
d
, and a decomposition into smooth subbundles
E^{p,q}
that interacts
with the connection in a certain way. The polarization defines a smooth metric on the
bundle
E
, called the Hodge metric. In order to compare different fibers of
E
, we
use the vector space
V
of multi-valued flat sections as a reference frame; together
with the monodromy transformation
T
, it contains the same information as the flat
bundle
(E,d)
. The
first result
is that, in this reference frame, the Hodge
metric degenerates in a very simple manner: near the origin, the Hodge metric of any
nonzero multi-valued flat section grows or decays like a power of
\lvert\log\lvert t\rvert\rvert
, where
t
is the coordinate on the disk; the exponents are determined by
the monodromy transformation
T
. This means that, up to a constant, the asymptotic
behavior of the Hodge metric only depends on the underlying flat bundle.

3
.

The fact that the Hodge metric grows and decays at different rates of course
prevents the Hodge structures from having any sort of limit as
t\to 0
. We then
take the natural step of “rescaling” the Hodge metric, in order to even out these
different rates. The rescaling can be done by moving the Hodge structures by
elements of the symmetry group of the period domain; it involves a choice of
splitting for the filtration by order of growth of the Hodge metric.
The
second result
is that the rescaled Hodge structures converge to a limit, which
is again a polarized Hodge structure of the same type. In particular, the asymptotic
behavior of the Hodge metric also controls the asymptotic behavior of the Hodge
structures in the variation. Without rescaling, one gets a “limiting” mixed
Hodge structure on the vector space
V
, whose weight filtration is, up to a shift, the
filtration by order of growth of the Hodge metric. Each subquotient of the weight
filtration is again a polarized Hodge structure; the general principle at work,
coming from analysis, is that one can only expect to get a meaningful limit when
everything has the same order of growth.

1.2
What is new?

4
.

For the benefit of those readers who are already familiar with Schmid’s results, we
briefly summarize the new features of our approach. We mentioned already that we
treat arbitrary polarized complex variations of Hodge structure on the punctured
disk, without assuming that the eigenvalues of the monodromy transformation are roots
of unity. In this generality, we give new – and, we think, more conceptual – proofs
for all the major results in Schmid’s paper, such as the estimates for the rate of
growth of the Hodge norm; the existence of a limiting mixed Hodge structure; the
nilpotent orbit theorem; and a simplified (but still sufficiently powerful) version
of the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem. The results as such are of course just special cases
of Mochizuki’s monumental work on tame harmonic bundles
[
MochizukiI
]
, but we
think that it is worthwhile to have a self-contained and simple
treatment.
1
1

1

In several places, for example
[
Zucker
, p. 453]
, it is claimed
that Schmid’s results carry over to polarized variations of Hodge structure whose
monodromy is not quasi-unipotent; but despite considerable effort, we have not been
able to adapt Schmid’s proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem to this setting.

5
.

Our starting point is a direct proof for the Hodge norm estimates, based on
calculations with harmonic bundles and on a comparison with certain model variations
of Hodge structure. Technically, the crucial point is Simpson’s “basic estimate”
for the Hodge norm of the Higgs field, which is proved using Ahlfors’ lemma. In
Schmid’s paper, the Hodge
norm estimates are deduced from the orbit theorems, and therefore appear towards the
end; here, they stand at the beginning. We feel that this gives a
better conceptual explanation for the appearance of the monodromy weight filtration.
Next, we give a different proof for the nilpotent orbit theorem, based on curvature
properties of the Hodge metric and
L^{2}
-extension theorems; analytically, the key point is
that one can make the curvature of the Hodge metric either positive or negative by
multiplying by a suitable power of
\lvert\log\lvert t\rvert\rvert
; this general idea is due to
Cornalba-Griffiths
[
CG
]
and Simpson
[
SimpsonVHS
]
. The parameter dependence
of various constants, important for extending the theory to more than one dimension,
is handled by using the maximum principle. We then use the Hodge norm estimates and
the nilpotent orbit theorem to prove that the rescaled period mapping has a
well-defined limit inside the period domain.

6
.

From the convergence of the rescaled period mapping, we deduce the
existence of a limiting mixed Hodge structure; the argument is a pleasant mix of
linear algebra and representation theory, and unlike in Schmid’s paper, does not rely
on the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem. (This is useful because there is a simplified proof
for the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem by Cattani-Kaplan-Schmid
[
CKS
, §6]
, whose input
is the existence of the limiting mixed Hodge structure.) We also show that, after a
choice of splitting, the space of multi-valued flat sections becomes what we call a
“polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure”; this is the same kind of structure that one
has on the cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold. Finally, we prove a cheap version
of the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem that applies to an arbitrary splitting of the monodromy
weight filtration (instead of the
\SL(2)
-splitting). The degree of approximation is
slightly worse than in Schmid’s version, but in return, the proof is much easier. As
an application, we describe the asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric in the frame
of multi-valued flat sections, something that is only implicit in Schmid’s paper.

.

2
Overview of the results

2.1
Complex variations of Hodge structure

9
.

Since we are going to be working with complex Hodge structures, let us briefly recall the
definition. For the purposes of this text, a
Hodge structure
of weight
n

on a complex vector space
V
is a decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V^{p,q},

and a
polarization
is a hermitian pairing
Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that the decomposition is orthogonal with respect to
Q
, and such that

(-1)^{q}Q
is positive definite on the subspace
V^{p,q}
. The
Hodge norm
of
a vector
v\in V
is

\lVert v\rVert^{2}=\sum_{p+q=n}(-1)^{q}\,Q(v^{p,q},v^{p,q}).

Unlike in the case of real Hodge structures, it takes
two
filtrations to
describe an arbitrary complex Hodge structure: the usual Hodge filtration

F^{p}=F^{p}V=\bigoplus_{i\geq p}V^{i,n-i}

and the conjugate Hodge filtration

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}V=\bigoplus_{j\geq q}V^{n-j,j},

because
V^{p,q}=F^{p}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}
. When a polarization is given, the Hodge
structure is still determined by the Hodge filtration
F=F^{\bullet}V
alone: the
reason is that

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in F^{n-q+1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

We are going to use the notation
\lVert v\rVert_{F}^{2}
whenever we want to emphasize the
dependence of the Hodge norm on the Hodge filtration.

Example 1
.

A Hodge structure is called
real
if
V=V_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
for an

\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V_{\mathbb{R}}
, and if
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}=\sigma(F)
, where
\sigma\in\End_{\mathbb{R}}(V)

is the conjugation operator
\sigma(v\otimes z)=v\otimes\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
. In the real case,
one has the familiar Hodge symmetry
V^{q,p}=\sigma\bigl(V^{p,q}\bigr)
for
every
p+q=n
.

10
.

The paper contains a certain amount of analysis, and for that reason, it will be
convenient to describe variations of Hodge structure in the language of smooth vector
bundles. Let
E
be a smooth vector bundle on a complex manifold
X
. We denote
by
A^{k}(X,E)
the space of smooth
k
-forms with coefficients in
E
, and by

A^{i,j}(X,E)
the space of smooth
(i,j)
-forms with coefficients in
E
. With this
notation, a
variation of Hodge structure
of weight
n
on
E
is decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}E^{p,q}

into smooth subbundles, together with a flat connection
d\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1}(X,E)
that maps each
A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})
into the direct sum of the subspaces

A^{1,0}(X,E^{p,q})\oplus A^{1,0}(X,E^{p-1,q+1})\oplus A^{0,1}(X,E^{p,q})\oplus A^{0,1}(X,E^{p+1,q-1}).

In the analysis that follows, the most important component of the connection turns
out to be the
Higgs field
, which is the linear operator

\theta\colon A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})\to A^{1,0}(X,E^{p-1,q+1}).

A
polarization
is a hermitian pairing

Q\colon A^{0}(X,E)\otimes_{A^{0}(X)}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to A^{0}(X)

that is flat with respect to
d
, such that the expression

h(v,w)=\sum_{p+q=n}(-1)^{q}\,Q(v^{p,q},w^{p,q})\quad\text{for $v,w\in A^{0}(X,E)$}

defines a smooth hermitian metric on the bundle
E
, and the different subbundles

E^{p,q}
are orthogonal to each other with respect to
h
. This metric is called the

Hodge metric
on the bundle
E
; for the sake of clarity, we sometimes denote
it by the symbol
h_{E}
.

11
.

The relation with the more familiar holomorphic description comes from
decomposing
d=d^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
into its
(1,0)
-component
d^{\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1,0}(X,E)
and its
(0,1)
-component
d^{\prime\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{0,1}(X,E)
.
Then
d^{\prime\prime}
gives
E
the structure of a holomorphic vector bundle, which we denote by
the symbol
\mathscr{E}
; and
d^{\prime}
defines an integrable holomorphic connection
\nabla\colon\mathscr{E}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{E}
on this bundle. The condition on
d
is
saying that the Hodge bundles

F^{p}E=\bigoplus_{i\geq p}E^{i,n-i}

come from holomorphic subbundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}
, and that the connection satisfies Griffiths’
transversality relation
\nabla(F^{p}\mathscr{E})\subseteq\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}F^{p-1}\mathscr{E}
.
From this point of view, the Higgs field is simply the holomorphic operator

\theta\colon F^{p}\mathscr{E}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}F^{p-1}\mathscr{E}/F^{p}\mathscr{E}

induced by the action of the connection
\nabla
.

2.2
Multi-valued flat sections and monodromy

12
.

Our main concern in this paper is the asymptotic behavior of a polarized variation
of Hodge structure on the punctured disk. Let

\Delta^{\ast}=\bigl\{\thinspace t\in\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace 0<\lvert t\rvert<1\thinspace\bigr\}

be the punctured unit disk, and let
E
be a polarized variation of Hodge structure
on
\Delta^{\ast}
. In order to compare the Hodge structures on different fibers of the bundle

E
, we need a common reference frame; the most natural choice is the space of all
multi-valued flat sections of the flat bundle
(E,d)
.

13
.

Since the fundamental group of
\Delta^{\ast}
is cyclic, a flat bundle
(E,d)
on the
punctured disk is uniquely determined by the pair
(V,T)
, where
V
is the complex vector space of all multi-valued flat sections,
and
T\in\GL(V)
is the monodromy transformation.
To define
V
and
T
more precisely, recall that the universal covering space of

\Delta^{\ast}
is naturally the
left
half-plane

\mathbb{H}=\bigl\{\thinspace z\in\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\operatorname{Re}z<0\thinspace\bigr\},

which maps to
\Delta^{\ast}
via the exponential function
\exp\colon\mathbb{H}\to\Delta^{\ast}
. Let
V

be the complex vector space of all
multi-valued flat sections
of
E
;
concretely,
V
is defined to be the space of
d
-flat sections of the pullback
bundle
\exp^{\ast}\!E
. The polarization on
E
induces a nondegenerate hermitian form

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{V}\to\mathbb{C},

and translation by
2\pi i
induces the
monodromy operator

T=T_{s}\cdot T_{u}=T_{s}\cdot e^{2\pi i\,N}\in\GL(V).

Here
T_{s}
is diagonalizable and
N
is nilpotent, and one has

Q(T_{s}v,T_{s}w)=Q(v,w)\quad\text{and}\quad Q(Nv,w)=Q(v,Nw).

Note that our definition of the nilpotent operator
N
differs from Schmid’s
definition
[
Schmid
, (4.6)]
by a factor of
2\pi i
, because
\log T_{u}=2\pi iN
. The advantage is that this makes
N
independent of the choice of
i=\sqrt{-1}
.

14
.

The fact that the flat bundle
(E,d)
is part of a polarized variation of Hodge
structure puts the following restriction on the monodromy transformation (see

3
).

Proposition 1

(Monodromy theorem)
.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of
T
, then
\lvert\lambda\rvert=1
.

For
integral
variations of Hodge structure, it follows (because of Kronecker’s
theorem) that all eigenvalues of
T
are roots of unity. This fact is
traditionally called the “monodromy theorem”; Schmid
[
Schmid
, Lem. 4.5]

attributes it to Borel.

2.3
The Hodge norm estimates

15
.

Conceptually, the most important piece of the theory are the Hodge norm estimates,
which describe the rate of growth of the pointwise Hodge norm for multi-valued
flat sections. This result is the starting point of our treatment of the theory; in
Schmid’s work, it comes towards the end
[
Schmid
, Thm. 6.6]
. For a multi-valued flat
section
v\in V
, the Hodge metric gives us a smooth function

h(v,v)=\sum_{p+q=n}(-1)^{q}\,Q(v^{p,q},v^{p,q})\colon\mathbb{H}\to[0,\infty).

The asymptotic behavior of this function as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
turns out to be
surprisingly simple – in fact, it is completely controlled by the following elementary
construction. The nilpotent operator
N\in\End(V)
determines an increasing
filtration
W_{\bullet}=W_{\bullet}V
, called the
monodromy weight
filtration
. This filtration is uniquely characterized by the following two properties:

(a)

For every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, one has
NW_{k}\subseteq W_{k-2}
.

(b)

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the induced operator
N^{k}\colon\gr_{k}^{W}\to\gr_{-k}^{W}

is an isomorphism.

An explicit formula for the monodromy weight filtration is

W_{k}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}N^{j}\bigl(\ker N^{k+2j+1}\bigr)\quad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

The content of the Hodge norm estimates is that the monodromy weight filtration
precisely captures the asymptotic behavior of the Hodge norm.

Theorem 1

(Hodge norm estimates)
.

For
v\in V
any multi-valued flat section,

v\in W_{k}\setminus W_{k-1}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad h(v,v)\sim\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k},

as long as
\operatorname{Im}z
stays in a fixed interval.

On the punctured disk, the statement is that
h(v,v)\sim\lvert\log{\lvert t\rvert}\rvert^{k}
on
each angular sector.

16
.

The main ingredient of the proof is the following basic estimate

[
Simpson
, §2]
for the pointwise Hodge norm of the Higgs field
\theta
, viewed
as a smooth section of the induced variation of Hodge structure on the bundle

\End(E)
. It is analogous to the distance-decreasing property of period mappings
used in Schmid’s paper
[
Schmid
, Cor. 3.17]
.

Theorem 2

(Basic estimate)
.

One has the inequality

h_{\End(E)}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t},\theta_{\partial/\partial_{t}}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{0}^{2}}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}},

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
.

We use some elementary calculations with harmonic bundles, already done by Simpson

[
Simpson
, §2]
, together with the fact that
\theta
is nilpotent, to show that
the Laplacian of the smooth function
f=h_{\End(E)}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t},\theta_{\partial/\partial_{t}}\bigr)
satisfies the
differential inequality

\Delta\log f\geq 8f/C_{0}^{2}.

The basic estimate then follows by applying Ahlfors’ lemma
[
Ahlfors
]
. The
value of the constant
C_{0}
is optimal; more important than the exact value,
however, is the fact that
C_{0}
depends on nothing but the rank of the bundle
E
.

17
.

To prove the Hodge norm estimates, we first establish a special case.

Lemma 1
.

If
v\in V
is a multi-valued flat section with
Tv=\lambda v
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, then the function
\varphi_{v}=\log h(v,v)
remains bounded from above
as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
.

This is indeed a special case of the Hodge norm estimates, because
Tv=\lambda v

implies
Nv=0
, and therefore
v\in W_{0}
. Some further calculations with harmonic
bundles, together with the basic estimate, show that:

1.

The function
\varphi_{v}
is subharmonic on
\mathbb{H}
.

2.

The first derivatives of
\varphi_{v}
are bounded by a constant times

\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-1}
.

3.

One has
\varphi_{v}(z+2\pi i)=\varphi_{v}(z)
for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
.

By a simple calculus argument, these three properties are enough to conclude that

\varphi_{v}
is bounded from above as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
.

18
.

This special case is already enough to prove that the asymptotic behavior of
the Hodge norm only depends on the underlying flat bundle
(E,d)
.

Theorem 3

(Comparison theorem)
.

Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be two polarized variations of Hodge structure on the
punctured disk. If
(E_{1},d_{1})\cong(E_{2},d_{2})
as flat bundles, then the Hodge
metrics
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are mutually bounded, up to a constant, as
t\to 0
.

This follows by considering the induced variation of Hodge structure on the flat
bundle
\Hom(E_{1},E_{2})
. To prove the Hodge norm estimates in general, we then
proceed as follows. Using representation theory, we construct a “model”
variation of Hodge structure on the given flat bundle
(E,d)
, whose Hodge norm has
the correct asymptotic behavior; the comparison theorem guarantees that, up to a
constant, the Hodge norm on
E
has the same behavior. The construction of the models
is done by decomposing
V
into irreducible representations of the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and then explicitly constructing a model variation on each irreducible
representation. The construction uses the theory of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structures, which we study in some depth in
Section

5
. (See also

35
in this chapter.)

Example 2
.

In the model variation of Hodge structure coming from the standard representation of

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the Hodge metric is given by the formula

\begin{pmatrix}\lvert x\rvert+y^{2}\lvert x\rvert^{-1}&-iy\lvert x\rvert^{-1}\\
iy\lvert x\rvert^{-1}&\lvert x\rvert^{-1}\end{pmatrix},

where
z=x+iy
. Since
(1,0)\in W_{1}
and
(0,1)\in W_{-1}
, this is in
complete agreement with the Hodge norm estimates.

2.4
Period domains and period mappings

19
.

Once we understand the behavior of the Hodge metric, the next question is what
happens to the Hodge structures as
t\to 0
. The answer is best stated in the
language of period domains and period mappings. The space of multivalued flat
sections gives us a trivialization of the bundle
\exp^{\ast}E
, and so we can
consider all the Hodge structures in the variation as living on the same vector space

V
. From our polarized variation of Hodge structure
E
, we then get a holomorphic

period mapping

\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to D.

The
period domain

D
parametrizes all Hodge structures of weight
n
on

V
, with fixed Hodge numbers
\dim V^{p,q}
, that are polarized by the pairing
Q
;
a basic fact is that
D
is a homogeneous space for the real Lie group

G=\bigl\{\thinspace g\in\GL(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(gv,gw)=Q(v,w)$ for all $v,w\in V$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

The period domain is an open subset of the so-called
compact dual

\check{D}
,
which parametrizes all decreasing filtrations
F^{\bullet}
on
V
such that
\dim F^{p}=\dim V^{p,q}+\dim V^{p+1,q-1}+\dotsb
for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. The compact dual
is a projective complex manifold, and a homogeneous space for the complex Lie group

\GL(V)
; the complex structure on the period domain
D
comes from the embedding
D\subseteq\check{D}
.

20
.

The period mapping is holomorphic, and its differential is represented by the Higgs
field
\theta
; see
15
for the precise statement. Moreover, the
monodromy transformation
T\in G
is defined in such a way that

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=T\cdot\Phi(z).

The eigenvalues of
T=T_{s}T_{u}
lie on the unit circle, and so we can write
T_{s}=e^{2\pi i\,S}
, where
S\in\End(V)
is a semisimple operator with
real eigenvalues in a fixed half-open interval of length
1
; then
T=e^{2\pi i(S+N)}
. One can interpret the operator
S+N
as the residue of the connection

\nabla
on the canonical extension of the holomorphic
vector bundle
\mathscr{E}
; see
177
.

2.5
Convergence of the rescaled period mapping

21
.

Now we come to a crucial point in the argument. The Hodge norm estimates

v\in W_{k}\setminus W_{k-1}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\sim\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}

suggest “rescaling” the period mapping, in order to even out the behavior of the
Hodge norm on different parts of the weight filtration. To do this
efficiently, we choose a
splitting

H\in\End(V)
for the weight
filtration, with the following three properties:

1.

H
is semisimple, with integer eigenvalues.

2.

W_{k}=E_{k}(H)\oplus W_{k-1}
and
[H,N]=-2N
.

3.

[H,T_{s}]=0
and
Q(Hv,w)+Q(v,Hw)=0
for all
v,w\in V
.

It is not hard to show that such a splitting always exists (see

9
); in general, it is far from
unique. For a nonzero multi-valued flat section
v\in E_{k}(H)
, we then have

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\sim\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{k}{2}}v

Consequently, the Hodge norm of
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v
stays bounded as

\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
. We can achieve the same effect by moving the Hodge
structures by the operator
e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}
. Either way, the
conclusion is that the
rescaled period mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}\colon\mathbb{H}\to D,\quad\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z),

(21.1)

is invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
, and the Hodge norm
of every multi-valued flat section is uniformly bounded as
\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty
. The
two exponential factors are elements of the real Lie group
G
; this is the
reason why the rescaled period mapping stays in
D
.

22
.

The second key result is that, after rescaling, the polarized Hodge structures
now converge to a well-defined limit. As indicated by the notation, the limit
does depend on the choice of splitting: different splittings lead to different limits.

Theorem 4

(Convergence of the rescaled period mapping)
.

For any choice of splitting
H\in\End(V)
of the monodromy weight filtration as
above, the limit

e^{-N}F_{H}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)\in D

exists in the period domain. Moreover, the filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}

satisfies

T_{s}(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\quad H(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\quad N(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet-1}.

Together with the Hodge norm estimates, this is saying that a polarized variation of
Hodge structure on the punctured disk behaves in a very simple way. Namely, as
t\to 0
, the Hodge metric grows (and decays) at several different rates, and this of course
prevents the Hodge structures from approaching any sort of limit. But once we rescale
to even out the behavior of the Hodge metric,
both
the Hodge metric and the
Hodge structures converge.

2.6
The nilpotent orbit theorem

23
.

The most difficult step in proving the convergence of the rescaled period mapping is the

nilpotent orbit theorem
, an important result in its own right. Recall that
the period mapping satisfies

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=T\cdot\Phi(z),

and that we have
T=e^{2\pi i(S+N)}
, where
S\in\End(V)
is semisimple with
real eigenvalues in a fixed interval of length
<1
. Consequently, the mapping

\mathbb{H}\to\check{D},\quad z\mapsto e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z),

is invariant under
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
, and therefore descends
to a holomorphic mapping

\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\check{D},\quad\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z),

sometimes called the
untwisted period mapping
. The exponential factor

e^{-z(S+N)}
is now an element of the complex Lie group
\GL(V)
, and so the
untwisted period mapping takes values in the compact dual
\check{D}
.

24
.

The nilpotent orbit theorem has two parts. The first half is a convergence
statement: it says that
\Psi_{S}
extends holomorphically over the origin.

Theorem 5

(Nilpotent orbit theorem, convergence)
.

The holomorphic mapping

\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\check{D},\quad\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z),

extends holomorphically across the origin, and the limit filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
has the property that
(S+N)\Psi_{S}^{\bullet}(0)\subseteq\Psi_{S}^{\bullet-1}(0)
.

The limit
\Psi_{S}(0)
in the nilpotent orbit theorem depends on the choice of
S
,
but one can show that the
limiting Hodge filtration

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}e^{-zN}\Phi(z)=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}

is independent of
S
. It satisfies

T_{s}(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}\quad\text{and}\quad N(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet-1}.

The limiting Hodge filtration does not come from a polarized Hodge structure on
V

(except when
N=0
); instead, it is part of a
mixed Hodge structure
, whose
weight filtration is given by the monodromy weight filtration
W_{\bullet-n}
from
above. We are going to discuss this point in more detail in
Section

2.7

below.

25
.

We can now explain why the rescaled period mapping
\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}\colon\mathbb{H}\to D

converges to a limit in
D
. The argument needs both the Hodge norm estimates
(in
1
) and the nilpotent orbit theorem
(in
5
). The identities
[H,N]=-2N
and
[H,S]=0
can be used
to write

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{-N}\cdot e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}\,e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\,\Psi_{S}(e^{z}).

The nilpotent orbit theorem implies that
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})\to\Psi_{S}(0)
at a rate
proportional to
\lvert e^{z}\rvert=e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
. By analyzing the effect of the two
exponential factors, and by using the fact that the eigenvalues of
S
lie in an
interval of length
<1
, one deduces the existence of the limit

F_{H}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}\,e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\,\Psi_{S}(e^{z})\in\check{D}.

The filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}
is derived from the limit
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
in the
nilpotent orbit theorem in two stages:

1.

The effect of the first limit

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(0)

is to make the filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)
compatible with the eigenspace
decomposition of
T_{s}
, by projecting to the subquotients of the filtration by
decreasing eigenvalues of
S
. (Decreasing because of the minus sign in the
exponent.)

2.

The effect of the second limit

F_{H}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}

is to make the filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
compatible with the eigenspace decomposition
of
H
, by projecting to the subquotients of the weight filtration

W_{\bullet}
(which is the filtration by increasing eigenvalues of
H
).

It follows that
\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)
converges to
e^{-N}F_{H}\in\check{D}
as
\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty
. At the same time, the Hodge norm estimates tell us that the rescaled
Hodge metric is comparable to a constant metric as
\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty
. These two
facts together imply quite easily that
e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
.

26
.

The nilpotent orbit theorem also has a second half, which says that the original period
mapping is very closely approximated by the
nilpotent orbit

\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\check{D},\quad\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)=e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

In fact, the nilpotent orbit is itself the period mapping of a polarized variation of
Hodge structure (on a punctured disk of smaller radius).

Theorem 6

(Nilpotent orbit theorem, approximation)
.

There are constants
C>0
,
x_{0}<0
,
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)\in D\quad\text{and}\quad d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(z),\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)\bigr)\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
. The constants
C,x_{0}
only depend on
the base point
o\in D
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
; the integer
m
only depends on
r=\rk E
.

Here
d_{D}
is the
G
-invariant distance function on the period domain, defined by
the Hodge metric (see (
74.2
)). The distance between the period mapping

\Phi(z)
and the nilpotent orbit
\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)
is exponentially small; the constant

\delta(T)
in the exponent is the minimal distance between consecutive eigenvalues
of
T
(on the unit circle), divided by
2\pi
. For developing the theory in more
than one variable, it is very important that the values of the constants are
essentially independent of the variation of Hodge structure.

27
.

When
N=0
, the nilpotent orbit theorem is asserting that
F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in D
; in other
words, when the weight filtration is trivial,
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
is the Hodge filtration of a
polarized Hodge structure of weight
n
. When the weight filtration is not trivial,
what we get instead is a limiting mixed Hodge structure (see
8
).

Note
.

This is not true for
\Psi_{S}(0)
: even when
N=0
, the filtration

\Psi_{S}(0)
is not necessarily the Hodge filtration of a polarized Hodge
structure (see
6
).

28
.

The geometric meaning of the nilpotent orbit theorem is the following. Recall that
the operator
S\in\End(V)
depended on a choice of half-open interval
I
of length

1
. The holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}
has a
canonical extension
to a
holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}_{I}
on the disk. Up to isomorphism, it is
characterized by the fact that the holomorphic connection
\nabla
becomes a
logarithmic connection

\nabla\colon\mathscr{E}_{I}\to\Omega_{\Delta}^{1}(\log 0)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\Delta}}\mathscr{E}_{I}

whose residue
\Res_{0}\nabla
has real eigenvalues in the interval
I
. There is a
distinguished trivialization
\mathscr{E}_{I}\cong\mathscr{O}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V
in which

\nabla(v\otimes 1)=\frac{\mathit{dt}}{t}\otimes(S+N)v.

The content of
5
is that the Hodge bundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}
extends to holomorphic subbundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{I}
of the canonical extension; and
that the Higgs field

\theta\colon F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{I}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}_{I}\to\Omega_{\Delta}^{1}(\log 0)\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{\Delta}}F^{p-1}\mathscr{E}_{I}/F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{I}

has a logarithmic pole at
t=0
, with residue
S+N
. The content of

6
is that if we replace the extended Hodge
bundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{I}
by trivial subbundles with fiber
\Psi_{S}^{p}(0)\subseteq V
,
then we still get a polarized variation of Hodge structure of weight
n
, at least on
a punctured disk of a fixed smaller radius.

29
.

Our proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem is quite different from Schmid’s original
argument
[
Schmid
, §8]
. It uses in a crucial way the curvature properties of the
Hodge metric. A short calculation (see
2
) shows that the
curvature tensor of the Hodge metric
h_{E}
on the bundle
E^{p,q}
is given by

\Theta=-(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)
, where

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})\to A^{1,0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p-1,q+1})

\displaystyle\theta^{\ast}

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})\to A^{0,1}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p+1,q-1})

are the Higgs field and its adjoint. (There are similar formulas for the curvature
tensor on the Hodge bundles
F^{p}E
and on the quotient bundles
E/F^{p}E
.)
Consequently, the expression

h_{E}\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\bigr)=h_{E}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u,\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u\bigr)-h_{E}\bigl(\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u,\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u\bigr)

is in general neither positive nor negative definite. However, because of the basic
estimate (in
2
), any metric of the form

h_{E}\cdot\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\quad\text{with $a\in\mathbb{R}$ and $b\in\mathbb{Z}$}

will have positive curvature for
b\gg 0
, and negative curvature for
b\ll 0
.
This insight already appears in Simpson’s work
[
SimpsonVHS
, §10]
, who
attributes it to the important paper by Cornalba and Griffiths
[
CG
, p. 29]
. We
use both of these properties in our proof.

30
.

Let us now outline the steps in our proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem.
The nilpotent orbit theorem can also be deduced from Mochizuki’s work on harmonic bundles

[
Mochizuki-nilpotent
]
; another analytic proof using
L^{2}
-estimates has recently
been given by Deng
[
Deng
]
.

1.

The bundle
\End(E)
inherits a polarized variation of Hodge structure of
weight
0
. The Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
is a holomorphic section
of
\End(E)^{-1,1}
, and the basic estimate tells us that its
pointwise Hodge norm is bounded by a constant multiple of
\lvert t\rvert^{-2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{-2}
. The first step is to construct a lifting of
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
to a holomorphic section
\vartheta
of the
Hodge bundle
F^{-1}\End(E)
, whose
L^{2}
-norm is controlled by an inequality
of the form

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h_{\End(E)}(\vartheta,\vartheta)\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq C

where
a>-2
and
b\gg 0
. This uses Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates, for
which we include an elementary proof (in
Section

7.1
); the key point
is that the metric
h_{\End(E)}\cdot\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}
has positive
curvature for
b\gg 0
. Using
L^{2}
-estimates to extend holomorphic bundles
goes back to Cornalba-Griffiths
[
CG
]
, and in the setting of harmonic
bundles, to Simpson
[
SimpsonVHS
]
.

2.

Pulling back to
\mathbb{H}
gives us a holomorphic mapping
\vartheta\colon\mathbb{H}\to\End(V)
that satisfies
\vartheta(z+2\pi i)=T\vartheta(z)T^{-1}
.
After untwisting, we get

B\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\End(V),\quad B(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\vartheta(z)e^{z(S+N)}.

Since the eigenvalues of
S
lie in an interval of length
<1
, the

L^{2}
-estimate from the first step can be used to show that
B
is
square-integrable on a punctured neighborhood of the origin, and therefore extends
holomorphically to the entire disk.

3.

We can now describe the differential of the untwisted period mapping

\Psi_{S}
using holomorphic data.
The holomorphic tangent space to the complex manifold
\check{D}
at the point

\Phi(z)
is isomorphic to
\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}
, and the
differential of the period mapping
\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\check{D}
is equal to

\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}.

A short calculation with derivatives shows that the differential of the mapping

z\mapsto\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is therefore equal to

\displaystyle e^{-z(S+N)}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}e^{z(S+N)}

\displaystyle-(S+N)

\displaystyle\equiv B(e^{z})

\displaystyle-(S+N)\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}.

The point is that the operator on the right-hand side is holomorphic.

4.

Let
g\colon\mathbb{H}\to\GL(V)
be the unique holomorphic solution of the
ordinary differential equation

g^{\prime}(z)=\Bigl(B(e^{z})-(S+N)\Bigr)g(z),

subject to the initial condition
g(-1)=\id
. A short calculation shows that

g(z)^{-1}\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
must be constant, which means that

\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=g(z)\cdot\Psi_{S}(e^{-1}).

The differential equation has a
regular
singular point at
t=0
, and by the
basic theory of Fuchsian differential equations, one has

g(z)=M(e^{z})\cdot e^{zA},

with
M\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\GL(V)
meromorphic and
A\in\End(V)
constant. Since
the untwisted period mapping
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is single-valued, it follows that

\Psi_{S}(t)=M(t)\cdot\Psi_{S}(e^{-1}),

and because
\check{D}
is a projective complex manifold, this is enough to conclude
that
\Psi_{S}
extends holomorphically across the origin. (The close
relationship between the nilpotent orbit theorem and regularity was already
observed by Griffiths and Schmid, see
[
GS
, §9a]
and the references cited
there.)

5.

The remainder of the argument consists in deriving effective estimates
for the rate of convergence. Let
P_{\lambda}
denote the projection to the
eigenspace
E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
. The fact that
\Psi_{S}(t)
converges as
t\to 0

can be used to show that

\displaystyle\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-N^{-1,1}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq-2}\lVert N^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq-1}\lVert P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
; the exponent
m
only depends on
r=\rk E
, but we
have no control over the value of the constant
C
. We then use the
fact that the metric
h_{\End(E)}\cdot(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}
has negative
curvature for
b\ll 0
, together with the maximum principle for subharmonic functions, to deduce
that the above estimates actually hold on any half-plane of the form
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x<0
, with a constant
C
that only depends on
x
,
r
, and on the
minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
.

6.

Finally, we consider the curve

[0,\infty)\to\check{D},\quad x\mapsto e^{xN}\Phi(z-x),

joining the two points
\Phi(z)
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)
. Its derivative is
controlled by the inequalities in the previous step.
After integration, we obtain a bound for the distance in
\check{D}
between these
two points, and together with the Hodge norm estimates, this gives us the
desired bound for their distance in
D
, provided that
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
is
sufficiently large.

31
.

The estimates in the fifth step are saying that, modulo
F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}
, the
Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
converges to the
nilpotent operator
N
at a rate that is exponential in
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
. Moreover, the
eigenspace decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}E_{\lambda}(T_{s})

becomes orthogonal in the limit, at a rate that is exponential in
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
. Since the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
is compatible with this
decomposition, both of these facts are of course also contained in the statement of
the nilpotent orbit theorem itself.

2.7
The limiting mixed Hodge structure

32
.

Probably the most widely known result from Schmid’s paper
[
Schmid
, §6]
is
that, in the limit, a polarized variation of Hodge structure on the punctured disk
gives rise to a mixed Hodge structure on
V
. The weight filtration of this
“limiting” mixed Hodge structure is the shifted monodromy weight filtration

W_{\bullet-n}
, and the Hodge filtration is the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
.
Schmid obtained this result as a consequence of his
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem.

33
.

We take a somewhat different approach here, and deduce the existence of the limiting
mixed Hodge structure directly from the convergence of the rescaled period mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}\colon\mathbb{H}\to D
. In fact, we prove that the vector space
V
is an example
of a
polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
; this is the same kind of structure
that one finds on the cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold. The proof is
elementary and only uses linear algebra and some basic representation theory of the
Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, but no further analysis.

34
.

Let us briefly recall how
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
enters into the picture. Recall that this Lie
algebra is
3
-dimensional, and is generated by the three matrices

\mathsf{H}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

subject to the relations
[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X}]=2\mathsf{X}
,
[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y}
, and

[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}]=\mathsf{H}
. In any finite-dimensional representation, the operator
\mathsf{H}

acts semisimply with integer eigenvalues, and the eigenspace
E_{k}(\mathsf{H})
are called
the
weight spaces
. The splitting
H
that we chose in
21

uniquely determines a representation

\rho\colon\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to\End(V)

of this Lie algebra such that
\rho(\mathsf{H})=H
and
\rho(\mathsf{Y})=-N
; the minus
sign is dictated by our sign conventions for Hodge structures. It lifts to a
representation of the Lie group
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; in particular, we have the
Weil
element

\mathsf{w}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

which can also be written as
\mathsf{w}=e^{\mathsf{X}}e^{-\mathsf{Y}}e^{\mathsf{X}}
. The Weil element
is a sort of linear algebra analogue of the Hodge
\ast
-operator on a compact
Kähler manifold: it satisfies
\mathsf{w}^{2}=(-1)^{\mathsf{H}}
, and induces isomorphisms
between the two weight spaces
E_{\pm k}(\mathsf{H})
. Using a dagger to denote the adjoint
of an operator with respect to the nondegenerate pairing
Q
, it is easy to see that

\mathsf{H}^{{\dagger}}=-\mathsf{H}
,
\mathsf{Y}^{{\dagger}}=\mathsf{Y}
,
\mathsf{X}^{{\dagger}}=\mathsf{X}
, and hence also

\mathsf{w}^{{\dagger}}=\mathsf{w}
.

35
.

We can now define
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures and their polarizations. The
definition is modeled on the cohomology
H^{n+\bullet}(X,\mathbb{C})
of
an
n
-dimensional compact Kähler manifold, but perhaps surprisingly, it is equally
relevant in our context. An
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
on a finite-dimensional
complex vector space
V
is a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
V
with the
following properties:

(a)

Each weight space
V_{k}=E_{k}(\mathsf{H})
has a Hodge structure of weight
n+k
;
the integer
n
is called the
weight
of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure.

(b)

The two operators

\mathsf{X}\colon V_{k}\to V_{k+2}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Y}\colon V_{k}\to V_{k-2}(-1)

are morphisms of Hodge structures.

In the literature, this kind of structure is also called a “Hodge-Lefschetz
structure”
[
Cattani
]
or an “
\mathbb{R}
-split mixed Hodge structure”
[
CKS
]
,
but we feel that the name “
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure” is more descriptive.

36
.

The polarization on the cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold is usually
discussed only for the primitive cohomology (which is the kernel of
\mathsf{Y}
, in our
notation). The standard definition is that, for every
k\geq 0
, the hermitian
pairing

Q\circ\bigl(\id\otimes\mathsf{X}^{k}\bigr)\colon V_{-k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

should polarize the Hodge structure of weight
n-k
on the primitive subspace
\ker\mathsf{Y}\colon V_{-k}\to V_{-(k+2)}
. Since
\mathsf{w}
acts on the primitive subspace as

\frac{1}{k!}\mathsf{X}^{k}
, one can define polarizations more compactly with the help of
the Weil element. Following Deligne
[
Deligne-signs
]
, we say that a hermitian
form

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

is a
polarization
of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
V
if it satisfies the four
identities

\displaystyle Q\circ(\mathsf{H}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=-Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{H}),

\displaystyle Q\circ(\mathsf{X}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{X}),

\displaystyle Q\circ(\mathsf{Y}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{Y}),

\displaystyle Q\circ(\mathsf{w}\otimes\id)

\displaystyle=Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{w}),

and if
Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{w})
polarizes the Hodge structure of weight
n+k

on each
V_{k}
.

37
.

Because the Weil element
\mathsf{w}
acts as
\frac{1}{k!}(-\mathsf{Y})^{k}
on the “other”
primitive subspace
\ker\mathsf{X}\colon V_{k}\to V_{k+2}
for
k\geq 0
, an equivalent
formulation is that

Q\circ\bigl(\id\otimes(-\mathsf{Y})^{k}\bigr)\colon V_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

should polarize the Hodge structure of weight
n+k
on
\ker\mathsf{X}
. Since
\rho(-\mathsf{Y})=N
in our case, this matches Schmid’s definition
[
Schmid
, Lem. 6.4]
.

38
.

We now describe how the limiting
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure on
V
comes about.
The convergence of the rescaled period mapping (in
4
)
gives us a filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}
with the following properties:

(a)

The filtration
e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
is the Hodge filtration of a Hodge structure
of weight
n
, polarized by the pairing
Q
.

(b)

One has
N(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet-1}
,

H(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet}
, and
T_{s}(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet}
.

From these properties, we deduce by a mix of representation theory and linear algebra
that
F_{H}
is the “total” Hodge filtration of an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n

on
V
.

Theorem 7

(Limiting
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure)
.

The vector space
V
has a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
, whose
Hodge filtration is
F_{H}
. Concretely, this means the following:

1.

Each weight space
V_{k}=E_{k}(H)
has a Hodge structure of
weight
n+k
, whose Hodge filtration is
V_{k}\cap F_{H}
.

2.

The operator
N\colon V_{k}\to V_{k-2}(-1)
is a morphism of
Hodge structures.

3.

The Hodge structure on each primitive subspace
\ker N^{k+1}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k-2}
is polarized by the hermitian pairing
Q(-,N^{k}-)
.

4.

The operator
T_{s}
is an endomorphism of the polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure.

39
.

Recall from
25
that the filtration
F_{H}
is obtained from
the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
by the following procedure:
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
induces a
filtration on each subquotient
W_{k}/W_{k-1}
, and
V_{k}\cap F_{H}
is gotten by pulling
back along the isomorphism
V_{k}\cong W_{k}/W_{k-1}
. The fact that each
weight space
V_{k}
has a Hodge structure of weight
n+k
therefore means that

W_{\bullet-n}
is the weight filtration of a mixed Hodge structure

[
Schmid
, Thm. 6.16]
.

Theorem 8

(Limiting mixed Hodge structure)
.

The vector space
V
has a mixed Hodge structure, with weight filtration

W_{\bullet-n}
and Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
; the associated graded of this mixed
Hodge structure is a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
with
\mathsf{Y}=-N
and polarization
Q
. The two operators

N\colon V\to V(-1)\quad\text{and}\quad T_{s}\colon V\to V

are morphisms of mixed Hodge structures.

The
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure depends on the choice of splitting
H
; the limiting
mixed Hodge structure is independent of it. Without choosing a splitting, one gets a
canonical polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure on the associated graded

\gr_{\bullet}^{W}
with respect to the monodromy weight filtration. In the terminology
of
[
CKS
, Def. 2.26]
, the limiting mixed Hodge structure is therefore
“polarized” by the pairing
Q
and the nilpotent operator
N
.

2.8
Asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric

40
.

Finally, we describe the asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric
h(v,v^{\prime})
where

v,v^{\prime}\in V
are two multi-valued flat sections. The Hodge norm estimates cover the
case
v=v^{\prime}
, but something new happens when
v\neq v^{\prime}
, namely that the Hodge
metric
h(v,v^{\prime})
grows more slowly than expected as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
. This
result does not appear as such in Schmid’s paper, but it is implicitly contained in
the statement about Siegel sets in
[
Schmid
, Cor. 5.29]
.

Theorem 9

(Asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric)
.

For any two multi-valued flat sections
v,v^{\prime}\in V
, there is a
constant
C(v,v^{\prime})>0
such that

\bigl\lvert\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle_{\Phi(z)}\bigr\rvert\leq C(v,v^{\prime})\cdot\min\Bigl(\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2},\lVert v^{\prime}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\Bigr)

for all sufficiently large values of
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
.

An equivalent formulation is that if
v\in V_{k}
and
v^{\prime}\in V_{k^{\prime}}
, then

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle_{\Phi(z)}=O\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{\min(k,k^{\prime})}\bigr)

which is typically much smaller than the
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}(k+k^{\prime})}
one
would expect based on the Hodge norm estimates. We can also restate the theorem in
terms of the rescaled period mapping from (
21.1
): the result is that
if
v\in V_{k}
and
v^{\prime}\in V_{k^{\prime}}
belong to different weight spaces, then

\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)}=O\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}\lvert k-k^{\prime}\rvert}\bigr).

(40.1)

Since different weight spaces are orthogonal to each other in the Hodge structure

F_{\sharp}=e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
, this is not unexpected; but not only are the weight spaces
becoming orthogonal in the limit, they are doing so at a rate that depends on the
difference between their weights.

41
.

We end the introduction with a brief sketch of the proof. Up to an error of size

e^{-\varepsilon\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
, which is negligible in this context, the nilpotent orbit
theorem allows us to assume that the period mapping has the form
\Phi(z)=e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. The rescaled period mapping then looks like

\hat{\Phi}_{H}(z)=e^{-N}\cdot e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

This expression only depends on
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
; since we are interested in its
behavior as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
, we make the substitution
u=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, so that

\hat{\Phi}_{H}\bigl(1/u^{2}\bigr)=e^{-N}\cdot e^{-(\log u)H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

Recall from
25
that we have

\lim_{u\to 0}e^{-(\log u)H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=F_{H},

(41.1)

and hence that
\hat{\Phi}_{H}\bigl(1/u^{2}\bigr)
converges to
F_{\sharp}=e^{-N}F_{H}

as
u\to 0
.

42
.

To get a handle on the rate of convergence, we prove the following cheap
version of the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem
. Schmid’s original result

[
Schmid
, Thm. 5.13]
has two functions: it provides a distinguished splitting

H
for the weight filtration, called the “
\SL(2)
-splitting” (or “canonical
splitting”); and it gives a very precise description, using power series with values
in the real group
G
, for the rate of convergence of (
41.1
). Here we
focus on the second aspect, using an arbitrary splitting
H
chosen as in

139
.

Theorem 10

(Cheap
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem)
.

There is a convergent power series

g\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to G,\quad g(u)=\id+ug_{1}+u^{2}g_{2}+\dotsb,

defined for some
\varepsilon>0
, such that for
0<\lvert u\rvert<\varepsilon
, one has

e^{-N}\cdot e^{-(\log u)H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=g(u)^{-1}\cdot e^{-N}F_{H}.

The coefficients
g_{n}\in\End(V)
of the series are
(S_{n}\oplus S_{n-2}\oplus\dotsb)
-isotypical with respect to the action of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
\End(V)
.

The point is that we can then write the rescaled period mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{H}\bigl(1/u^{2}\bigr)=g(u)^{-1}\cdot F_{\sharp}

in terms of the real group
G
. Now suppose that
v\in V_{k}
and
v^{\prime}\in V_{k^{\prime}}
.
Since different weight spaces are orthogonal in the Hodge structure
F_{\sharp}
, and since

10
gives us some control on how much the coefficients
g_{n}
can
change the weight of a given vector, it is an easy matter to deduce that

\displaystyle\langle v,v^{\prime}\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{H}(1/u^{2})}

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle g(u)\cdot v,\,g(u)\cdot v^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{F_{\sharp}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m+n\geq\lvert k-k^{\prime}\rvert}u^{m+n}\,\bigl\langle g_{m}v,\,g_{n}v^{\prime}\bigr\rangle_{F_{\sharp}}=O\bigl(u^{\lvert k-k^{\prime}\rvert}\bigr),

which is equivalent to (
40.1
).

43
.

The original proof of the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem in
[
Schmid
, §9]
is very difficult;
and even the simplified argument in
[
CKS
, §6]
(assuming the limiting mixed Hodge
structure) is quite involved. By contrast, our proof of the cheap
\SL(2)
-orbit
theorem is short and elementary. The crucial point is that

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=h\cdot F_{H},

where
h=\id+h_{1}+h_{2}+\dotsb\in\GL(V)
has the property that

h_{n}\in E_{-n}(\ad H)\cap\ker(\ad N).

Such an element
h
can be constructed by comparing Deligne’s decomposition of the
limiting mixed Hodge structure (whose Hodge filtration is
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
) and the Hodge
decomposition of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure (whose Hodge filtration in
F_{H})
, and
using the fact that, up to a Tate twist,
N
is a morphism in both structures.

10
follows from this by a formal argument. The crucial fact from
the representation theory of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
is that the tensor product

S_{k}\otimes S_{\ell}\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\min(k,\ell)}S_{k+\ell-2i}

of two irreducible representation decomposes in a very simple way.

3
Variations of Hodge structure and harmonic bundles

3.1
Harmonic bundles and harmonic metrics

44
.

Since we are going to do a certain amount of analysis, it will be convenient to
describe variations of Hodge structure in terms of smooth
vector bundles. Let
E
be a smooth vector bundle on a complex manifold
X
. We denote
by
A^{k}(X,E)
the space of smooth
k
-forms with coefficients in
E
, and by

A^{i,j}(X,E)
the space of smooth
(i,j)
-forms with coefficients in
E
.

Definition 1
.

Let
E
be a smooth vector bundle on a complex manifold
X
.
A
variation of Hodge structure
of weight
n
on
E
is a decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}E^{p,q}

into smooth subbundles, together with a flat connection
d\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1}(X,E)
that maps each
A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})
into the direct sum of the subspaces

A^{1,0}(X,E^{p,q})\oplus A^{1,0}(X,E^{p-1,q+1})\oplus A^{0,1}(X,E^{p,q})\oplus A^{0,1}(X,E^{p+1,q-1}).

45
.

The definition gives us a decomposition
d=\partial+\theta+\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}+\theta^{\ast}
into
four operators

\displaystyle\partial

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})\to A^{1,0}(X,E^{p,q})

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})\to A^{1,0}(X,E^{p-1,q+1})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})\to A^{0,1}(X,E^{p,q})

\displaystyle\theta^{\ast}

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})\to A^{1,0}(X,E^{p+1,q-1}).

It is easy to see that
\partial
and
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
are again connections of type
(1,0)
and

(0,1)
, whereas
\theta
and
\theta^{\ast}
are linear over smooth functions. As usual,
we can extend all four operators to the space of all
E
-valued forms

A^{\ast}(X,E)=\bigoplus_{i,j,p,q}A^{i,j}(X,E^{p,q})

by enforcing the Leibniz rule. With respect to the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-grading on
this space,
\partial
is an operator of bidegree
(1,0)\otimes(0,0)
, because it maps

A^{i,j}(X,E^{p,q})
into
A^{i+1,j}(X,E^{p,q})
; similarly,
\theta
has bidegree

(1,0)\otimes(-1,1)
,
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
has bidegree
(0,1)\otimes(0,0)
, and
\theta^{\ast}
has bidegree
(0,1)\otimes(1,-1)
. By decomposing the
identity
d^{2}=0
according to bidegree, one can deduce the following set of
identities for these four operators.

Lemma 2
.

The four operators
\partial
,
\theta
,
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
, and
\theta^{\ast}
satisfy the
following identities:

\displaystyle\partial^{2}=\theta^{2}=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}^{2}=(\theta^{\ast})^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial\theta+\theta\partial=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\theta+\theta\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}=\partial\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\partial

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}+\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\partial+\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta

\displaystyle=0

46
.

In order to define polarizations, we consider a hermitian pairing

Q\colon A^{0}(X,E)\otimes_{A^{0}(X)}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to A^{0}(X).

It takes as input two smooth sections of the bundle
E
, and outputs a smooth
function on
X
. We require that
Q
is hermitian symmetric and

A^{0}(X)
-linear in its first argument, meaning that for
v,w\in A^{0}(X,E)

and
f\in A^{0}(X)
, one has

Q(w,v)=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\quad\text{and}\quad Q(f\cdot v,w)=f\cdot Q(v,w).

Of course, it follows that
Q
is also conjugate
A^{0}(X)
-linear in its second argument.
We say that
Q
is
flat
with respect to the connection
d
if

dQ(v,w)=Q(dv,w)+Q(v,dw)\quad\text{for $v,w\in A^{0}(X,E)$.}

Definition 2
.

Let
E
be a variation of Hodge structure of weight
n
on a complex manifold
X
.
A
polarization
is a flat hermitian pairing

Q\colon A^{0}(X,E)\otimes_{A^{0}(X)}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to A^{0}(X),

such that the formula

h(v,w)=\sum_{p+q=n}(-1)^{q}\,Q(v^{p,q},w^{p,q})\quad\text{for $v,w\in A^{0}(X,E)$}

defines a smooth hermitian metric on the bundle
E
, and the subbundles

E^{p,q}
are orthogonal to each other with respect to this metric.

The hermitian metric in the definition is called the
Hodge metric
; it is the
most important object in the theory. For the sake of clarity, we sometimes denote the
Hodge metric on
E
by the symbol
h_{E}
.

47
.

The fact that
Q
is flat with respect to
d
gives us the following additional
information about the operators
\theta
and
\theta^{\ast}
.

Lemma 3
.

With respect to the Hodge metric on
E
, the operator
\theta^{\ast}
is the adjoint of
the operator
\theta
, in the sense that

h(\theta v,w)=h(v,\theta^{\ast}w)\quad\text{for $v,w\in A^{0}(X,E)$.}

Proof.

Let
v\in A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})
and
w\in A^{0}(X,E^{p-1,q+1})
be two arbitrary sections.
Then
Q(v,w)=0
, and therefore

0=dQ(v,w)=Q(dv,w)+Q(v,dw)=Q(\theta v,w)+Q(v,\theta^{\ast}w)

because all other terms have the wrong type. But then

h(\theta v,w)=(-1)^{q+1}Q(\theta v,w)=(-1)^{q}Q(v,\theta^{\ast}w)=h(v,\theta^{\ast}w).

Since the decomposition of
E
is orthogonal with respect to
h
, the result
follows.
∎

48
.

Let us briefly recall the relation with the (more familiar) holomorphic
description of variations of Hodge structure. As usual, we decompose the flat
connection into
d=d^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
, where
d^{\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1,0}(X,E)
is a connection of type
(1,0)
, and
d^{\prime\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{0,1}(X,E)
is a connection of type
(0,1)
. The identity
d^{2}=0
then becomes

(d^{\prime})^{2}=d^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}d^{\prime}=(d^{\prime\prime})^{2}=0
. The operator
d^{\prime\prime}
gives
E
the structure
of a holomorphic vector bundle, which we denote by the symbol
\mathscr{E}
, and the
operator
d^{\prime}
defines a flat holomorphic connection

\nabla\colon\mathscr{E}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{E}.

Moreover, for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Hodge bundle

F^{p}E=E^{p,q}\oplus E^{p+1,q-1}\oplus E^{p+2,q-2}\oplus\dotsb

is preserved by the operator
d^{\prime\prime}
, and therefore defines a holomorphic subbundle

F^{p}\mathscr{E}
. Since
d^{\prime}=\partial+\theta
, we also get Griffiths’ transversality condition

\nabla(F^{p}\mathscr{E})\subseteq\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}F^{p-1}\mathscr{E}.

From this perspective, we can see the other operators
by looking at the quotient bundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}
. The operator
\bar{\partial}

gives each smooth bundle
E^{p,q}
the structure of a holomorphic vector bundle

\mathscr{E}^{p,q}
, and because
d^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}+\theta^{\ast}
,

\mathscr{E}^{p,q}\cong F^{p}\mathscr{E}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}

are isomorphic as holomorphic vector bundles. Since
\bar{\partial}\theta+\theta\bar{\partial}=0
, the operator
\theta
defines a morphism of holomorphic vector bundles

\theta\colon\mathscr{E}^{p,q}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{E}^{p-1,q+1},

and one checks that the following diagram is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{E}^{p,q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}\mathscr{E}^{p-1,q+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{p}\mathscr{E}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes_{\mathscr{O}_{X}}F^{p-1}\mathscr{E}/F^{p}\mathscr{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla}

The two remaining operators
\theta^{\ast}
and
\partial
in the decomposition of
d

have the following interpretation:
\theta^{\ast}
is the adjoint of the Higgs field with
respect to the Hodge metric (
3
), and
\partial
is the Chern
connection on the bundle
E^{p,q}
.

49
.

We are now going to do some computations with the Hodge metric; these become
easier if we use the language of harmonic bundles. In fact, a polarized variation of Hodge
structure is an example of a harmonic bundle, with the harmonic metric being the
Hodge metric. We briefly recall the general definition. Let
E
be a smooth vector
bundle on a complex manifold
X
, let
d=d^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
be a flat connection on
E
, and let
h
be a hermitian metric on
E
. As
in the construction of the Chern connection, there are unique operators

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1,0}(X,E)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{0,1}(X,E)

with the property that
d^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
are metric connections on

E
. Then

\partial=\frac{1}{2}(d^{\prime}+\delta^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}=\frac{1}{2}(d^{\prime\prime}+\delta^{\prime\prime})

are again connections of type
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, and

\theta=\frac{1}{2}(d^{\prime}-\delta^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\ast}=\frac{1}{2}(d^{\prime\prime}-\delta^{\prime\prime})

are linear over smooth functions. The metric
h
is called a
harmonic
metric
, and the triple
(E,d,h)
is called a
harmonic bundle
, if the
identity
(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}+\theta)^{2}=0
holds. This is equivalent to the identities in

2
. The associated Higgs bundle is
E
, with the
holomorphic structure defined by
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
, and with Higgs field
\theta
. As suggested
by the notation, in the case of a polarized variation of Hodge structure,
\partial
,
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
,

\theta
, and
\theta^{\ast}
agree with the operators that we had defined above.

Lemma 4
.

Let
E
be a variation of Hodge structure with polarization
Q
. Then

d^{\prime}=\partial+\theta,\quad d^{\prime\prime}=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}+\theta^{\ast},\quad\delta^{\prime}=\partial-\theta,\quad\delta^{\prime\prime}=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}-\theta^{\ast},

and so
E
is a harmonic bundle and the Hodge metric
h
is a harmonic metric. The
associated Higgs bundle is the graded holomorphic vector bundle

\bigoplus_{p+q=n}\mathscr{E}^{p,q}

with Higgs field
\theta\colon\mathscr{E}^{p,q}\to\Omega_{X}^{1}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{p-1,q+1}
.

Proof.

The claim is that the two operators
(\partial+\bar{\partial})\pm(\theta-\theta^{\ast})

are metric connections. For any two sections
v,w\in A^{0}(X,E^{p,q})
, we have

dh(v,w)=(-1)^{q}Q(dv,w)+(-1)^{q}Q(v,dw)=h\bigl((\partial+\bar{\partial})v,w\bigr)+h\bigl(v,(\partial+\bar{\partial})w\bigr),

because all other terms have the wrong type. Therefore
\partial+\bar{\partial}
is a
metric connection on
E
. Since
\theta^{\ast}
is the adjoint of
\theta
with respect
to
h
, it follows that
(\partial+\bar{\partial})\pm(\theta-\theta^{\ast})
are also
metric connections. The identities in
2
imply that

(\bar{\partial}+\theta)^{2}=0
, and so
E
is a harmonic bundle with harmonic metric

h
. The remaining assertions are clear from the discussion in the previous
paragraph.
∎

50
.

The Hodge metric
h
gives us a hermitian metric on the holomorphic vector bundle

\mathscr{E}
and on the subbundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}
. It is not hard to derive a formula for the
curvature of this metric, using the identities among the operators
\partial
,

\bar{\partial}
,
\theta
, and
\theta^{\ast}
; Schmid does this, in a more cumbersome
way, in
[
Schmid
, §7]
. The curvature of the Hodge bundles will
be important later, in
Section

7
, when we prove the nilpotent orbit
theorem. We need one more piece of notation to state the result: we denote by

\pi^{p,q}\colon E\to E^{p,q}

the projection operator to the direct summand
E^{p,q}
; of course,

\pi^{p,q}
is also orthogonal projection with respect to the Hodge metric.

Proposition 2
.

Let
E
be a variation of Hodge structure with polarization
Q
.

(a)

On the holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}
, the curvature operator of the Hodge
metric
h
is equal to
-2(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)
.

(b)

On the holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}^{p,q}
, the curvature operator of the Hodge
metric
h
is equal to
-(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)
.

(c)

On the holomorphic quotient bundle
\mathscr{E}/F^{p}\mathscr{E}
, the curvature
operator of the induced metric is equal to
-2(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)+\theta\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}
.

Proof.

Since the holomorphic structure on
\mathscr{E}
is defined by the operator
d^{\prime\prime}
, the
Chern connection of
h
is nothing but the metric connection
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}=\partial+\bar{\partial}-\theta+\theta^{\ast}
. A short computation shows that the
curvature operator is

(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime})^{2}=(\partial+\bar{\partial}-\theta+\theta^{\ast})^{2}=(\partial\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\partial)-(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)=-2(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta),

using the identities in
2
. This proves (a). The proof
of (b) is very similar. The holomorphic structure on
\mathscr{E}^{p,q}
is defined by
the operator
\bar{\partial}
, and so the Chern connection of
h
is the metric connection

\partial+\bar{\partial}
, and the curvature operator is

(\partial+\bar{\partial})^{2}=(\partial\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\partial)=-(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta).

The derivation of (c) is slightly more complicated, because the operator
d^{\prime\prime}=\bar{\partial}+\theta^{\ast}
does not preserve the individual summands
E^{p,q}
. We can
correct this with the help of the projection
\pi^{p,q}\colon E\to E^{p,q}
.
Note that
\pi^{p,q}
commutes with
\partial
and
\bar{\partial}
, whereas

\pi^{p,q}\theta=\theta\pi^{p+1,q-1}\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{p,q}\theta^{\ast}=\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}.

Now for the proof. The Hodge decomposition is orthogonal with respect to the Hodge
metric, and so, as smooth vector bundles with metric,

E/F^{p}E\cong E^{p-1,q+1}\oplus E^{p-2,q+2}\oplus E^{p-3,q+3}\oplus\dotsb

(50.1)

Under this isomorphism, the holomorphic structure on
E/F^{p}E
is represented by
the operator
d^{\prime\prime}-\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}
, which preserves the
bundle on the right-hand side. A short computation shows that the modified
operator

\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}-\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}=\partial+\bar{\partial}-\theta+\theta^{\ast}-\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}

is a metric connection, and therefore equal to the Chern connection.
It follows that the curvature operator of the induced metric is

\displaystyle\bigl(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle-\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}\bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime})^{2}-(\partial+\bar{\partial}-\theta+\theta^{\ast})\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}-\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1}(\partial+\bar{\partial}-\theta+\theta^{\ast})

\displaystyle=-2(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta)+\theta\theta^{\ast}\pi^{p-1,q+1},

using the identities in
2
and the fact that

\pi^{p-1,q+1}\theta=\theta\pi^{p,q}=0
on the vector bundle in
(
50.1
).
∎

3.2
Multivalued flat sections and the Hodge metric

51
.

Now we are ready to start doing some computations specifically for polarized
variations of Hodge structure over the punctured disk. As usual, let

\Delta^{\ast}=\bigl\{\thinspace t\in\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace 0<\lvert t\rvert<1\thinspace\bigr\}

be the punctured unit disk, with coordinate
t
. Let
E
be a polarized variation of
Hodge structure of weight
n
on
\Delta^{\ast}
. We are going to denote by
V
the space of
all multi-valued flat sections of
(E,d)
. More precisely, we use the letter

\mathbb{H}=\bigl\{\thinspace z\in\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\operatorname{Re}z<0\thinspace\bigr\}

for the
left
half-plane; the exponential function
\exp\colon\mathbb{H}\to\Delta^{\ast}
makes it
into the universal covering space of
\Delta^{\ast}
, independently of the choice of
i=\sqrt{-1}
. To keep the notation simple, let us denote the pullbacks of
E
,
d
,
and
h
by the same symbols; then
V\subseteq A^{0}(\mathbb{H},E)
is exactly the kernel of the
operator
d
.

52
.

If
v\in V
is a nontrivial multi-valued flat section, the pointwise Hodge norm

h(v,v)
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{H}
that is everywhere nonzero. The first
observation is that the Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
controls the first
derivatives of
h(v,v)
.

Lemma 5
.

For every nonzero
v\in V
, one has
\partial h(v,v)=-2h(\theta v,v)
.

Proof.

This is true for arbitrary harmonic bundles. By assumption, we have
d^{\prime}v=d^{\prime\prime}v=0
, and therefore
\partial v=-\theta v
. Since
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
is a metric
connection,

\partial\,h(v,v)=h(\delta^{\prime}v,v)+h(v,d^{\prime\prime}v)=h(\partial v-\theta v,v)=-2h(\theta v,v),

as claimed.
∎

53
.

Another important property of the function
h(v,v)
, discovered by
Griffiths and Schmid
[
Schmid
, Lem 7.19]
, is that the logarithm of
h(v,v)
is
subharmonic.

Lemma 6
.

If
v\in V
is nonzero, the function
\log h(v,v)
is subharmonic
on
\mathbb{H}
.

Proof.

This is again true for arbitrary harmonic bundles. From
d^{\prime}v=d^{\prime\prime}v=0
, we get

\partial v=-\theta v
and
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}v=-\theta^{\ast}v
. For the same reason as in

5
, we have

\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\,h(v,v)=-2h(v,\theta v).

Since
\partial+\bar{\partial}
is a metric connection, we then get

\displaystyle\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\,h(v,v)=-2h(\partial v,\theta v)-2h(v,\bar{\partial}\theta v)

\displaystyle=2h(\theta v,\theta v)+2h(v,\theta\bar{\partial}v)

\displaystyle=2h(\theta v,\theta v)-2h(\theta^{\ast}v,\theta^{\ast}v),

using the identity
\bar{\partial}\theta+\theta\bar{\partial}=0
and

3
. To shorten the next couple of formulas, we introduce the
two operators

A=\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\quad\text{and}\quad A^{\ast}=\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}},

which are both smooth sections of the bundle
\End(E)
. The notation is justified
because
A
and
A^{\ast}
are adjoints under the Hodge metric

h
, according to
3
. We then get
