\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,h(v,v)=2\Bigl(h(Av,Av)+h(A^{\ast}v,A^{\ast}v)\Bigr).

We also know from
5
that

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,h(v,v)=-2h(v,Av)=-2h(A^{\ast}v,v).

An application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality now gives

\left\lvert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,h(v,v)\right\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(4h(v,v)h(Av,Av)+4h(v,v)h(A^{\ast}v,A^{\ast}v)\Bigr)=h(v,v)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,h(v,v).

Since the Laplacian of
\varphi=\log h(v,v)
satisfies

\frac{1}{4}\Delta\varphi=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h(v,v)=\frac{1}{h(v,v)}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,h(v,v)-\frac{1}{h(v,v)^{2}}\left\lvert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,h(v,v)\right\rvert^{2}

this inequality is what we need to conclude that
\Delta\varphi\geq 0
.
∎

54
.

The argument above also shows that the first derivatives of
\log h_{E}(v,v)

are controlled by the operator
A=\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
, in the
following sense. From
5
and the triangle inequality, we get

\displaystyle\left\lvert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\,h_{E}(v,v)\right\rvert=2\bigl\lvert h_{E}(Av,v)\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2h_{E}(v,v)^{\frac{1}{2}}h_{E}(Av,Av)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2h_{E}(v,v)\,h_{\End(E)}(A,A)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
h_{\End(E)}(A,A)
is the Hodge norm of
A
under the induced
hermitian metric on the bundle
\End(E)
(which is an upper bound for the operator
norm of
A
with respect to the Hodge metric
h_{E}
). After dividing by

h_{E}(v,v)
, this says that

\left\lvert\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\log h_{E}(v,v)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h_{E}(v,v)\right\rvert\leq 2h_{\End(E)}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial z},\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(54.1)

The Hodge norm of the Higgs field therefore gives us an upper bound for the derivatives of
the function
\log h_{E}(v,v)
.

3.3
A universal upper bound for the Higgs field

55
.

Clearly, our next task is to bound the quantity

h_{\End(E)}(\theta_{\partial/\partial z},\theta_{\partial/\partial z})
. This
is analogous to the distance-decreasing property of period mappings, and the proof
naturally uses
Ahlfors’ lemma
, a generalization of
the Schwarz-Pick lemma from complex analysis
[
Ahlfors
]
. The result is usually
stated for the open unit disk; for the sake of completeness, we include a proof that
covers both cases.

Lemma 7

(Ahlfors)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to(0,+\infty)
be a positive smooth function such that

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log f\geq Cf

for a positive constant
C>0
. Then

f(z)\leq\frac{(2C)^{-1}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2}}\quad\text{for all $z\in\mathbb{H}$.}

Proof.

We start by proving the result on the open unit disk
\Delta
, where the technique
is easier to understand. Here the statement of Ahlfors’ lemma is the following:
for any positive smooth function
g\colon\Delta\to(0,+\infty)
, we have the
implication

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\log g\geq g\quad\Longrightarrow\quad g(t)\leq\frac{2}{(1-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}\quad\text{for $t\in\Delta$.}

Let
\Delta_{R}
denote the open disk of radius
R>0
. We are going to argue that

g(t)\leq\frac{2R^{2}}{(R^{2}-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}\quad\text{for $t\in\Delta_{R}$}

(55.1)

for every
R<1
; this is enough, because we can then let
R\to 1
to get the
result. Define an auxiliary function
u\colon\Delta_{r}\to(0,+\infty)
by the formula

g=u\cdot\frac{2R^{2}}{(R^{2}-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}.

We observe that
u
goes to zero near the boundary of
\Delta_{R}
, because

g
is bounded on
\Delta_{R}
, whereas
2R^{2}/(R^{2}-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}
goes to
infinity near the boundary. Therefore
u
must achieve its maximum at some
interior point
t_{0}\in\Delta_{R}
. In particular, the function
\log u

has a local maximum at
t_{0}
, and by looking at the second derivatives, we get

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\log u=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x\partial x}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y\partial y}\right)\log u\leq 0

for
t=t_{0}
, where
t=x+iy
. It follows that

0\geq\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\log u=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\log g-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\left(\log\frac{2r^{2}}{(r^{2}-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}\right)\geq g-\frac{2r^{2}}{(r^{2}-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}

for
t=t_{0}
, which says exactly that
u(t_{0})\leq 1
. But
u
had a maximum at

t_{0}
, and therefore
u\leq 1
on the entire disk
\Delta_{R}
, which is the
content of (
55.1
).

It remains to deduce the analogous result for a smooth function
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to(0,+\infty)
. Let us write the hypothesis in the symbolic form
\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\log f\geq f\mathit{dz}\wedge d\bar{z}
. After pulling back both
2
-forms along the isomorphism

\Delta\to\mathbb{H},\quad t\mapsto\frac{t+1}{t-1},

this becomes the condition that

\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\log f\left(\frac{t+1}{t-1}\right)\geq\frac{4}{\lvert t-1\rvert^{4}}\cdot f\left(\frac{t+1}{t-1}\right)\mathit{dt}\wedge d\bar{t}.

If we now set
g(t)=4\lvert t-1\rvert^{-4}\cdot f\bigl((t+1)/(t-1)\bigr)
, then we
get

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\log g\geq g,

and therefore, according to the discussion above, the inequality

g(t)\leq\frac{2}{(1-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2}}\quad\text{for $t\in\Delta$.}

If we convert this back into a statement about
f
, the result follows.
∎

56
.

The following lemma establishes the fundamental inequality that we need in order to
apply Ahlfors’ lemma to our setting
[
Simpson
, Thm. 1]
.

Lemma 8
.

With the notation
A=\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
and
A^{\ast}=\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}
, we have

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h_{\End(E)}(A,A)\geq\frac{h_{\End(E)}\bigl([A^{\ast},A],[A^{\ast},A]\bigr)}{h_{\End(E)}(A,A)}.

Proof.

It is best to view this as a special case of a more general result. Let
(E,d,h)
be a harmonic bundle on
\mathbb{H}
, and let
s
be a smooth section of
E
that
satisfies
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}s=0
and
\theta s=0
; in other words,
s
is a holomorphic
section of the Higgs bundle
E
that lies in the kernel of the Higgs field. Then
we claim that

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h(s,s)\geq\frac{h(A^{\ast}s,A^{\ast}s)}{h(s,s)},

(56.1)

where again
A^{\ast}=\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}
. The calculations are very
similar to those in the proof of
6
. The identity

\partial\bar{\partial}+\bar{\partial}\partial+\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta=0
in

2
gives us
\bar{\partial}\partial s=-\theta\theta^{\ast}s
.
Since
\partial+\bar{\partial}
is a metric connection, we have

\bar{\partial}\,h(s,s)=h(\bar{\partial}s,s)+h(s,\partial s)=h(s,\partial s).

Applying the same reasoning again, we get

\partial\bar{\partial}\,h(s,s)=h(\partial s,\partial s)+h(s,\bar{\partial}\partial s)=h(\partial s,\partial s)-h(s,\theta\theta^{\ast}s)=h(\partial s,\partial s)-h(\theta^{\ast}s,\theta^{\ast}s).

As before, we can now use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to conclude that

\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h(s,s)\geq\frac{h(A^{\ast}s,A^{\ast}s)}{h(s,s)},

which is of course just (
56.1
).

Now let us return to our specific situation. The vector bundle
\End(E)
, with the
flat connection induced by
d
and the metric induced by
h
, is again a harmonic
bundle; moreover, it is easy to see that
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
acts on

\End(E)
as the commutator with
A
, and
\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}
acts
as the commutator with
A^{\ast}
. If we consider
A
as a section of

\End(E)
, it is holomorphic and trivially commutes with
A
, hence belongs to the
kernel of both
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
and
\theta
. As such, the calculation from above
applies to it, and so

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h(A,A)\geq\frac{h\bigl([A^{\ast},A],[A^{\ast},A]\bigr)}{h(A,A)}.

This is the desired inequality.
∎

57
.

At each point of
\mathbb{H}
, the fiber of
E
is a vector space of dimension
r=\rk E
with a hermitian inner product, and
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
is a nilpotent
endomorphism. The following lemma bounds the pointwise norm
of the commutator.

Lemma 9
.

Let
V
be a complex vector space of finite dimension
r\geq 1
, with
a hermitian inner product. If
A\in\End(V)
is a nilpotent endomorphism, and if

A^{\ast}\in\End(V)
denotes its adjoint with respect to the inner product, then

\bigl\lVert[A^{\ast},A]\bigr\rVert^{2}\leq 2\lVert A\rVert^{4}\leq\binom{r+1}{3}\bigl\lVert[A^{\ast},A]\bigr\rVert^{2}.

Proof.

Choose an orthonormal basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{r}\in V
such that the matrix
representing
A
is upper triangular; the matrix representing
A^{\ast}
is
the conjugate transpose, hence lower triangular. Setting

a_{i,j}=\langle Ae_{i},e_{j}\rangle
, we get

\lVert A\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i<j}\lvert a_{i,j}\rvert^{2}.

The
k
-th diagonal entry of the matrix representing
[A^{\ast},A]
is easily
seen to be

d_{k,k}=\bigl(\lvert a_{1,k}\rvert^{2}+\dotsb+\lvert a_{k-1,k}\rvert^{2}\bigr)-\bigl(\lvert a_{k,k+1}\rvert^{2}+\dotsb+\lvert a_{k,r}\rvert^{2}\bigr),

and this gives us the rather simple-minded inequality

\bigl\lVert[A^{\ast},A]\bigr\rVert\geq\sqrt{\lvert d_{1,1}\rvert^{2}+\dotsb+\lvert d_{r,r}\rvert^{2}}.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
be arbitrary. It is easy to see that

\sum_{k=1}^{r}(x+k)d_{k,k}=\sum_{i<j}\bigl((x+j)-(x+i)\bigr)\lvert a_{i,j}\rvert^{2}=\sum_{i<j}(j-i)\lvert a_{i,j}\rvert^{2},

and from the trivial lower bound
j-i\geq 1
, we therefore get

\sum_{i<j}\lvert a_{i,j}\rvert^{2}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{r}(x+k)\lVert d_{k,k}\rVert\leq\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r}(x+k)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{r}\lvert d_{k,k}\rvert^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The right-hand side becomes minimal for
x=-\frac{1}{2}(r+1)
, with the result that

\lVert A\rVert^{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{r(r+1)(r-1)}{12}}\bigl\lVert[A^{\ast},A]\bigr\rVert.

This inequality is actually sharp: equality is achieved for the
r\times r
-matrix with

\lvert a_{k,k+1}\rvert^{2}=k(r-k)\quad\text{for $k=1,\dotsc,r-1$,}

and all other entries zero. Interestingly, this is exactly the case where
V
is
an irreducible representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, with
\rho(\mathsf{Y})=A
,
\rho(\mathsf{X})=A^{\ast}
, and
\rho(\mathsf{H})=[A^{\ast},A]
. We leave the verification
of this claim to the reader.

The proof of the other inequality is easier. Without loss of generality, we can
assume that
\lVert A\rVert=\lVert A^{\ast}\rVert=1
. The operator
[A^{\ast},A]
is
self-adjoint; by the spectral theorem, there is an orthonormal basis
e_{1}^{\prime},\dotsc,e_{r}^{\prime}
such that
[A^{\ast},A]e_{i}^{\prime}=\lambda_{i}e_{i}^{\prime}
with
\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore

\bigl\lVert[A^{\ast},A]\bigr\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\bigl(\lVert Ae_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}-\lVert A^{\ast}e_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}\bigr)^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert Ae_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{4}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert A^{\ast}e_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{4},

because
\lambda_{i}=\bigl\langle(A^{\ast}A-AA^{\ast})e_{i}^{\prime},e_{i}^{\prime}\bigr\rangle=\lVert Ae_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}-\lVert A^{\ast}e_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{2}
. At the same time,

1=\lVert A\rVert^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lVert Ae_{i}^{\prime}\rVert^{2},

and so both sums on the right-hand side are
\leq 1
.
∎

58
.

We can now apply the version of Ahlfors’ lemma in
7
, and
deduce the following very striking upper bound for the Hodge norm of the Higgs field.

Corollary 1
.

Let
E
be a polarized variation of Hodge structure of rank
r\geq 1
on the left
half-plane
\mathbb{H}
. Then the Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
satisfies
the inequality

h_{\End(E)}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial z},\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{0}^{2}}{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{2}},

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
.

Proof.

Since the Higgs field is trivial when
r=1
, we can assume that
r\geq 2
.
Let us again use the abbreviations
A=\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
and
A^{\ast}=\theta_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}
. If we put the inequalities in

8
and
9
together, we get

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\log h_{\End(E)}(A,A)\geq\frac{12}{r(r+1)(r-1)}h_{\End(E)}(A,A).

Now apply
7
to reach the desired conclusion.
∎

From an analytic point of view, this is really the central fact about
polarized variations of Hodge structure. The amazing thing is that the constant
depends on nothing but the rank of the variation of Hodge structure. This fact turns
out to be especially useful for the theory in several variables.

59
.

The induced variation of Hodge structure on the bundle
\End(E)
has rank
r^{2}
, but
in that case, there is a bound on the Higgs field that is much better than simply
replacing
r
by
r^{2}
is the result above.

Corollary 2
.

Let
E
be a polarized variation of Hodge structure of rank
r\geq 1
on the left
half-plane
\mathbb{H}
. Then the Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
of the
induced variation of Hodge structure on
\End(E)
satisfies the inequality

h_{\End(\End(E))}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial z},\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\bigr)\leq\frac{2rC_{0}^{2}}{(\operatorname{Re}z)^{2}},

where again
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
.

Proof.

Let
A=\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
be the Higgs field acting on
V
.
The induced Higgs field on
\End(V)
is then equal to the commutator
(\ad A)(X)=[A,X]
. It is therefore enough to prove that for any nilpotent matrix
A\in\End(V)
, one has

\lVert\ad A\rVert^{2}\leq 2r\lVert A\rVert^{2}.

Let
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{r}\in V
be an orthonormal basis such that the matrix for
A

is strictly upper triangular. Then the matrices
E_{i,j}
, whose only nonzero entry
is a
1
in the position
(i,j)
, form an orthonormal basis for
\End(V)
, and
a short calculation gives

(\ad A)(E_{i,j})=\sum_{k<i}a_{k,i}E_{k,j}-\sum_{j<k}a_{j,k}E_{i,k}.

We therefore get

\displaystyle\lVert\ad A\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\lvert(\ad A)(E_{i,j})\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j}\Bigl(\lvert a_{1,i}\rvert^{2}+\dotsb+\lvert a_{i-1,i}\rvert^{2}+\lvert a_{j,j+1}\rvert^{2}+\dotsb+\lvert a_{j,r}\rvert^{2}\Bigr)\leq 2r\lVert A\rVert^{2},

as claimed.
∎

3.4
Proof of the monodromy theorem

60
.

Let us summarize the results so far. We have shown that if
v\in V
is a nontrivial
multi-valued flat section of
E
, then the function
\varphi=\log h(v,v)
is smooth
and subharmonic on
\mathbb{H}
, and its first derivatives are bounded by

\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

(60.1)

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
. Everything up to this point,
maybe except for the optimal value of the constant, was known to Simpson

[
Simpson
, §2]
back in the 1990s. Now we add a small – but crucial! – new
insight, namely that the uniform bound on the first derivatives can be used directly
to control the behavior of the function
\varphi
for
\operatorname{Re}z\ll 0
.

61
.

Before we can explain how this works, we first need to introduce the
monodromy
operator

T\in\Aut(V)
. Following Schmid
[
Schmid
, (4.4)]
, we define this by
the formula

(Tv)(z)=v(z-2\pi i),

where
i=\sqrt{-1}
. That is to say, for any
v\in V
, the function
z\mapsto v(z-2\pi i)
is again a flat section of the bundle
E
on
\mathbb{H}
, and we define
Tv\in V
to be this section. The flat pairing on
E
induces a hermitian pairing

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C},

and one checks easily that
T
preserves
Q
, in the sense that
Q(Tv,Tw)=Q(v,w)
for every
v,w\in V
. We write the Jordan decomposition of
T
in the
form

T=T_{s}\cdot e^{2\pi iN},

with
T_{s}\in\Aut(V)
semisimple and
N\in\End(V)
nilpotent. Then

Q(T_{s}v,T_{s}w)=Q(v,w)\quad\text{and}\quad Q(Nv,w)=Q(v,Nw),

for every
v,w\in V
, because
Q
is conjugate-linear in the second argument. Note
that
T
depends on the choice of
i=\sqrt{-1}
, but
N
is independent of it.

62
.

The space
V
of multi-valued flat sections gives us a trivialization of the vector
bundle
E
on the left half-plane
\mathbb{H}
. We can therefore consider the polarized
variation of Hodge structure as a family of Hodge structures on
V
; as usual, we
denote by the letter
\Phi(z)
the Hodge filtration of the Hodge structure at the
point
z\in\mathbb{H}
. The definition of the monodromy operator has the following
consequence.

Lemma 10
.

For every
z\in\mathbb{H}
, we have the equality

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=T\,\Phi(z),

where both sides are filtrations on
V
.

Proof.

This may be a bit confusing, so let us write out the argument. The
trivialization of the pullback of the bundle
E
along
\exp\colon\mathbb{H}\to\Delta^{\ast}

gives us, for every point
z\in\mathbb{H}
, an isomorphism of complex vector spaces

\phi_{z}\colon V\to(\exp^{\ast}E)\big|_{z}\cong E_{t},\quad\phi_{z}(v)=v(z).

Here
t=e^{z}
. Since
v(z+2\pi i)=(T^{-1}v)(z)
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{z+2\pi i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{z}}

commutes. The way the period mapping is constructed, we have

F_{\Phi(z+2\pi i)}^{p}=\phi_{z+2\pi i}^{-1}\bigl(F^{p}E_{t}\bigr)=(T^{-1})^{-1}\phi_{z}^{-1}\bigl(F^{p}E_{t}\bigr)=TF_{\Phi(z)}^{p},

which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

63
.

Given a multi-valued flat section
v\in V
, we can write
the value of the function
h(v,v)
at the point
z\in\mathbb{H}
in the equivalent form

h(v,v)(z)=\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2},

using the notation for the Hodge norm from
9
. Since
T\in\Aut(V,Q)
, we have

h(v,v)(z+2\pi i)=\lVert v\rVert_{T\Phi(z)}^{2}=\lVert T^{-1}v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=h(T^{-1}v,T^{-1}v)(z).

(63.1)

It is this relation that connects the asymptotic behavior of the Hodge norm to the
monodromy transformation
T
.

64
.

To illustrate the power of the derivative bound in (
60.1
), we
are now going to prove the monodromy theorem
[
Schmid
, Lem. 4.5]
.

Proposition 3
.

If
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of
T
, then
\lvert\lambda\rvert=1
.

Proof.

Let
v\in V
be a nonzero eigenvector with
Tv=\lambda v
, and consider the
smooth function
\varphi=\log h(v,v)
. The relation in (
63.1
)
implies that

\varphi(z+2\pi i)=\log\lvert\lambda\rvert^{-2}+\varphi(z)

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
. On the other hand, we clearly have

\varphi(z+2\pi i)-\varphi(z)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(i\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}(z+iy)-i\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}(z+iy)\right)\mathit{dy},

and on account of (
60.1
), this leads to the inequality

\bigl\lvert\varphi(z+2\pi i)-\varphi(z)\bigr\rvert\leq\frac{8\pi C_{0}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
. Letting
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
, we conclude that
\log\lvert\lambda\rvert=0
, whence
\lvert\lambda\rvert=1
.
∎

3.5
Effective bounds for the monodromy transformation

65
.

We can use the derivative bound in (
60.1
) to control the
Hodge norms of the nilpotent operator
N
and of the projections
P_{\lambda}\colon V\to E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
to the eigenspaces of
T_{s}
. The fact that
N
and

\theta_{\partial/\partial z}
are both of order
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-1}
is no accident:
during the proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem, we will see that the difference
between these two operators, modulo
F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}
, is of order
e^{-\varepsilon\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
. (The precise statement is in
16
.)

Proposition 4
.

There is a constant
C>0
, whose value only depends on
\dim V
and on the
minimal polynomial of the monodromy transformation
T\in\GL(V)
, such that:

(a)

For every
v\in V
and every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1
, one has

\lVert Nv\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq\frac{C}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}.

(b)

For every
v\in V
and every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1
, one has

\lVert P_{\lambda}v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq C\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}.

66
.

Given the bound on the derivative of the function
\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}
,
the proof is just linear algebra. By the same argument as in the proof of

3
, we have

\Bigl\lvert\log\lVert Tv\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}-\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\Bigr\rvert=\Bigl\lvert\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z-2\pi i)}^{2}-\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{8\pi C_{0}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert};

after exponentiating, this becomes

e^{-8\pi C_{0}/\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\leq\frac{\lVert Tv\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}}{\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}}\leq e^{8\pi C_{0}/\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

If we restrict to
\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1
for simplicity, it follows that

\Bigl\lvert\lVert Tv\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}-\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\Bigr\rvert\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2},

(66.1)

where
C^{\prime}=(e^{8\pi C_{0}}-1)/(8\pi C_{0})
.

67
.

The following elementary lemma, applied to the inner products

\langle-,-\rangle_{\Phi(z)}
, turns the inequality in (
66.1
)
into a bound for the operator norm of the nilpotent operator
N
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional complex vector space with an inner product

\langle-,-\rangle
. Let
T\in\GL(V)
be an endomorphism whose eigenvalues
have absolute value
1
. Suppose that
T
is close to unitary, in the sense that
there is a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that

\Bigl\lvert\lVert Tv\rVert^{2}-\lVert v\rVert^{2}\Bigr\rvert\leq\varepsilon\lVert v\rVert^{2}\quad\text{for all $v\in V$.}

Then there are two constants
C>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, whose values only depends on
the integer
\dim V
and on the minimal polynomial of the operator
T
, such that

\lVert Nv\rVert\leq C\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon^{m})\cdot\lVert v\rVert\quad\text{for all $v\in V$,}

where
N=(2\pi i)^{-1}\log T_{u}
is the logarithm of the unipotent part of
T
.

Proof.

Set
r=\dim V
, and choose an orthonormal basis
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{r}\in V
such
that the matrix representing
T
is upper triangular. Write
T=U(\id+B)
,
where
U
is a diagonal matrix and
B
is strictly upper triangular. The entries
of
U
are the eigenvalues of
T
, hence of absolute value
1
, and so
U
is
unitary. Pick any two integers
1\leq i<j\leq r
, and let
b_{i,j}=\langle Be_{j},e_{i}\rangle
denote the corresponding entry of the matrix representing
B
. By
applying the hypothesis to vectors of the form
v=e_{i}+ze_{j}
with
\lvert z\rvert=1
, we get

2\operatorname{Re}(zb_{i,j})=2\operatorname{Re}\bigl\langle B(e_{i}+ze_{j}),e_{i}+ze_{j}\bigr\rangle\leq\varepsilon\lVert e_{i}+ze_{j}\rVert^{2}=2\varepsilon,

and by choosing
z
appropriately, this gives
\lvert b_{i,j}\rvert\leq\varepsilon
.
Consequently, the
L^{2}
-norm of the operator
B\in\End(V)
is bounded by

\lVert B\rVert^{2}\leq\binom{r}{2}\varepsilon^{2}
, and therefore

\lVert Bv\rVert\leq r\varepsilon\lVert v\rVert\quad\text{for all $v\in V$.}

Now it is a basic fact from linear algebra that the nilpotent operator
N=(2\pi i)^{-1}\log T_{u}
can be written as a polynomial in
T
, of the form

N=P(T)\cdot\prod_{\lambda}(T-\lambda\id),

where the product runs over the distinct eigenvalues of
T
; the polynomial
P(t)\in\mathbb{C}[t]
is uniquely determined by the minimal polynomial of
T
. Consequently,

N=P(T)\cdot\prod_{\lambda}\bigl((U-\lambda\id)+UB\bigr),

and because the operator norm of
P(T)
is easily bounded using the inequality

\lVert Tv\rVert^{2}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\lVert v\rVert^{2}
, it suffices to estimate the
operator norm of the product. Given our bound
r\varepsilon
for the operator norm of

B
, this comes down to

\prod_{\lambda}(U-\lambda\id)=0,

which holds because
U
is a diagonal matrix.
∎

68
.

The same argument also proves that the projection operators

P_{\lambda}\colon V\to E_{\lambda}(T_{s})

are uniformly bounded, meaning that there is a constant
C>0
, whose value again
only depends on the integer
\dim V
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
,
such that

\lVert P_{\lambda}v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq C\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\quad\text{for $v\in V$ and $\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1$.}

(68.1)

The reason is that
P_{\lambda}
can again be expressed as a certain
polynomial in
T
, whose operator norm can then be bounded using
(
66.1
). This finishes the proof of
4
.

4
Period domains and period mappings

69
.

In this chapter, we briefly review the basic properties of period domains and period
mappings, in the setting of
complex
Hodge structures. The theory is actually
simpler than in the case of rational Hodge structures
[
Schmid
, §3]
, because the
Lie groups that are involved are just real forms of the general linear group

\GL(V)
.

4.1
The period domain and its compact dual

70
.

Fix a finite-dimensional complex vector space
V
and a hermitian
pairing
Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
. Let
D
be the
period domain

parametrizing Hodge structures of the type we are interested in: each point
o\in D

corresponds to a Hodge structure

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V_{o}^{p,q}

on the vector space
V
, with fixed Hodge numbers
\dim V_{o}^{p,q}
, and polarized by
the hermitian pairing
Q
. We assume that there is at least one such Hodge
structure; in particular,
Q
is nondegenerate and of the correct signature. In this
setting, the real Lie group

G=\bigl\{\thinspace g\in\GL(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(gv,gw)=Q(v,w)$ for all $v,w\in V$}\thinspace\bigr\}

acts transitively on
D
, according to the rule
V_{g\cdot o}^{p,q}=g(V_{o}^{p,q})
.
(This is easily proved by chosing an orthonormal basis adapted to the Hodge
decomposition, and then mapping one such orthonormal basis to another.)
The inner products at the two points
o
and
g\cdot o
are related by the
formula

\langle gv,gw\rangle_{g\cdot o}=\langle v,w\rangle_{o},

which follows from the fact that
g\in G
.

71
.

The Lie algebra of
G
is easily seen to be

\mathfrak{g}=\bigl\{\thinspace A\in\End(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(Av,w)+Q(v,Aw)=0$
for all $v,w\in V$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

Note that
\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}=\End(V)
, which means that
G
is a
non-compact real form of the complex Lie group
\GL(V)
. This also gives us a real
structure on
\End(V)
; the “complex conjugate” of an endomorphism
A\in\End(V)
is
-A^{\dagger}
, where the adjoint
A^{\dagger}
with respect to the
hermitian pairing
Q
is defined by the formula

Q(Av,w)=Q(v,A^{\dagger}w)\quad\text{for all $v,w\in V$.}

This makes sense because
Q
, being a polarization, is nondegenerate.

72
.

The stabilizer of a point
o\in D
is the compact subgroup

H_{o}=\bigl\{\thinspace g\in G\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$g(V_{o}^{p,q})=V_{o}^{p,q}$ for all $p,q$}\thinspace\bigr\}\subseteq G,

and this gives us an isomorphism
D\cong G/H_{o}
. We can use it to
describe the real tangent space
T_{o}D
in terms of Hodge structures. The Hodge
structure on
V
induces a Hodge structure of weight
0
on
\End(V)
, by setting

\End(V)_{o}^{j,-j}=\bigl\{\thinspace A\in\End(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$A(V_{o}^{p,q})\subseteq V_{o}^{p+j,q-j}$ for all $p,q$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

It is an easy exercise to check that the decomposition

\End(V)=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\End(V)_{o}^{j,-j}

(72.1)

is an
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structure (with respect to the real structure
\mathfrak{g}
), and that
the induced polarization on
\End(V)
is equal to the trace pairing

(A,B)\mapsto\tr(AB^{\dagger}).

Since the conjugate of
B
is
-B^{\dagger}
, we can view the polarization on

\End(V)
as coming from the
\mathbb{R}
-valued bilinear pairing
(A,B)\mapsto-\tr(AB)

on
\mathfrak{g}
.

73
.

Here is the proof that the trace pairing is indeed a polarization for the induced Hodge
structure on
\End(V)
. The same argument is needed in
Section

5
, and so we
give some details.

Lemma 12
.

The pairing

\End(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C},\quad(A,B)\mapsto\tr(AB^{\dagger}),

polarizes the Hodge structure on
\End(V)
.

Proof.

It is easy to see that
\tr A^{\dagger}=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
for every
A\in\End(V)
.
Therefore

\tr(BA^{\dagger})=\tr\bigl((AB^{\dagger})^{\dagger}\bigr)=\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}},

and so the pairing is hermitian symmetric. The Hodge decomposition is
orthogonal with respect to the trace pairing. Indeed, if
A\in\End(V)_{o}^{i,-i}

and
B\in\End(V)_{o}^{j,-j}
, with
i\neq j
, then
AB^{\dagger}\in\End(V)_{o}^{i-j,j-i}
is a nilpotent endomorphism, and so
\tr(AB^{\dagger})=0
.

Finally, we need to explain why
(-1)^{j}\tr(AA^{\dagger})>0
if
A\in\End(V)_{o}^{j,-j}
is nonzero. The point is that
(-1)^{j}A^{\dagger}

is exactly the adjoint of
A
with respect to the inner product on
V
.
Indeed, for
v\in V^{p,q}
and
w\in V^{p+j,q-j}
, we have

\langle Av,w\rangle_{o}=(-1)^{q-j}Q(Av,w)=(-1)^{q-j}Q(v,A^{\dagger}w)=(-1)^{j}\langle v,A^{\dagger}w\rangle_{o}.

Consequently, the endomorphism
(-1)^{j}A^{\dagger}A
is self-adjoint with respect to
the inner product, and also positive definite, because

\bigl\langle(-1)^{j}A^{\dagger}Av,v\bigr\rangle_{o}=\langle Av,Av\rangle_{o}=\lVert Av\rVert_{o}^{2}.

This clearly implies that
(-1)^{j}\tr(AA^{\dagger})>0
. In fact, by choosing an
orthonormal basis in
V
, one can easily see that this expression is nothing but the
operator norm of
A
with respect to the inner product.
∎

74
.

Using the notation from (
72.1
), the Lie algebra of the stabilizer subgroup

H_{o}
is exactly
the intersection
\mathfrak{g}_{o}^{0,0}=\mathfrak{g}\cap\End(V)_{o}^{0,0}
, and so we conclude that
the real tangent space to the period domain at a point
o\in D
is

T_{o}D\cong\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{o}^{0,0}.

(74.1)

From the polarized Hodge structure on
\End(V)
, we get a positive-definite inner
product on
\mathfrak{g}
. It induces an inner product on the quotient

\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{o}^{0,0}
, hence on each tangent space
T_{o}D
. More precisely, define

\mathfrak{m}_{o}=\mathfrak{g}\cap\bigoplus_{k\neq 0}\End(V)_{o}^{k,-k},

so that
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{m}_{o}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{o}^{0,0}
. Note that
\mathfrak{m}_{o}
is just a linear
subspace of
\mathfrak{g}
, not a Lie subalgebra. Then
T_{o}D\cong\mathfrak{m}_{o}
,
and the inner product on
T_{o}D
is simply the inner product on
\mathfrak{m}_{o}
coming
from the Hodge structure on
\End(V)
. In this way, we obtain a Riemannian metric on
the period domain
D
; one shows without trouble that this metric is

G
-invariant. We denote by

d_{D}\colon D\times D\to[0,+\infty]

(74.2)

the resulting
G
-invariant distance function on the period domain. (The value

+\infty
is included because
D
does not have to be connected.)

Example 3
.

Consider Hodge structures of the form

V=V^{1,0}\oplus V^{0,1}

on the vector space
V=\mathbb{C}^{2}
that are polarized by the indefinite hermitian pairing

Q=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

Writing
V^{1,0}=\mathbb{C}(1,t)
, we must have
1-\lvert t\rvert^{2}>0
, hence
t\in\Delta
. The period domain is therefore the unit disk
\Delta
; the Hodge
structure corresponding to the point
t\in\Delta
is

\mathbb{C}^{2}=\mathbb{C}(1,t)\oplus\mathbb{C}(\bar{t},1).

The
G
-invariant Riemannian metric on the period domain is the usual Poincaré
metric on
\Delta
. Indeed,
G
is just the automorphism group of
\Delta
, and so
it is enough to compute the metric on the tangent space
T_{0}\Delta
. In the Hodge
structure at the point
0\in\Delta
, the two standard basis vectors form an
orthonormal basis. A short computation shows that

\mathfrak{m}_{0}=\left\{\thinspace\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{w}\\
w&0\end{pmatrix}\thinspace\thinspace\middle|\thinspace\thinspace w\in\mathbb{C}\thinspace\right\},

and that the
\mathbb{R}
-linear mapping
\mathfrak{m}_{0}\to T_{0}\Delta
takes the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&\bar{w}\\
w&0\end{pmatrix}
to the complex number
w
.
The inner product on the
\mathbb{R}
-vector space
\mathbb{C}=T_{0}\Delta

is therefore just the usual Euclidean inner product.

75
.

If two points
o,p\in D
are close to each other, then of course the corresponding
inner products
\langle v,w\rangle_{o}
and
\langle v,w\rangle_{p}
must also be close. The
following lemma makes this precise.

Lemma 13
.

There are two constants
\delta>0
and
C>0
, such that if
o,p\in D
are two
points with
d_{D}(p,o)<\delta
, then one has

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{p}-\langle v,w\rangle_{o}\bigr\rvert\leq C\lVert v\rVert_{o}\lVert w\rVert_{o}\cdot d_{D}(p,o)

for every
v,w\in V
.

Proof.

Because of the
G
-invariance of the metric on
D
, it suffices to prove this
when
o\in D
is a fixed choice of base point. The exponential mapping

\mathfrak{m}_{o}\to D,\quad A\mapsto e^{A}\cdot o,

is a real-analytic local diffeomorphism. It follows that there is a constant

\delta>0
such that every point
p\in D
with
d_{D}(p,o)<\delta
can be
uniquely written in the form
p=e^{A}\cdot o
, where
A\in\mathfrak{m}_{o}
satisfies

\lVert A\rVert_{o}\leq 2d_{D}(p,o)
. Clearly,

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{p}-\langle v,w\rangle_{o}\bigr\rvert=\bigl\lvert\langle e^{-A}v,e^{-A}w\rangle_{o}-\langle v,w\rangle_{o}\bigr\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\lVert v\rVert_{o}\lVert w\rVert_{o}\cdot\lVert A\rVert_{o}

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
that is independent of
v,w\in V
. This gives the
desired result (with
C=2C^{\prime}
).
∎

76
.

The period domain
D
is an open subset of the
compact dual

\check{D}
, which is
a closed algebraic subvariety of a product of Grassmannians, and therefore a
projective complex manifold. The points of
\check{D}
parametrize all decreasing
filtrations
F=F^{\bullet}V
on the vector space
V
, subject only to the
constraint that

\dim F^{p}=\dim V^{p,q}+\dim V^{p+1,q-1}+\dim V^{p+2,q-2}+\dotsb\quad\text{for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

Since we are working with complex Hodge structures, there are no further
restrictions on
F
, unlike in Schmid’s paper
[
Schmid
, §3]
. The complex
Lie group
\GL(V)
acts transitively on
\check{D}
, by the rule
(gF)^{p}=g(F^{p})
. The
holomorphic tangent space at a point
F\in\check{D}
is therefore

T_{F}^{1,0}\check{D}\cong\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V),

(76.1)

where we are using the notation

F^{0}\End(V)=\bigl\{\thinspace A\in\End(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$A(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p}$ for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

If we go to a point
o\in D
, and denote by
F_{o}
the Hodge filtration in the Hodge
structure on
V
, the natural isomorphism of
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces

T_{o}D\cong T_{F_{o}}^{1,0}\check{D}

becomes, in terms of Hodge structures, the isomorphism

\mathfrak{m}_{o}\cong\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{o}^{0,0}\cong\End(V)/F_{o}^{0}\End(V),

(76.2)

which is of course valid in any
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge structure of weight
0
. Via the
embedding
D\subseteq\check{D}
, the period domain
D
inherits the structure of a
complex manifold.

77
.

We can also define a hermitian metric on the compact dual
\check{D}
, which is
however not
\GL(V)
-invariant. For that, choose a base point
o\in D
, and
denote by
\langle v,w\rangle=\langle v,w\rangle_{o}
the resulting inner product on the vector space

V
. It induces an inner product on
\End(V)
, and therefore on each holomorphic
tangent space

T_{F}^{1,0}\check{D}\cong\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V).

This gives us a hermitian metric on the compact dual
\check{D}
. Note that, unlike the

G
-invariant metric on the period domain
D
, this metric is
not
invariant
under the full symmetry group
\GL(V)
, only under the (much smaller) unitary group

U_{o}\subseteq\GL(V)
of the reference inner product
\langle v,w\rangle
. The resulting
distance function is

d_{\check{D}}\colon\check{D}\times\check{D}\to[0,+\infty);

note that
\check{D}
has finite diameter, because it is compact and connected. It is
easy to see that the unitary group
U_{o}
acts transitively on
\check{D}
: choose a
basis adapted to a given filtration, and convert it into an orthonormal basis adapted
to the filtration with the help of the Gram-Schmid process.

78
.

We can use the exponential mapping to get local coordinates on
\check{D}
.

Lemma 14
.

There is a constant
\delta>0
such that for every point
p\in\check{D}
, the set

B_{\delta}(p)=\bigl\{\thinspace x\in\check{D}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace d_{\check{D}}(p,x)<\delta\thinspace\bigr\}

is biholomorphic to an open set in Euclidean space, and the function

d_{\check{D}}(p,-)
is comparable, on
B_{\delta}(p)
, to the Euclidean distance, up
to a factor of
2
.

Proof.

Fix a base point
o\in D
. Since
\check{D}
is a homogeneous space for the unitary
group
U_{o}\subseteq\GL(V)
, and since the distance function
d_{\check{D}}
is

U_{o}
-invariant, it suffices to prove the statement when
p=o
. From the induced
Hodge structure on
\End(V)
, we have a direct sum decomposition
\End(V)=W\oplus F_{o}^{0}\End(V)
, where

W=\bigoplus_{j<0}\End(V)_{o}^{j,-j},

By general theory, the exponential mapping

W\to\check{D},\quad A\mapsto e^{A}\cdot o,

is a local diffeomorphism; it is also holomorphic, and therefore a local isomorphism
of complex manifolds. It follows that there is a constant
\delta>0
such that
the exponential mapping induces an isomorphism between
B_{\delta}(o)\subseteq\check{D}
and an open neighborhood of the origin in the vector space
W
.
Since the distance function
d_{\check{D}}
is continuous, we can arrange moreover that

\frac{1}{2}\lVert A\rVert_{o}\leq d_{\check{D}}\bigl(e^{A}\cdot o,o\bigr)\leq 2\lVert A\rVert_{o}

on the open ball in question.
∎

79
.

We record one additional fact about the distance function
d_{\check{D}}
that we are going to
need towards the end of the proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem (in

34
). Fix an operator
g\in\GL(V)
, and consider the mapping

g\colon\check{D}\to\check{D},\quad F\mapsto gF.

What is the effect of this on tangent vectors? Let
\Ad g\colon\End(V)\to\End(V)
be the linear mapping
A\mapsto gAg^{-1}
. Then one checks that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\End(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{F}^{1,0}\check{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{gF}^{1,0}\check{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ad g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces dg}

is commutative. In particular, left translation by
g
changes the length of
holomorphic tangent vectors, computed using the hermitian metric on
\check{D}
, at most
by the operator norm of
\Ad g
, relative to our fixed inner product on
\End(V)
.
After integrating this, we arrive at the useful formula

d_{\check{D}}(g\cdot p,g\cdot q)\leq\max_{\lVert A\rVert=1}\lVert(\Ad g)A\rVert\cdot d_{\check{D}}(p,q),

(79.1)

valid for any two points
p,q\in\check{D}
.

4.2
The period mapping

80
.

We return to our variation of Hodge structure
E
on the punctured disk. At each
point
z\in\mathbb{H}
, we have a polarized Hodge structure on
V
, whose Hodge filtration
we denoted by the symbol
\Phi(z)
. This gives us the
period mapping

\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to D;

we shall argue in a moment that it is holomorphic (with respect to the complex
structure on the period domain induced by
D\subseteq\check{D}
.) The monodromy
transformation satisfies
T\in G
, hence also
T_{s}\in G
and
2\pi i\,N\in\mathfrak{g}
, and we have

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=T\cdot\Phi(z).

From now on, we use the subscript
\Phi(z)
to refer to the Hodge
structure at the point
z\in\mathbb{H}
, to avoid confusion. So the Hodge decomposition is

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V_{\Phi(z)}^{p,q},

the Hodge norm is
\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}
, and so on.

Lemma 15
.

The derivative of the period mapping is given by

d\Phi\big|_{z}=(\theta+\theta^{\ast})\big|_{z}+\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi(z)}^{0,0},

where we consider both sides as
\mathbb{R}
-linear mappings from
T_{z}\mathbb{H}
to
the quotient
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi(z)}^{0,0}
.

Proof.

Fix a point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
. Choose an orthonormal frame for the bundle
E
,
consisting of smooth sections
e_{1},\dotsc,e_{r}\in A^{0}(\mathbb{H},E)
with the property that

\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\Phi(z)}=\delta_{i,j}\quad\text{for all $z\in\mathbb{H}$,}

and such that
e_{i}\in A^{0}(\mathbb{H},E^{p_{i},q_{i}})
for certain
p_{i},q_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Denote by

v_{1},\dotsc,v_{r}\in V
the unique multi-valued flat sections such that
v_{i}(z_{0})=e_{i}(z_{0})
. We can define a smooth function
g\colon\mathbb{H}\to\GL(V)
by the
condition that

e_{j}(z)=g(z)\cdot v_{j}\quad\text{for $j=1,\dotsc,r$ and $z\in\mathbb{H}$}.

Clearly,
g(z_{0})=\id
, and since

(-1)^{q_{i}}Q\Bigl(g(z)v_{i},g(z)v_{j}\Bigr)=\langle e_{i},e_{j}\rangle_{\Phi(z)}=\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle_{\Phi(z_{0})}=(-1)^{q_{i}}Q(v_{i},v_{j}),

we have
g(z)\in G
for all
z\in\mathbb{H}
. The function
g\colon\mathbb{H}\to G
is a smooth lifting of the period mapping, which can now be described very
concretely as

\Phi(z)=g(z)\cdot o\quad\text{for $z\in\mathbb{H}$.}

The derivative
d\Phi
is easily computed from this formula. Writing

e_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}g_{i,j}v_{i}

with smooth functions
g_{i,j}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}
, our matrix-valued function
g\colon\mathbb{H}\to G
is represented by the
r\times r
-matrix with entries

g_{i,j}
. At the point
z_{0}
, the derivative

dg\big|_{z_{0}}\colon T_{z_{0}}\mathbb{H}\to\mathfrak{g}

is a lifting for the derivative of the period mapping

d\Phi\big|_{z_{0}}\colon T_{z_{0}}\mathbb{H}\to T_{\Phi(z_{0})}D.

It is represented by the
r\times r
-matrix with entries
dg_{i,j}
. Recall that
the flat connection on
E
is
d=\partial+\bar{\partial}+\theta+\theta^{\ast}
.
Since
v_{1},\dotsc,v_{r}
are flat sections, we get

(\partial+\bar{\partial}+\theta+\theta^{\ast})e_{j}=de_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}dg_{i,j}\otimes v_{i}.

Now the operator
\partial+\bar{\partial}
preserves the subbundle
E^{p,q}
, and so this
term disappears when we project into
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi(z_{0})}^{0,0}
. The result
is that

dg\big|_{z_{0}}\equiv(\theta+\theta^{\ast})\big|_{z_{0}}\mod\mathfrak{g}_{\Phi(z_{0})}^{0,0},

and so the right-hand side indeed represents the derivative of the period mapping
at the point
z_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
. Note that

\theta\big|_{z_{0}}\in\End(V)_{\Phi(z_{0})}^{-1,1},\quad\text{and}\quad\theta^{\ast}\big|_{z_{0}}\in\End(V)_{\Phi(z_{0})}^{1,-1},

which is the horizontality condition for the period mapping.
∎

81
.

We can now deduce quite easily that the period mapping is holomorphic.

Corollary 3
.

The period mapping
\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to D
is holomorphic.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that
\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\check{D}
is holomorphic. Recall from
(
76.1
) that the holomorphic tangent space at
\Phi(z)\in\check{D}
is

T_{\Phi(z)}^{1,0}\check{D}\cong\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}.

Using
15
, the differential of
\Phi
is therefore equal to

\theta\big|_{z}\colon T_{z}^{1,0}\mathbb{H}\to\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)},

and since this is
\mathbb{C}
-linear,
\Phi
is indeed holomorphic.
∎

5
Results about
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures

82
.

This section contains some background on
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures and their
polarizations. Roughly speaking, an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure is a representation of
the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, but in the category of Hodge structures. The
cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold is a typical example, but
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structures also appear naturally in the study of degenerating variations of Hodge
structure.

5.1
The Weil element and polarizations

83
.

Following one of several competing conventions, we will denote the three generators
of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by the letters
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}
. Concretely,

\mathsf{H}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{Y}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},

and the relations among the three generators are

[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X}]=2\mathsf{X},\quad[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y},\quad[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}]=\mathsf{H}.

In any finite-dimensional representation
V
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, the element
\mathsf{H}
acts
semisimply with integer eigenvalues, and so the underlying vector space is the direct
sum of the eigenspaces
V_{k}=E_{k}(\mathsf{H})
, for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

84
.

The following definition is of central importance in Hodge theory.

Definition 3
.

An
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
on a finite-dimensional complex vector space

V
is a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
V
with the following
properties:

(a)

Each weight space
V_{k}=E_{k}(\mathsf{H})
has a Hodge structure of weight
n+k
;
the integer
n
is called the
weight
of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure.

(b)

The two operators

\mathsf{X}\colon V_{k}\to V_{k+2}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Y}\colon V_{k}\to V_{k-2}(-1)

are morphisms of Hodge structures.

Example 4
.

Here are two useful examples.

1.

The prototypical example of a
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure is the total
cohomology

\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}H^{n+k}(X,\mathbb{C})

of an
n
-dimensional compact Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
; in this case,

\mathsf{X}=2\pi i\,L_{\omega}
and
\mathsf{Y}=(2\pi i)^{-1}\Lambda_{\omega}
,
and the weight is
n=\dim X
.

2.

Any Hodge structure
V
of weight
n
may be viewed as an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure of weight
n
by letting the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
act in a
trivial way.

85
.

Equivalently, an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
is a bigraded vector space

V=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{i,j}

that is simultaneously a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, in a way that is
compatible with the bigrading. This means that

\mathsf{X}\colon V^{i,j}\to V^{i+1,j+1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Y}\colon V^{i,j}\to V^{i-1,j-1},

and that
\mathsf{H}
acts on the subspace
V^{i,j}
as multiplication by the integer
(i+j)-n
.
This makes each of the weight spaces

V_{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j=n+k}V^{i,j}

into a Hodge structure of weight
n+k
. In what follows, we are always going to use
the notation
V_{k}^{i,j}
for the individual subspaces, to avoid confusion with the
Hodge decomposition in a polarized Hodge structure on
V
.

86
.

Let
V
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure. From general principles, it follows that

\mathsf{X}^{k}\colon V_{-k}\to V_{k}(k)\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Y}^{k}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}(-k)

are isomorphisms of Hodge structures. Another way to understand this symmetry is
through the action of the Lie group
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Every finite-dimensional
representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
lifts to a representation of the Lie
group
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, in a way that is compatible with the exponential map:
for a matrix
M\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, the element

\exp(M)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{M^{n}}{n!}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

acts on the representation as the exponential series
\id+M+\frac{1}{2}M^{2}+\dotsb
.
Now consider the
Weil element

\mathsf{w}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C}),

which plays a similar role as the Hodge
\ast
-operator in classical Hodge theory. A brief
computation shows that
\mathsf{w}=\exp(\mathsf{X})\exp(-\mathsf{Y})\exp(\mathsf{X})
, and so one can use the
exponential series to see how
\mathsf{w}
acts on representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
.

87
.

The point of introducing
\mathsf{w}
is that it explains the symmetry between the weight
spaces
V_{k}
and
V_{-k}
. Namely, the Lie group
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on its Lie algebra

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
by conjugation, and under this action, one has

\mathsf{w}\mathsf{X}\mathsf{w}^{-1}=-\mathsf{Y},\quad\mathsf{w}\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{w}^{-1}=-\mathsf{X},\quad\mathsf{w}\mathsf{H}\mathsf{w}^{-1}=-\mathsf{H}.

This can be checked by direct computation: for example,

\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}.

The identity
\mathsf{w}\mathsf{H}\mathsf{w}^{-1}=-\mathsf{H}
means that the action of
\mathsf{w}

interchanges the two weight spaces
V_{k}
and
V_{-k}
, which must therefore be of the
same dimension.

Note
.

The square of the Weil element

\mathsf{w}^{2}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})

acts on the weight space
V_{k}
as
(-1)^{k}
, and
not
as multiplication by

-1
. This is easiest to remember with the help of the symbolic identity
\mathsf{w}^{2}=(-1)^{\mathsf{H}}
, whose right-hand side is a convenient abbreviation for the power series

e^{i\pi\mathsf{H}}
.

88
.

We know that
\mathsf{w}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}
is an isomorphism for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
But since
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
are only morphisms up to a Tate twist, each term in the series
expansion of
\mathsf{w}=e^{\mathsf{X}}e^{-\mathsf{Y}}e^{\mathsf{X}}
needs a different Tate twist, and
so it is not immediately clear that
\mathsf{w}
is a morphism of Hodge structures.

Lemma 16
.

If
V
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure, then
\mathsf{w}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}(-k)
is an
isomorphism of Hodge structures (of weight
n+k
).

Proof.

We first prove an auxiliary formula. Suppose that
b\in V_{-\ell}
is primitive, in
the sense that
\mathsf{Y}b=0
(and therefore
\ell\geq 0
). From
\mathsf{w}e^{-\mathsf{X}}=e^{\mathsf{X}}e^{-\mathsf{Y}}
, we get
\mathsf{w}e^{-\mathsf{X}}b=e^{\mathsf{X}}b
, and after expanding and
comparing terms in degree
\ell-2j
, also

\mathsf{w}\frac{\mathsf{X}^{j}}{j!}b=(-1)^{j}\frac{\mathsf{X}^{\ell-j}}{(\ell-j)!}b.

(88.1)

Now any
a\in V_{k}
has a unique Lefschetz decomposition

a=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}\frac{\mathsf{X}^{j}}{j!}a_{j},

where
a_{j}\in V_{k-2j}
satisfies
Ya_{j}=0
. Here we only need to consider
j\geq k
in the sum because
\mathsf{X}^{2j-k+1}a_{j}=0
, which implies that
\mathsf{X}^{j}a_{j}=0
for
j<k
. Suppose further that
a\in V^{p,q}
, where
p+q=n+k
. Then
\mathsf{X}^{i}a_{j}\in V^{p+i,q+i}
, and by descending induction on
j\geq\max(k,0)
, we deduce that

a_{j}\in V^{p-j,q-j}.

In other words, the Lefschetz decomposition holds in the category of Hodge
structures. We can now check what happens when we apply
w
. Using
(
88.1
),

\mathsf{w}a=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}w\frac{\mathsf{X}^{j}}{j!}a_{j}=\sum_{j\geq\max(k,0)}(-1)^{j}\frac{\mathsf{X}^{j-k}}{(j-k)!}a_{j}\in V^{p-k,q-k},

and so
\mathsf{w}
is a morphism of Hodge structures. Since
\mathsf{w}
is bijective, it must be an
isomorphism of Hodge structures, as claimed.
∎

89
.

Using the Weil element, one can give a very concise definition of polarizations for

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures. This idea is due to Deligne
[
Deligne-signs
]
.

Definition 4
.

A
polarization
of an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
V
is a hermitian form

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

that satisfies the four identities
H^{\dagger}=-H
,
X^{\dagger}=X
,

Y^{\dagger}=Y
, and
w^{\dagger}=w
, such that
Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{w})

polarizes the Hodge structure of weight
n+k
on each
V_{k}
.

Here the dagger again means the adjoint of an operator with respect to the
nondegenerate hermitian pairing
Q
.

90
.

The relation
Q\circ(\mathsf{H}\otimes\id)=-Q\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{H})
implies that

Q\big|_{V_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}=\begin{cases}Q_{k}&\text{if $\ell=-k$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

and so
Q
is really a family of sesquilinear pairings
Q_{k}\colon V_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
. The second condition in

4
is then saying that the hermitian pairing

Q_{k}\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{w})\colon V_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

polarizes the Hodge structure of weight
n+k
on
V_{k}
. In particular, this means
that the Hodge structure on the
primitive
subspace
V_{-k}\cap\ker\mathsf{Y}

is polarized by the hermitian pairing
Q_{k}\circ(\id\otimes\mathsf{X}^{k})
; this is the
usual way to describe the polarization on the cohomology of a compact Kähler
manifold. Deligne’s definition has the advantage of giving a concise formula for the
polarization on all of
V
, not just on the primitive subspaces.

91
.

With the exception of positivity, all the conditions in the definition have
a nice functorial interpretation. The conjugate complex vector space
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}

is again an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
: the action of
\mathsf{H}

is unchanged, but
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Y}
act with an extra minus sign. This sign change is
dictated by the geometric case, where
\mathsf{X}=2\pi i\,L_{\omega}
and
\mathsf{Y}=(2\pi i)^{-1}\Lambda_{\omega}
. Likewise, if
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
are
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structures of weights
n^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime\prime}
, then the tensor product
V^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V^{\prime\prime}
is naturally an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}
:
to be precise,

\bigl(V^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V^{\prime\prime}\bigr)_{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j=k}V^{\prime}_{i}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}V^{\prime\prime}_{j},

and the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-action is given by the usual formulas

\displaystyle\mathsf{X}(v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{X}v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime}+v^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{X}v^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}(v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Y}v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime}+v^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{Y}v^{\prime\prime},

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}(v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{H}v^{\prime}\otimes v^{\prime\prime}+v^{\prime}\otimes\mathsf{H}v^{\prime\prime}.

Lastly, we can turn
\mathbb{C}(-n)
into an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
2n
by
letting
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
act trivially. Then all the identities in

4
can be summarized in one line by saying
that the hermitian form

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}(-n)

is a morphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures of weight
2n
.

92
.

From a polarization
Q
, we obtain a hermitian inner product on the vector
space
V
as follows. The individual Hodge structures on the weight spaces
V_{k}
give
us a direct sum decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{p+q-n}^{p,q},

and we denote by
v=\sum_{p,q}v^{p,q}
the components of a given vector. The
formula

\langle v,w\rangle=\sum_{p,q\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{q}Q\bigl(v^{p,q},\mathsf{w}(w^{p,q})\bigr)

(92.1)

then defines a positive definite hermitian inner product on
V
. By construction, the
above decomposition is orthogonal with respect to this inner product. We will
see in
11
how this inner product relates to polarized Hodge structures.

5.2
The irreducible representations

93
.

Recall that
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
has, up to isomorphism, a unique irreducible representation
of dimension
m+1
. We denote this representation by
S_{m}
. Clearly,
S_{0}

is the trivial representation, and
S_{1}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the standard representation; for

m\geq 2
, one has
S_{m}=\Sym^{m}(S_{1})
. Since finite-dimensional
representations of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
are completely reducible, one has by Schur’s lemma a
canonical decomposition

V\cong\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}.

(93.1)

We can turn this into a decomposition in the category of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures,
by the following construction.

94
.

First, we argue that each irreducible representation
S_{m}
comes with a canonical

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
m
. For the trivial representation
S_{0}=\mathbb{C}
, this is the trivial Hodge structure of type
(0,0)
. The
standard
representation

S_{1}
also has a canonical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure. Denote by

a=(1,0)
and
b=(0,1)
the two standard basis vectors, so that
\mathsf{Y}a=b
and
\mathsf{X}b=a
. We get an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
1
by considering

E_{1}(\mathsf{H})=\mathbb{C}a\quad\text{and}\quad E_{-1}(\mathsf{H})=\mathbb{C}b

as Hodge structures of type
(1,1)
and
(0,0)
, respectively. It is polarized by the
unique hermitian pairing with
Q(a,b)=1
and
Q(a,a)=Q(b,b)=0
; indeed,

\displaystyle(-1)^{1}Q(a,\mathsf{w}a)

\displaystyle=-Q(a,-b)=Q(a,b)=1,

\displaystyle(-1)^{0}Q(b,\mathsf{w}b)

\displaystyle=Q(b,a)=1,

since
\mathsf{w}a=-b
and
\mathsf{w}b=a
. In particular, we have

\langle a,a\rangle=\langle b,b\rangle=1\quad\text{and}\quad\langle a,b\rangle=0,

which means that
a,b\in S_{1}
form an orthonormal basis with respect to
(
92.1
).

95
.

Since everything is compatible with taking symmetric powers, the irreducible
representation
S_{m}=\Sym^{m}(S_{1})
inherits an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of
weight
m
. We can describe this concretely as follows. The
m+1
elements

v_{0}=a^{m},v_{1}=a^{m-1}b,\dotsc,v_{k}=a^{m-k}b^{k},\dotsc,v_{m}=b^{m}

form a basis for the vector space
S_{m}
. It is easy to see that

\mathsf{H}v_{k}=(m-2k)v_{k},\quad\mathsf{X}v_{k}=kv_{k-1},\quad\mathsf{Y}v_{k}=(m-k)v_{k+1},

if we declare that
v_{-1}=v_{m+1}=0
. They are morphisms up to the required Tate
twists if we consider each weight space

E_{m-2k}(\mathsf{H})=\mathbb{C}v_{k}

as a Hodge structure of type
(m-k,m-k)
. The induced hermitian pairing
Q
on
S_{m}

has the property that
Q(v_{j},v_{k})=0
if
k+j\neq m
, while

Q(v_{k},v_{m-k})=\frac{k!(m-k)!}{m!}.

Since
\mathsf{w}v_{k}=(\mathsf{w}a)^{m-k}(\mathsf{w}b)^{k}=(-1)^{m-k}v_{m-k}
, we get

(-1)^{m-k}Q(v_{k},\mathsf{w}v_{k})=Q(v_{k},v_{m-k})=\frac{k!(m-k)!}{m!}>0,

as required by
4
. With respect to the hermitian
inner product in (
92.1
), the vectors
v_{0},\dotsc,v_{m}\in S_{m}
are again orthogonal, and

\langle v_{k},v_{k}\rangle=\frac{k!(m-k)!}{m!}.

Note
.

Recall that a hermitian pairing
Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
induces a
hermitian pairing on the symmetric product
\Sym^{m}V
by the formula

Q\bigl(v_{1}\dotsm v_{m},w_{1}\dotsm w_{m}\bigr)=\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}\prod_{j=1}^{m}Q\bigl(v_{j},w_{\sigma(j)}\bigr),

where
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
is the group of permutations of the set
\{1,\dotsc,m\}
. This comes about by viewing the symmetric product as a subspace of

V^{\otimes m}
, via the embedding

\Sym^{m}V\hookrightarrow V^{\otimes m},\quad v_{1}\dotsm v_{m}\mapsto\frac{1}{m!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}}v_{\sigma(1)}\otimes\dotsb\otimes v_{\sigma(m)}.

This explains the term
m!
in the formulas above.

96
.

We continue working on the decomposition in (
93.1
). Next, we note
that in any
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
V
of weight
n
, the subspace of

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-invariants

V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V_{0}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\mathsf{X}v=\mathsf{Y}v=0\thinspace\bigr\}=\ker\bigl(\mathsf{Y}\colon V_{0}\to V_{-2}(-1)\bigr)

is a sub-Hodge structure of
V_{0}
, and hence a Hodge structure of weight
n
. If
V

is polarized by
Q
, then
V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
is polarized by the restriction of
Q
.

97
.

Now let
V
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
. For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the vector space
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
inherits an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of
weight
n-m
. According to the preceding paragraph, the subspace of

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-invariants

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}

is therefore a Hodge structure of weight
n-m
. If we view it as an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure by letting
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
act trivially, the evaluation mapping

S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\to V

becomes a morphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures of weight
n
, and the
isomorphism in (
93.1
) becomes an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structures.

98
.

The following lemma shows that the decomposition in (
93.1
) is also
compatible with polarizations.

Lemma 17
.

Let
V
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
. Then
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)

inherits an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n-m
,
such that the evaluation morphism

S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\to V,\quad s\otimes f\mapsto f(s),

is a morphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures. If
V
is polarized, then

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
is also polarized, and the evaluation morphism respects the
polarizations.

Proof.

The first assertion is obvious, and so we focus on the second one. For clarity,
let us write
Q_{V}
for the polarization on
V
, and
Q_{S_{m}}
for the polarization
on
S_{m}
. Given
f\colon S_{m}\to V
, let
f^{\dagger}\colon V\to S_{m}
be
the adjoint, defined by the rule

Q_{V}\bigl(v,f(s)\bigr)=Q_{S_{m}}\bigl(f^{\dagger}(v),s\bigr).

A short calculation, similar to
12
, shows that the trace
pairing

(f,g)\mapsto\frac{\tr(g^{\dagger}\circ f)}{\dim S_{m}}

polarizes the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure on
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
. The induced
polarization on the tensor product is therefore

(f\otimes s,g\otimes t)\mapsto Q_{S_{m}}(s,t)\cdot\frac{\tr(g^{\dagger}\circ f)}{\dim S_{m}}.

Now suppose that
f,g\in\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
. By Schur’s lemma, we have

g^{\dagger}\circ f=c(f,g)\id_{S_{m}}

for a constant
c(f,g)\in\mathbb{C}
. Consequently,

Q_{S_{m}}(s,t)\cdot\frac{\tr(g^{\dagger}\circ f)}{\dim S_{m}}=c(f,g)Q_{S_{m}}(s,t)=Q_{S_{m}}\bigl(g^{\dagger}(f(s)),t\bigr)=Q_{V}\bigl(f(s),g(t)\bigr),

and so the induced polarization on the tensor product is indeed compatible with
the given polarization on
V
.
∎

5.3
The associated Hodge structure

99
.

One justification for the name “
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure” is that
every (polarized)
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure has canonically associated to it a
(polarized) Hodge structure of the same weight. We now describe this construction.
Suppose
V
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
. We denote by

V=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}

the “total” Hodge decomposition, and by

F^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigoplus_{j\geq q,i}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}

the total Hodge filtration and its conjugate. Clearly,
\mathsf{X}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p+1}

and
\mathsf{Y}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p-1}
, and likewise for the other filtration

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
. If
Q
is a polarization of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure, one can recover
the conjugate Hodge filtration
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
from the Hodge filtration
F
because

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in F^{n-q+1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

(99.1)

Note
.

In fact, a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure can be described completely by
specifying the representation
V
, the weight
n
, the Hodge filtration
F
,
and the hermitian pairing
Q
. This data determines the conjugate Hodge filtration

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
according to (
99.1
), and therefore the subspaces

V_{k}^{i,j}=E_{k}(\mathsf{H})\cap F^{i}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j}

in the Hodge decomposition of each weight space. Note that the weight is
not

determined by
F
and
Q
alone.

100
.

The following theorem explains how to pass from a (polarized)
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure to a (polarized) Hodge structure of the same weight. Later in this chapter,
we are going to prove a sort of converse to this result, which is the key to the
existence of limiting mixed Hodge structures.

Theorem 11
.

Let
V
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
, and let
Q

be a polarization. Then the following is true:

(a)

e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
and
e^{-\mathsf{Y}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
are the two Hodge filtrations of a polarized Hodge
structure of weight
n
, with polarization
Q
.

(b)

The inner product determined by this polarized Hodge structure is equal to
the one determined by the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure in (
92.1
).

(c)

We have
\mathsf{H}^{\ast}=\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Y}^{\ast}=\mathsf{X}
, where
\ast
denotes
the adjoint with respect to the inner product determined by the polarized Hodge
structure.

Proof.

The decomposition in (
93.1
) implies that it is enough to prove
this for the irreducible representations
S_{m}
. Furthermore, everything is
compatible with taking symmetric powers, and so we only need to consider the
standard representation
S_{1}
. Here
F^{1}=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{1}=\mathbb{C}a
, and consequently

e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{1}=\mathbb{C}(a+b)\quad\text{and}\quad e^{-\mathsf{Y}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{1}=\mathbb{C}(a-b).

Since
Q(a+b,a-b)=0
, we obtain a Hodge structure
V=V^{1,0}\oplus V^{0,1}

with

V^{1,0}=\mathbb{C}(a+b)\quad\text{and}\quad V^{0,1}=\mathbb{C}(a-b).

Also,
Q
is a polarization because
Q(a+b,a+b)=2=-Q(a-b,a-b)
. The Hodge
decompositions of the two standard basis vectors are

a=\frac{1}{2}(a+b)+\frac{1}{2}(a-b)\quad\text{and}\quad b=\frac{1}{2}(a+b)-\frac{1}{2}(a-b),

from which it follows that
a
and
b
form an orthonormal basis with respect to the
inner product coming from the Hodge structure. Since
\mathsf{H}a=a
and
\mathsf{H}b=-b
, we
see that
\mathsf{H}
is self-adjoint with respect to the inner product; similarly,

\mathsf{Y}a=b
and
\mathsf{X}b=a
imply that
\mathsf{Y}^{\ast}=\mathsf{X}
. Recalling the
definition of the inner product in (
92.1
), we obviously have

\langle a,a\rangle=\langle b,b\rangle=1\quad\text{and}\quad\langle a,b\rangle=0,

and so the two inner products agree.
∎

101
.

The analogy with the cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold is striking:

V
has a hermitian inner product;
\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{X}^{\ast}
is the adjoint of the operator

\mathsf{X}
with respect to this inner product;
\mathsf{H}=[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{X}^{\ast}]
is the
commutator of
\mathsf{X}
and its adjoint.

102
.

The next result relates the Weil operator in the Hodge structure
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F

to representation theory. Using the total Hodge decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{i,j}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j},

we define an involution
C\in\GL(V)
by the rule

Cv=(-1)^{j}v\quad\text{for $v\in V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}$.}

This is the analogue of the Weil operator for an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure.

Proposition 5
.

Let
C_{\sharp}\in\End(V)
be the Weil operator of the Hodge structure whose Hodge
filtration is
F_{\sharp}=e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
. Then
C_{\sharp}=\mathsf{w}\circ C
.

Proof.

As before, it suffices to prove this in the case of the standard
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure on
S_{1}
. If we again denote the two basis vectors by
a
and
b
,
then

\displaystyle Ca=-a,\quad Cb=b,\quad\mathsf{w}a=-b,\quad\mathsf{w}b=a,\quad C_{\sharp}a=b,\quad C_{\sharp}b=a,

from which it follows that
C_{\sharp}=\mathsf{w}\circ C
.
∎

103
.

For later use, let us also record the Hodge decompositions of the three operators

\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\End(V)
that constitute the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation. Let

\End(V)=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\End(V)^{j,-j}

denote the Hodge structure of weight
0
on
\End(V)
, induced by the Hodge structure

e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
on
V
. For any
A\in\End(V)
, we write the Hodge decomposition in the
form

A=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{j},

with
A_{j}\in\End(V)^{j,-j}
.

Lemma 18
.

The Hodge decompositions of
\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{H}\in\End(V)
look like

\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1},\quad\mathsf{X}=-\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}-\mathsf{Y}_{1},\quad\mathsf{H}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+2\mathsf{Y}_{1},

and conversely, we have

4\mathsf{Y}_{-1}=\mathsf{Y}-\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{X},\quad 2\mathsf{Y}_{0}=\mathsf{Y}+\mathsf{X},\quad 4\mathsf{Y}_{1}=\mathsf{Y}+\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{X}

Proof.

It is again enough to prove this for the standard
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure on

S_{1}
. Denote the two basis vectors by
a
and
b
; then the Hodge decomposition
is

S_{1}=\mathbb{C}(a+b)\oplus\mathbb{C}(a-b).

With respect to the basis
a,b
, we have

\mathsf{Y}-\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&-1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{Y}+\mathsf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathsf{Y}+\mathsf{H}-\mathsf{X}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix},

and it is easy to see that these three matrices have Hodge type
(-1,1)
,
(0,0)
,
and
(1,-1)
, respectively. This suffices to conclude the proof.
∎

104
.

One nice consequence of these identities is that the isotypical components of the

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation are actually sub-Hodge structures. This is most easily
proved with the help of the
Casimir operator

\Omega=2(\mathsf{X}\mathsf{Y}+\mathsf{Y}\mathsf{X})+\mathsf{H}^{2}+\id=4\mathsf{X}\mathsf{Y}+(\mathsf{H}+\id)^{2}\in\End(V).

By general theory,
\Omega
commutes with
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}
, and acts on the
m
-th summand
in the decomposition (
93.1
) as multiplication by

(m+1)^{2}
. A brief computation using the identities in
18
gives

\displaystyle\Omega

\displaystyle=2(-\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}-\mathsf{Y}_{1})(\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1})

\displaystyle\hphantom{=2(-\mathsf{Y}_{-1}\,\,}+2(\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1})(-\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}-\mathsf{Y}_{1})+4(\mathsf{Y}_{1}-\mathsf{Y}_{-1})^{2}+\id

\displaystyle=(2\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\id)^{2}-16\mathsf{Y}_{1}\mathsf{Y}_{-1}.

This means that
\Omega
is an endomorphism of the Hodge structure on
V
, and
therefore that each isotypical component
S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
in the decomposition (
93.1
) is automatically a
sub-Hodge structure.

5.4
The associated variation of Hodge structure

105
.

In fact, a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
determines a polarized
variation of Hodge structure of weight
n
on the punctured disk
\Delta^{\ast}
. These
“model variations” are important, both as examples of the general theory, and
because they are used to prove the Hodge norm estimates.

106
.

Let
V
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
, and let
Q
be a polarization.
From the previous section, we keep the notation

F^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}

for the total Hodge filtration. By construction,
\mathsf{H}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p}
,

\mathsf{Y}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p-1}
, and
\mathsf{X}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p+1}
. We now define a
holomorphic mapping

\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to\check{D},\quad\Phi(z)=e^{-z\mathsf{Y}}F.

At the point
z=-1
, the filtration
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
is the Hodge filtration of a
polarized Hodge structure of weight
n
(by
11
), and
therefore
\Phi(-1)\in D
. Now write
z=x+iy
, with
x<0
. From the relation

[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y}
, we get

e^{-z\mathsf{Y}}=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{\lvert x\rvert\mathsf{Y}}=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}e^{\mathsf{Y}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}},

and therefore

\Phi(z)=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\cdot e^{\mathsf{Y}}F=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\cdot\Phi(-1).

(106.1)

Both exponential factors are elements of the real group
G=\Aut(V,Q)
, because

\mathsf{H}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Y}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}
, and so we conclude that

\Phi(z)\in D
for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
.

107
.

We are going to show that
\Phi
is the period mapping of a polarized variation of Hodge
structure. Let
E=\mathbb{H}\times V
be the trivial smooth vector bundle with fiber
V
,
and let
d\colon A^{0}(\mathbb{H},E)\to A^{1}(\mathbb{H},E)
be the flat connection induced by
differentiation. The Hodge structures
\Phi(z)
give us a decomposition

E=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}E^{p,q}

into smooth subbundles. This can be described concretely as follows. Let

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V^{p,q}

denote the Hodge decomposition in the Hodge structure
\Phi(-1)
. Due to
(
106.1
), the Hodge decomposition in the Hodge structure
\Phi(z)
is
then equal to
