V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V_{\Phi(z)}^{p,q}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}V^{p,q},

and so the subbundle
E^{p,q}\subseteq E
is the image of the smooth mapping

\mathbb{H}\times V^{p,q}\to\mathbb{H}\times V,\quad(z,v)\mapsto e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}v.

108
.

It is now a simple matter to verify the conditions in
1
. Every
smooth section in
A^{0}(\mathbb{H},E^{p,q})
can be written in the form

e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}f,

where
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to V^{p,q}
is a smooth function. We have

\displaystyle d\Bigl(e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}f\Bigr)

\displaystyle=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\biggl(-ie^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\mathsf{Y}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}f\otimes dy+\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{H}f\otimes dx+df\biggr)

\displaystyle=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\biggl(-\frac{i}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}f\otimes dy+\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{H}f\otimes dx+df\biggr)

Remembering the identities (from
18
)

\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{H}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+2\mathsf{Y}_{1},

we can rewrite the factor in parentheses as

-\frac{i}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}f\otimes dy+\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{H}f\otimes dx+df=df-\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{-1}f\otimes\mathit{dz}+\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{1}f\otimes d\bar{z}-\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{0}f\otimes(\mathit{dz}-d\bar{z})

It follows that
d=\partial+\theta+\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}+\theta^{\ast}
, just as in

1
, with

\displaystyle g^{-1}\partial g

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}-\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{0}\otimes\mathit{dz},

\displaystyle g^{-1}\theta g

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{-1}\otimes\mathit{dz},

\displaystyle g^{-1}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}g

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}+\frac{1}{2\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{0}\otimes d\bar{z},

\displaystyle g^{-1}\theta^{\ast}g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert}\mathsf{Y}_{1}\otimes\mathit{dz},

where
g=e^{-iy\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\in G
. Evidently, these
operators have the required bidegrees
(1,0)\otimes(0,0)
,
(0,1)\otimes(0,0)
,
(1,0)\otimes(-1,1)
, and
(0,1)\otimes(1,-1)
, respectively. Therefore
\Phi
is indeed
the period mapping of a polarized variation of Hodge structure on
\mathbb{H}
. From the
identity

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{Y}}\Phi(z),

we see that the variation of Hodge structure is pulled back from the punctured disk

\Delta^{\ast}
; the monodromy operator is the unipotent operator

T=e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{Y}}\in G,

and in our usual notation, we therefore have
N=-\mathsf{Y}
.

109
.

In fact, we can easily turn this into an example with non-unipotent monodromy as
well. Let
S\in\End(V)
be an operator with the property that
S^{\dagger}=S
,
and assume that
S
is an endomorphism of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure on
V
. Concretely, this means that
[\mathsf{H},S]=[\mathsf{Y},S]=0
and
that
S(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p}
for every
p\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows that
S
is
self-adjoint with respect to the inner product in (
92.1
), and
so
S
is automatically semisimple with real eigenvalues. We still have

\Phi(z+2\pi i)=e^{2\pi i(S-\mathsf{Y})}\Phi(z),

and so we can also consider
\Phi
as the period mapping of a polarized variation of
Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
with monodromy operator

T=T_{s}T_{u}=e^{2\pi iS}e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{Y}}\in G.

This is the Jordan decomposition of
T
.

110
.

As an aside, let us verify the result in
1
about the
pointwise Hodge norm of the Higgs field in this example. Since
\Phi(z)=g\cdot\Phi(-1)
, we have

\bigl\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=\bigl\lVert g^{-1}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}g\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}=\frac{1}{\lvert x\rvert^{2}}\bigl\lVert Y_{-1}\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2},

and so the pointwise Hodge norm of the Higgs field is exactly a constant multiple of

\lvert x\rvert^{-1}
in this case. (It is a pleasant exercise to check that when
V=S_{m}
is the irreducible representation of dimension
m+1
, and hence
\rk E=m+1
,
one has
\bigl\lVert Y_{-1}\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\binom{m+2}{3}
, and so equality
is achieved in
1
; this explains what we found during
the proof of
9
.)

111
.

We are now going to derive a simple formula for the Hodge metric in our “model”
variation of Hodge structure. To simplify the notation, let us write
\lVert v\rVert
and

\langle v,w\rangle
for the inner product in (
92.1
); according to

11
, we have

\lVert v\rVert=\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle v,w\rangle=\langle v,w\rangle_{\Phi(-1)},

and so this agrees with our usual notation. From (
106.1
), we get

\bigl\langle v,w\bigr\rangle_{\Phi(z)}=\Bigl\langle e^{\frac{1}{2}\log{\lvert x\rvert}\,\mathsf{H}}e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}v,\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}w\Bigr\rangle

and since
\mathsf{H}^{\ast}=\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Y}^{\ast}=\mathsf{X}
by

11
, we can rewrite this as

\bigl\langle v,w\bigr\rangle_{\Phi(z)}=\Bigl\langle e^{\log{\lvert x\rvert}\,\mathsf{H}}e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}v,\,e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}w\Bigr\rangle=\Bigl\langle e^{-iy\mathsf{X}}e^{\log{\lvert x\rvert}\,\mathsf{H}}e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}v,\,w\Bigr\rangle.

112
.

The conclusion that we can draw from this is that the weight filtration

W_{\ell}=E_{\ell}(\mathsf{H})\oplus E_{\ell-1}(\mathsf{H})\oplus E_{\ell-2}(\mathsf{H})\oplus\dotsb

controls the rate of growth of the Hodge norm in these examples. Indeed, suppose that
we have a vector
v\in V
with
\mathsf{H}v=\ell v
. Then

e^{-iy\mathsf{X}}e^{\log\lvert x\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}e^{iy\mathsf{Y}}v=e^{-iy\mathsf{X}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(iy)^{k}}{k!}\lvert x\rvert^{\ell-2k}\mathsf{Y}^{k}v=\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{(iy)^{j+k}}{j!k!}\lvert x\rvert^{\ell-2k}\mathsf{X}^{j}\mathsf{Y}^{k}v,

and since different weight spaces are orthogonal under the inner product, we get

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{(iy)^{2k}}{(k!)^{2}}\lvert x\rvert^{\ell-2k}\langle\mathsf{X}^{k}\mathsf{Y}^{k}v,v\rangle=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{y^{2k}}{(k!)^{2}}\lvert x\rvert^{\ell-2k}\lVert\mathsf{Y}^{k}v\rVert^{2}.

As long as
y=\operatorname{Im}z
remains bounded, this expression grows like
\lvert x\rvert^{\ell}=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{\ell}
; in fact, the leading term is exactly
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{\ell}\lVert v\rVert^{2}
.

5.5
Recognizing polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures

113
.

We end our discussion of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures by proving a converse to

11
. The result below gives us a way to recognize polarized

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures, and is going to be the key step in the
construction of the limiting mixed Hodge structure. Let
V
be a finite-dimensional
representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and let
Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
be a nondegenerate hermitian pairing such that

\mathsf{X}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{X}
,
\mathsf{Y}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}
, and
\mathsf{H}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}
, where
the dagger always means the adjoint of an operator with respect to
Q
.

114
.

Now suppose that we have a decreasing filtration
F
on the vector
space
V
, with the following two properties:

(a)

One has
\mathsf{Y}(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet-1}
and
\mathsf{H}(F^{\bullet})=F^{\bullet}
.

(b)

The filtration
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
is the Hodge filtration of a polarized
Hodge structure of weight
n
on
V
, polarized by the pairing
Q
.

It will be convenient to have a name for this kind of object, so let us agree
to call such a filtration
F
an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration
(of weight
n
).

Theorem 12
.

If
F
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration on the vector space
V
, of weight
n
,
then
V
has a unique
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
such that

F^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}\quad\text{for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$,}

and this
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure is polarized by the pairing
Q
.

115
.

The proof is basically just linear algebra, with a little bit of representation
theory mixed in. We note the following useful corollary.

Corollary 4
.

If
S\in\End(V)
satisfies
[\mathsf{H},S]=[\mathsf{Y},S]=0
and
S^{\dagger}=S
,
and if
S(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet}
, then
S
is an endomorphism
of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure that also respects the polarization.

116
.

The proof of
12
uses the decomposition

V\cong\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}

into isotypical components, coming from Schur’s lemma. Recall that each
S_{m}
has a
canonical polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
m
. According to

11
, the total Hodge filtration
F^{\bullet}S_{m}
is an

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration in the above sense. We are going to argue that the

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtrations on
S_{m}
and
V
give each vector space
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
a polarized Hodge structure of weight
n-m
, in a way that is
compatible with the above decomposition. The result we want will then follow from the
fact that each irreducible representation
S_{m}
is a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure of weight
m
.

117
.

The first thing to do is to analyze the subspace of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-invariants;
everything else is going to follow from this case by functoriality.

Proposition 6
.

Let
F
be an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration. Then

V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\mathsf{H}v=\mathsf{Y}v=0\thinspace\bigr\}

has a Hodge structure of weight
n
, polarized by the restriction of
Q
, whose
Hodge filtration is
F\cap V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
. This Hodge structure is
compatible with the polarized Hodge structure on
V
defined by the filtration

e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
.

118
.

The idea of the proof is to analyze the Hodge decompositions of the two operators

\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}\in\End(V)
. Note that
\mathsf{H}
is “real”, in the sense that it belongs
to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\operatorname{Lie}G
; the other two operators
\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in i\mathfrak{g}
are “purely imaginary”, due to the fact that the pairing
Q
is hermitian.
(The same thing happens for the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation on the
cohomology of a compact Kähler manifold
(X,\omega)
, because
\mathsf{X}=2\pi i\,L_{\omega}
and
\mathsf{Y}=(2\pi i)^{-1}\Lambda_{\omega}
in that case.)

119
.

To simplify the notation, let us from now on denote by

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V^{p,q}

the Hodge structure of weight
n
whose Hodge filtration is
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
. Since
Q

is a polarization, it is easy to see that the conjugate Hodge filtration is

e^{-\mathsf{Y}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
, where

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in F^{n-q+1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

The Hodge structure on
V
induces a Hodge structure of weight
0
on the vector space

\End(V)
. Recall that the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{g}=\bigl\{\thinspace A\in\End(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace A^{\dagger}=-A\thinspace\bigr\}

defines a real structure; the induced polarization is
(A,B)\mapsto\tr(A\circ B^{\dagger})
, where
B^{\dagger}
means the adjoint of
B
relative to the
nondegenerate hermitian pairing
Q
. For an operator
A\in\End(V)
, we denote by

A=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}A_{j}

its Hodge decomposition in the Hodge structure on
\End(V)
, with
A_{j}\in\End(V)^{j,-j}
. Concretely, this means that
A_{j}(V^{p,q})\subseteq V^{p+j,q-j}
for
all
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

120
.

If we write the Hodge decompositions of
\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{H}\in\End(V)
as

\mathsf{Y}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{Y}_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{H}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathsf{H}_{j},

then our assumptions on
\mathsf{Y}
and
\mathsf{H}
can be expressed as follows.

Lemma 19
.

We have

\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{H}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{H}_{0}+2\mathsf{Y}_{1},

and these operators satisfy
\mathsf{Y}_{-1}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}_{1}
,
\mathsf{Y}_{0}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}_{0}
, and
\mathsf{H}_{0}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}_{0}
.

Proof.

From the fact that
\mathsf{Y}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}
, we get
(\mathsf{Y}_{j})^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}_{-j}
for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. The condition
\mathsf{Y}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p-1}

implies that
\mathsf{Y}(e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p})\subseteq e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p-1}
, which
means that
\mathsf{Y}_{j}=0
for
j\leq-2
. But then also
\mathsf{Y}_{j}=0

for
j\geq 2
, and so actually

\mathsf{Y}=\mathsf{Y}_{-1}+\mathsf{Y}_{0}+\mathsf{Y}_{1}.

Similarly, we have
\mathsf{H}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}
, hence
(\mathsf{H}_{j})^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}_{-j}
for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. The condition
\mathsf{H}(F^{p})\subseteq F^{p}

implies that

(\mathsf{H}+2\mathsf{Y})e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p}=e^{\mathsf{Y}}\mathsf{H}e^{-\mathsf{Y}}\cdot e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p}=e^{\mathsf{Y}}\mathsf{H}(F^{p})\subseteq e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p},

and so
\mathsf{H}_{j}+2\mathsf{Y}_{j}=0
for
j\leq-1
. In particular,

\mathsf{H}_{-1}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}
and
\mathsf{H}_{j}=0
for all
j\leq-2
. But then

\mathsf{H}_{j}=0
for
j\geq 2
, and
\mathsf{H}_{1}=-(\mathsf{H}_{-1})^{\dagger}=2(\mathsf{Y}_{-1})^{\dagger}=2\mathsf{Y}_{1}
.
∎

121
.

The relation
[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y}
gives us the following additional identity.

Lemma 20
.

We have
2\mathsf{Y}_{0}=2[\mathsf{Y}_{-1},\mathsf{Y}_{1}]+[\mathsf{Y}_{0},\mathsf{H}_{0}]
.

Proof.

Consider the component of
2\mathsf{Y}=[\mathsf{Y},\mathsf{H}]
in the subspace
\End(V)^{0,0}
.
From the preceding lemma, we get

2\mathsf{Y}_{0}=[\mathsf{Y}_{1},\mathsf{H}_{-1}]+[\mathsf{Y}_{0},\mathsf{H}_{0}]+[\mathsf{Y}_{-1},\mathsf{H}_{1}]=-2[\mathsf{Y}_{1},\mathsf{Y}_{-1}]+[\mathsf{Y}_{0},\mathsf{H}_{0}]+2[\mathsf{Y}_{-1},\mathsf{Y}_{1}],

which simplifies to the desired identity.
∎

122
.

We can now prove
6
. On
V
, we have a polarized Hodge
structure of weight
n
with Hodge filtration
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
. It is enough to show that

V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
is a sub-Hodge structure; the remaining assertions then follow
because
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
and
F
induce the same filtration on
V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
, due to
the fact that
\mathsf{Y}
acts trivially.

123
.

To say that
V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
is a sub-Hodge structure means that whenever we take
a vector
v\in V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
, and write its Hodge decomposition as

v=\sum_{p}v_{p},

with
v_{p}\in V^{p,q}
, then each
v_{p}\in V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
. This is trivially
satisfied if
v=0
. If
v\neq 0
, let
p\in\mathbb{Z}
be the least integer such
that
v_{p}\neq 0
. It is clearly enough to prove that
v_{p}\in V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
,
because we can then repeat the same argument for
v-v_{p}
. Our goal is therefore to
show that
\mathsf{Y}v_{p}=\mathsf{H}v_{p}=0
.

124
.

From the fact that
\mathsf{Y}v=0
and the Hodge decomposition, we deduce that

\mathsf{Y}_{-1}v_{p}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}+\mathsf{Y}_{-1}v_{p+1}=0.

From the fact that
\mathsf{H}v=0
, we get the additional piece of information that

\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p}-2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}v_{p+1}=0.

In particular,
\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}
, which is interesting, because
\mathsf{Y}_{0}

and
\mathsf{H}_{0}
behave very differently under taking adjoints. We can use this different
behavior to show that
Q(\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})=0
. Since
(\mathsf{Y}_{0})^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}_{0}
and

(\mathsf{H}_{0})^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}_{0}
, we have

Q(2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})=-Q(\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})=Q(v_{p},\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p})=-Q(v_{p},2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p})=-Q(2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p}),

and therefore
Q(2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})=0
. Now we combine this with the identity in

20
. Because we already know that
\mathsf{Y}_{-1}v_{p}=0
, this gives

\displaystyle 0=Q(2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})

\displaystyle=Q(2\mathsf{Y}_{-1}\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p},v_{p})+Q(\mathsf{Y}_{0}\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})-Q(\mathsf{H}_{0}\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},v_{p})

\displaystyle=2Q(\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p})+Q(\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p})+Q(\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p})

\displaystyle=2Q(\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p})-4Q(\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}).

Here we used the fact that
(\mathsf{Y}_{-1})^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}_{1}
and
(\mathsf{H}_{0})^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}_{0}
, and also
\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p}=-2\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}
. Now
\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}\in V^{p,q}
, and
since
Q
is a polarization,

Q(\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p})=(-1)^{q}\lVert\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}\rVert^{2},

where
\lVert-\rVert
means the Hodge norm in the polarized Hodge structure
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
. Likewise,
\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p}\in V^{p+1,q-1}
, and so

Q(\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p},\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p})=(-1)^{q-1}\lVert\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p}\rVert^{2}.

Putting everything together, we find that

0=\lVert\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p}\rVert^{2}+2\lVert\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}\rVert^{2},

which clearly implies that
\mathsf{Y}_{1}v_{p}=0
and
\mathsf{Y}_{0}v_{p}=0
. But then also
\mathsf{H}_{0}v_{p}=0
, and so we have proved that
\mathsf{Y}v_{p}=\mathsf{H}v_{p}=0
, hence
v_{p}\in V^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
. This finishes the proof of
6
.

125
.

The next step is to establish some basic functoriality. For the sake of clarity, let
us denote the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtrations on
S_{m}
and
V
by the symbols

F^{\bullet}S_{m}
and
F^{\bullet}V
; the resulting Hodge structures have weight
m

and
n
. We get an induced filtration on
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
according to the rule

F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon S_{m}\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$f(F^{p}S_{m})\subseteq F^{p+k}V$ for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

The induced
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation is easy to describe: for
f\colon S_{m}\to V
, one has

(\mathsf{H}f)(v)=\mathsf{H}f(v)-f(\mathsf{H}v),\quad(\mathsf{X}f)(v)=\mathsf{X}f(v)-f(\mathsf{X}v),\quad(\mathsf{Y}f)(v)=\mathsf{Y}f(v)-f(\mathsf{Y}v).

Observe that
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts trivially on a linear mapping
f\colon S_{m}\to V
exactly
when
f
is a morphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representations.

126
.

Since it is important, let us check very carefully that
F^{\bullet}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)

is indeed an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration (of weight
n-m
).

Lemma 21
.

Suppose that
F^{\bullet}V
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration of weight
n
. Then
the filtration
F^{\bullet}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration of
weight
n-m
.

Proof.

We need to check that the filtration on
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
satisfies the two
conditions in the definition. We can again use the modified trace pairing on

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
as a polarization. Given a linear mapping
f\colon S_{m}\to V
, we denote by
f^{\dagger}\colon V\to S_{m}
the adjoint with respect to the
nondegenerate pairings
Q_{S_{m}}
and
Q_{V}
; to be precise,

Q_{V}\bigl(f(v),s\bigr)=Q_{S_{m}}\bigl(v,f^{\dagger}(s)\bigr)\quad\text{for all $v\in V$ and $s\in S_{m}$.}

On
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
, we have the hermitian pairing

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)}\to\mathbb{C},\quad(f,g)\mapsto\frac{\tr(g^{\dagger}\circ f)}{\dim S_{m}}.

(126.1)

Let
f\in F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
be arbitrary. For any
s\in F^{p}S_{m}
, we have
f(s)\in F^{p+k}V
, and therefore

(\mathsf{H}f)(s)=\mathsf{H}f(s)-f(\mathsf{H}s)\in\mathsf{H}(F^{p+k}V)+f(F^{p}S_{m})\subseteq F^{p+k}V,

which proves that
\mathsf{H}f\in F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
. Similarly,
\mathsf{Y}f\in F^{k-1}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
.

It remains to show that the filtration
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{\bullet}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)

defines a Hodge structure of weight
n-m
on
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
, polarized by
the pairing in (
126.1
). Since

(e^{\mathsf{Y}}f)(v)=e^{\mathsf{Y}}f\bigl(e^{-\mathsf{Y}}v\bigr),

it is not hard to see that

e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon S_{m}\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$f\bigl(e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p}S_{m}\bigr)\subseteq e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p+k}V$
for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$}\thinspace\bigr\};

but the right-hand side is exactly the Hodge filtration of the induced Hodge
structure of weight
n-m
on
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
. The proof that the
pairing in (
126.1
) polarizes this Hodge structure is similar to the
proof of
12
.
∎

127
.

We can now prove
12
. Let
F^{\bullet}V
be an

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration on
V
, of weight
n
. Our starting point is the
decomposition in (
93.1
). Fix some
m\in\mathbb{N}
. According to

21
, the induced filtration

F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon S_{m}\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$f(F^{p}S_{m})\subseteq F^{p+k}V$ for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$}\thinspace\bigr\}

is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration of weight
n-m
, and the modified trace pairing

\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)}\to\mathbb{C},\quad(f,g)\mapsto\frac{\tr(g^{\dagger}\circ f)}{\dim S_{m}}

is a polarization.
6
tells us that the subspace

H_{m}=\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}\subseteq\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)

has a Hodge structure of weight
n-m
, with Hodge filtration

F^{k}H_{m}=F^{k}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)\cap H_{m}.

Moreover, we know that this Hodge structure is polarized by the restriction of the
modified trace pairing. But for
f,g\in H_{m}
, the composition
g^{\dagger}\circ f

is an endomorphism of
S_{m}
as an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation, hence (by Schur’s
lemma) a multiple of the identity. Thus
g^{\dagger}\circ f=c(f,g)\id
for some
constant
c(f,g)\in\mathbb{C}
, and because of how we defined the modified trace pairing,
this says exactly that

c\colon H_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}

polarizes the Hodge structure on
H_{m}=\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
.

Lemma 22
.

The evaluation morphism

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H_{m}\to V

induces an isomorphism between the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtrations on both sides.

Proof.

We know that the mapping is an isomorphism of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representations. By
the same argument as in
17
, one shows that the
isomorphism is compatible with the hermitian pairings on both sides. Let

S_{m}=\bigoplus_{p+q=m}S_{m}^{p,q}\quad\text{and}\quad H_{m}=\bigoplus_{p+q=n-m}H_{m}^{p,q}

denote the Hodge decompositions in the Hodge structures on
S_{m}
and
W
. By
construction,
H_{m}
is a sub-Hodge structure of
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)
, and so

H_{m}^{p,q}=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon S_{m}\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$f(S_{m}^{j,m-j})\subseteq V^{j+p,m-j+q}$ for all $j\in\mathbb{Z}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

We are going to argue that the evaluation morphism is a morphism of Hodge
structures of weight
n
. Let
p\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer. As with any tensor
product, the
(p,n-p)
-subspace in the Hodge decomposition of the left-hand side is

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}S_{m}^{j,m-j}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H_{m}^{p-j,n-m-p+j};

the evaluation morphism takes this into the subspace
V^{p,n-p}
, and is therefore
a morphism of Hodge structures of weight
n
. Since morphisms of Hodge structures
strictly respect the Hodge filtration, we get isomorphisms

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{j}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}F^{p-j}H_{m}\cong e^{\mathsf{Y}}F^{p}V

between the Hodge filtrations on both sides. After multiplying by
e^{-\mathsf{Y}}
,
this gives us the desired isomorphisms

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}F^{j}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}F^{p-j}H_{m}\cong F^{p}V.\qed

128
.

By construction, the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration on each
S_{m}
comes from a polarized

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
m
. If we let
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
act trivially on
the Hodge structure
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}
, the direct sum

\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{m},V)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}

inherits a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
m+(n-m)=n
. This puts an

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
on
V
, and the lemma above says that the

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge filtration
F^{\bullet}V
agrees with the total Hodge filtration of
this
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure, and that the polarizations are compatible.

12
is therefore proved in full.

Note
.

A posteriori, we can deduce from
18
that
\mathsf{H}_{0}=0
; but this
fact does not play a role during the proof.

129
.

In fact, one can improve
12
by taking out the
assumption that
V
is a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. This gives a minimal set of
conditions by which one can recognize a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure.
Interestingly, a version of this result is used in the proof of the decomposition
theorem
[
dCM
]
, in order to establish the relative Hard Lefschetz theorem. This
is another place where the global theory (cohomology with
coefficients in a polarized variation of Hodge structure) and the local theory
(degenerating variations of Hodge structure) meet rather unexpectedly.

130
.

Here is the minimal set of conditions. This time, we only assume that

V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{k}

is a finite-dimensional graded complex vector space; we let
\mathsf{H}\in\End(V)
be the
semisimple operator that acts as multiplication by
k
on the subspace
V_{k}
. Let

\mathsf{Y}\in\End(V)
be a nilpotent operator such that
[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y}
; note
that we are
not
assuming that
\mathsf{Y}^{k}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}
is an
isomorphism. Further, let
Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
be a
hermitian pairing such that
\mathsf{Y}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}
and
\mathsf{H}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}
,
where the dagger means the adjoint with respect to
Q
. Lastly, suppose that we have
a decreasing filtration
F
on the vector space
V
, with the following two properties:

(a)

One has
\mathsf{Y}(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet-1}
and
\mathsf{H}(F^{\bullet})=F^{\bullet}
.

(b)

The filtration
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F
is the Hodge filtration of a polarized
Hodge structure of weight
n
on
V
, polarized by the pairing
Q
(which is
therefore nondegenerate).

Corollary 5
.

Under these assumptions,
F
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
;
consequently,
V
is an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
,
polarized by the hermitian pairing
Q
.

Proof.

We are going to argue that
\mathsf{Y}^{k}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}
is an isomorphism
for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This implies that
\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Y}
determine a
representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on
V
, and together with the other conditions
above, is enough for applying
12
. An equivalent
formulation is that the increasing filtration

W_{k}=V_{k}\oplus V_{k-1}\oplus V_{k-2}\oplus\dotsb

is the monodromy weight filtration of the nilpotent operator
\mathsf{Y}
. By
assumption,
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F\in D
. Let us show that
e^{-z\mathsf{Y}}F\in D
for
every
z\in\mathbb{H}
. The two operators
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}
and

e^{i\operatorname{Im}z\,\mathsf{Y}}
belong to the real Lie group
G=\Aut(V,Q)
, due to the fact
that
\mathsf{H}^{\dagger}=-\mathsf{H}
and
\mathsf{Y}^{\dagger}=\mathsf{Y}
. From the relation

[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y}
and the fact that
\mathsf{H}(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet}
, we thus get

e^{-z\mathsf{Y}}F=e^{-i\operatorname{Im}z\mathsf{Y}}e^{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\mathsf{Y}}F=e^{-i\operatorname{Im}z\,\mathsf{Y}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}\cdot e^{\mathsf{Y}}F\in D.

Because
\mathsf{Y}(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet-1}
, the mapping

\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to D,\quad\Phi(z)=e^{-z\mathsf{Y}}F,

is the period mapping of a polarized variation of Hodge structure of weight
n
,
with monodromy operator
T=e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{Y}}
. In our usual notation, we
therefore have
N=-\mathsf{Y}
.

Now comes the crucial point: because of the Hodge norm estimates, we can recognize the
monodromy weight filtration of
N
– and hence that of
\mathsf{Y}
– by the order of
growth of the Hodge norm. Let
v\in V_{k}
be any nonzero vector. As long as
\operatorname{Im}z

remains bounded, we have

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=\bigl\lVert e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,\mathsf{H}}e^{i\operatorname{Im}z\,\mathsf{Y}}v\bigr\rVert_{e^{\mathsf{Y}}F}^{2}\sim\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}\cdot\lVert v\rVert_{e^{\mathsf{Y}}F}^{2},

and so according to
1
, the filtration
W
we defined
above must be the monodromy weight filtration of
\mathsf{Y}
. By construction,
\gr_{k}^{W}\cong V_{k}
, and so
\mathsf{Y}^{k}\colon V_{k}\to V_{-k}
is an isomorphism for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

6
The Hodge norm estimates

131
.

In this chapter, we use the results about harmonic bundles that we derived in

Section

3
to prove the Hodge norm estimates. The point of these estimates is to
control the order of growth of the Hodge norm of a multi-valued flat section in terms
of the monodromy weight filtration of the nilpotent operator
N
.

Note
.

In fact, exactly the same argument proves the Hodge norm estimates for an arbitrary
harmonic bundle on the punctured disk whose Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
is nilpotent.

6.1
Boundedness of the Hodge norm for flat sections

132
.

Let us start by showing that, because of the derivative bound in
(
60.1
), the Hodge norm of any multi-valued flat section can grow
at most like a power of
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
.

Proposition 7
.

Let
v\in V
be a multi-valued flat section. Then

\displaystyle\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{2C_{0}\pi}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2C_{0}},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2C_{0}}\Bigr)\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}

\displaystyle\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{2C_{0}\pi}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2C_{0}},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2C_{0}}\Bigr)\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}

on the horizontal strip
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert\leq\pi
, where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
.

Proof.

Assuming that
v\neq 0
, we consider the function
\varphi=\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}
. Set
z=x+iy
. Then

\bigl\lvert\varphi(x+iy)-\varphi(-1)\bigr\rvert\leq\bigl\lvert\varphi(x+iy)-\varphi(-1+iy)\bigr\rvert+\bigl\lvert\varphi(-1+iy)-\varphi(-1)\bigr\rvert.

The derivative bound in (
60.1
) implies that

\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial x}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}+\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

with a similar inequality for
\partial\varphi/\partial y
. Therefore

\bigl\lvert\varphi(x+iy)-\varphi(-1+iy)\bigr\rvert\leq\left\lvert\int_{-1}^{x}\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert t\rvert}\mathit{dt}\right\rvert=4C_{0}\bigl\lvert\log\lvert x\rvert\bigr\rvert;

in the same manner, we have for
\lvert y\rvert\leq\pi
the inequality

\bigl\lvert\varphi(-1+iy)-\varphi(-1)\bigr\rvert\leq 4C_{0}\lvert y\rvert\leq 4C_{0}\pi.

Putting everything together, we get

-4C_{0}\pi-4C_{0}\bigl\lvert\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\bigr\rvert\leq\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}-\log\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}\leq 4C_{0}\pi+4C_{0}\bigl\lvert\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\bigr\rvert,

from which the desired inequalities follow by exponentiation.
∎

133
.

The following result is a first step towards more precise estimates for Hodge norms
of multi-valued flat sections. The proof is disarmingly simple.

Proposition 8
.

Let
v\in V
be a multi-valued flat section such that
Tv=\lambda v

for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
. Then for every
x_{0}<0
, we have the inequality

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq e^{4C_{0}/\lvert x_{0}\rvert}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(x_{0})}

for all
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
; here
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
.

Using the Jordan decomposition
T=T_{s}e^{2\pi i\,N}
, the condition
Tv=\lambda v
is equivalent to
T_{s}v=\lambda v
and
Nv=0
. The inequality in the
proposition is therefore a special case of the Hodge norm estimates for multi-valued
flat sections. Indeed,
Nv=0
implies that
v\in W_{0}
, and so we expect the Hodge
norm of
v
to remain bounded as
\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty
. It turns out that this special
case is all that is needed to prove the Hodge norm estimates in general: the power of
the statement comes from the fact that it applies to
all
polarized variations
of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
. The universal character of the
inequality also makes it very useful for studying variations of Hodge structure in
several variables, as we plan to show in a sequel to this paper.

134
.

Now for the proof of the proposition. The semisimple part of the monodromy
transformation
T=T_{s}e^{2\pi iN}
give rise to a decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}E_{\lambda}(T_{s}),

and each eigenspace
E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
is preserved by
N
. Let us consider a fixed nonzero
vector
v\in E_{\lambda}(T_{s})\cap\ker N
. As in the proof of the monodromy
theorem (in
3
), these conditions imply that the function

\varphi=\log h(v,v)

is invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
. In addition, we have
already proved that
\varphi
is smooth and subharmonic
(
6
), and that

\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial z}\right\rvert=\left\lvert\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{2C_{0}}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

Recall that we derived this inequality in (
60.1
); here
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
.

Example 5
.

To get a feeling for these conditions, let us consider a toy example: a
smooth function
f\colon(-\infty,0)\to\mathbb{R}
with the property that
f^{\prime\prime}\geq 0

and
\lvert f^{\prime}(x)\rvert\leq C\lvert x\rvert^{-1}
. Since
f^{\prime\prime}\geq 0
, the function

f^{\prime}
must be increasing, and therefore

0=\lim_{x\to-\infty}f^{\prime}(x)\leq f^{\prime}.

But this means that
f
is itself increasing, and so we get

f(x)\leq f(x_{0})

for every
x\leq x_{0}<0
. In particular,
f
is bounded above as
x\to-\infty
. A similar convexity argument also shows up in Mochizuki’s work on the
asymptotic behavior of tame harmonic bundles
[
Mochizuki
, Lem. 2.23]
.

135
.

In order to apply the same reasoning as in the example, we need a function that
only depends on
x=\operatorname{Re}z
. We therefore consider the vertical averages

f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varphi(x+iy)\mathit{dy}.

Differentiation under the integral sign gives

f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varphi_{x,x}(x+iy)\mathit{dy}\geq-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varphi_{y,y}(x+iy)\mathit{dy},

since
\Delta\varphi=\varphi_{x,x}+\varphi_{y,y}\geq 0
. The integral evaluates to

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\varphi_{y,y}(x+iy)\mathit{dy}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\Bigl(\varphi_{y}(x+2\pi i)-\varphi_{y}(x)\Bigr)=0,

due to the fact that
\varphi(x+iy)
is periodic in
y
of period
2\pi
.
Therefore
f(x)
is a convex function of
x
. Moreover, we know from
(
60.1
) that

\lvert\varphi_{x}(x+iy)\rvert\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert},

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{n+1}{3}}
. Plugging this into the integral from
above gives

\lvert f^{\prime}(x)\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\lvert\varphi_{x}(x+iy)\rvert\mathit{dy}\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}.

As in the example, it follows that
f(x)\leq f(x_{0})
for every
x\leq x_{0}<0
.

136
.

All that is left is to relate the behavior of
\varphi(x+iy)
to that of
f(x)
.
We have

-\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}\leq\varphi_{y}(x+iy)\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert},

which can be integrated to give

-\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}u\leq\varphi(x+iy+iu)-\varphi(x+iy)\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}u

for all
u\geq 0
. If we now average over
u\in[0,2\pi]
, we get

-\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}\leq f(x)-\varphi(x+iy)\leq\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}.

For
x\leq x_{0}<0
, we have
f(x)\leq f(x_{0})
, and therefore

\varphi(x+iy)\leq f(x)+\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}\leq f(x_{0})+\frac{4C_{0}}{\lvert x\rvert}\leq\varphi(x_{0})+\frac{8C_{0}}{\lvert x_{0}\rvert}.

This gives the desired upper bound for
\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}
after exponentiation.

6.2
The comparison theorem

137
.

One striking consequence of
8
is that the behavior of the
Hodge norm as
t\to 0
only depends on the underlying flat vector bundle
(E,d)
.
Simpson proves a similar result for tame harmonic bundles, but with conditions that
appear to be much more restrictive
[
Simpson
, Cor. 4.3]
.

Theorem 13
.

Let
E_{1},E_{2}
be polarized variations of Hodge structure over
\Delta^{\ast}
. If

(E_{1},d_{1})
and
(E_{2},d_{2})
are isomorphic as smooth vector bundles with
connection, then for every
0<r<1
, there is a constant
C_{r}>0
such that

C_{r}^{-1}\cdot h_{E_{1}}\leq h_{E_{2}}\leq C_{r}\cdot h_{E_{1}}

at all points
t\in\Delta^{\ast}
with
\lvert t\rvert\leq r
.

Proof.

Let
f\colon E_{1}\to E_{2}
be an isomorphism between the underlying smooth vector
bundles that preserves the connections. We consider
f
as a single-valued flat
section of the bundle
\Hom(E_{1},E_{2})
. Since this bundle underlies a polarized
variation of Hodge structure,
8
applies to it. Taking

x_{0}=\log r
in the statement, we get a bound for the pointwise Hodge norm of

f
, of the form

h_{\Hom(E_{1},E_{2})}(f,f)\leq C_{r},

valid for every
t\in\Delta^{\ast}
with
\lvert t\rvert\leq r
. Here the constant
C_{r}
equals

e^{8C_{0}/\lvert\log r\rvert}
times the value of the Hodge norm at the point
t=r
.
Since the Hodge norm is an upper bound for the pointwise operator norm of
f
,
this gives us the inequality

h_{E_{2}}\leq C_{r}\cdot h_{E_{1}}.

The reverse inequality follows by applying the same reasoning to the inverse
isomorphism
f^{-1}\colon E_{2}\to E_{1}
.
∎

6.3
Order of growth and the weight filtration

138
.

Let
W=W_{\bullet}V
be the monodromy weight filtration of the nilpotent operator

N
. Our next goal is to prove that the weight filtration controls the behavior of
the function
h(v,v)
as
\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty
, at least on horizontal strips of
bounded height. The idea is to construct another variation of Hodge structure on the
same underlying flat vector bundle
(E,d)
, using representation theory.

13
guarantees that the Hodge metrics of the two variations
are equivalent up to a constant, and this implies the Hodge norm estimates.

139
.

To get started, we need to upgrade the nilpotent operator
N\in\End(V)

into a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
. Let us denote the standard basis
elements by
\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
; then

[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{X}]=2\mathsf{X},\quad[\mathsf{H},\mathsf{Y}]=-2\mathsf{Y},\quad[\mathsf{X},\mathsf{Y}]=\mathsf{H}.

As
N\in\End(V)
is nilpotent, one can find a semisimple endomorphism
H\in\End(V)
with integral eigenvalues, such that
[H,N]=-2N
. In fact, one can make a
more careful choice of
H
; the additional properties are going to be useful
for us later on.

Proposition 9
.

One can find
H\in\End(V)
with the following properties:

(a)

H
is semisimple with integral eigenvalues.

(b)

One has
[H,N]=-2N
and
W_{k}=E_{k}(H)\oplus W_{k-1}
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(c)

One has
Q(Hv,w)+Q(v,Hw)=0
for every
v,w\in V
.

(d)

H
commutes with
T_{s}
.

Proof.

The proof is easy, and so we only give a sketch. Consider an
arbitrary nilpotent endomorphism
N\in\End(V)
of a finite-dimensional vector
space
V
. Denote by
\Sigma(N)\subseteq\End(V)
the set of all semisimple
endomorphisms
H
with integer eigenvalues, such that
[H,N]=-2N

and
W_{j}=E_{j}(H)\oplus W_{j-1}
for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
. It is easy to see that

\Sigma(N)\neq\emptyset
, for example by choosing a basis that
puts
N
into Jordan canonical form. This already shows that splittings satisfying
(a) and (b) exist, a special case of the Jacobson-Morozov theorem.

Let us now compare two arbitrary elements
H,H^{\prime}\in\Sigma(N)
. The difference

H^{\prime}-H
commutes with
N
and satisfies
(H^{\prime}-H)(W_{\bullet})\subseteq W_{\bullet-1}
. Denote by
\ad H
the semisimple operator on
\End(V)
, defined as

(\ad H)(A)=[H,A]
; then
H^{\prime}-H
belongs to the direct sum of the eigenspaces

E_{k}(\ad H)
with
k\leq-1
. From this, one deduces that

H^{\prime}=e^{B}He^{-B}

for a unique endomorphism
B\in\End(V)
such that
[B,N]=0
and

B(W_{\bullet})\subseteq W_{\bullet-1}
. Conversely, for any
B\in\End(V)
with
these two properties, one has
e^{B}He^{-B}\in\Sigma(N)
.

Now we describe how to adjust a given splitting
H_{0}\in\Sigma(N)
so that
(c) holds. Suppose that
V
comes with a nondegenerate hermitian pairing

Q\colon V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
Q(Nv,w)=Q(v,Nw)
for all
v,w\in V
. For
A\in\End(V)
, denote by
A^{\dagger}\in\End(V)
the adjoint with
respect to
Q
; thus
N^{\dagger}=N
. It is easy to see that
-H_{0}^{\dagger}\in\Sigma(N)
, and so by the above, one has

-(H_{0})^{\dagger}=e^{B}H_{0}e^{-B}

for a unique
B\in\End(V)
with
[N,B]=0
and
B(W_{\bullet})\subseteq W_{\bullet-1}
; by uniqueness,
B^{\dagger}=B
. Consequently, the new splitting

H=e^{\frac{1}{2}B}H_{0}e^{-\frac{1}{2}B}\in\Sigma(N)

satisfies
H=-H^{\dagger}
, which is just a different way of writing (c).
If we suppose in addition that
S\in\End(V)
is semisimple, commutes with
N
,
and satisfies
S^{\dagger}=S
, then we can easily arrange that moreover
[H,S]=0

(by considering each eigenspace of
S

separately). This shows that splittings with all four properties exist.
∎

140
.

We now define a representation

\rho\colon\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to\End(V),\quad\rho(\mathsf{H})=H,\,\rho(\mathsf{Y})=-N.

The reason for using
-N
(instead of the seemingly more natural
N
) has to do with
the sign conventions for
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structures. With this choice, each eigenspace

E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
is a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
, and

V=\bigoplus_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}E_{\lambda}(T_{s}).

Moreover, the eigenspaces
E_{k}(H)
and
E_{\ell}(H)
are orthogonal with respect to
Q

unless
k=-\ell
.

6.4
Proof of the Hodge norm estimates

141
.

We can now prove the Hodge norm estimates in general.

Theorem 14
.

Let
E
be a polarized variation of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
. If
v\in V
is a
multi-valued flat section such that
v\in W_{k}
and
v\not\in W_{k-1}
, then
the function

\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-k}\cdot\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

is uniformly bounded on every region of the form
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}<0
and
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert\leq y_{0}
.

Proof.

As in
140
, we choose a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
on the
vector space

V=\bigoplus_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}E_{\lambda}(T_{s})

that commutes with
T_{s}
, is compatible with the pairing
Q
, and such that
\mathsf{Y}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts as the nilpotent operator
-N
. The representation is completely
reducible, hence

V\cong\bigoplus_{\lvert\lambda\rvert=1}\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl(S_{m},E_{\lambda}(T_{s})\bigr)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}.

Each
S_{m}
has a canonical
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
m
, and by putting
suitable Hodge structures on the vector spaces in the above
decomposition,
V

becomes an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
, polarized by the pairing
Q
.
By the construction in
109
, we can arrange that the
monodromy transformation of the resulting variation of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}

is equal to
T=T_{s}e^{2\pi iN}
. In this way, we obtain another polarized
variation of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
whose underlying flat vector bundle is
isomorphic to
(E,d)
.
13
shows that the two Hodge
metrics are mutually bounded up to a constant. Since we already know the Hodge
norm estimates for variations of Hodge structure coming from
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structures (by
112
), this finishes the proof of the Hodge norm
estimates in general.
∎

142
.

One can sharpen the Hodge norm estimates by using the decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{k}(H)

coming from the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation. If
v\in E_{k}(H)
is a nonzero in
the
k
-th weight space, then the Hodge norm estimates show that

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-k}

is bounded from above and below by positive constants (as long as
\operatorname{Im}z
lies in a
bounded interval). This suggests rescaling by the operator
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}
, because

e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{k}{2}}v,

The point of insisting that
H^{\dagger}=-H
is that
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}\in G
. Furthermore, we can remove the restriction on the imaginary part
by choosing a logarithm of
T_{s}
: a semisimple operator
S\in\End(V)
, with real
eigenvalues in a fixed half-open interval of length
1
, such that
T_{s}=e^{2\pi i\,S}
and
[S,N]=0
. The relation

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z+2\pi i)}^{2}=\lVert T^{-1}v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

means that the rescaled expression

\bigl\lVert e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

(142.1)

now depends only on
t=e^{z}
, and therefore descends to a smooth function on
\Delta^{\ast}

that is bounded from above and below by a positive constant as
t\to 0
. In

Section

8
, we are going to argue that (
142.1
) actually
converges to a positive definite hermitian inner product on
V
(as a consequence of
the nilpotent orbit theorem).

6.5
A more direct proof

143
.

We end this chapter by sketching another proof, without representation theory, for
the fact that the monodromy weight filtration governs the rate of growth of the Hodge
norm. In this section only, let us denote the monodromy weight filtration of the
nilpotent operator
N\in\End(V)
by the symbol
M_{\bullet}
. Let us also define
the growth order filtration

W_{k}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=O\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}\bigr)$ as $\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty$}\thinspace\bigr\},

again assuming that
\operatorname{Im}z
stays in a bounded interval. We are going to prove
directly that
W_{k}=M_{k}
for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

144
.

We know from
7
that
W_{k}=0
for
k\leq-2C_{0}
, and
W_{k}=V

for
k\geq 2C_{0}
. This means that the growth order filtration has finite length,
just like the monodromy weight filtration. Another simple observation is that

Q(W_{k},W_{\ell})=0\quad\text{if $k+\ell\leq-1$.}

(144.1)

Indeed, for
v\in W_{k}
and
w\in W_{\ell}
, we have

\lvert Q(v,w)\rvert^{2}\leq\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\lVert w\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=O\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k+\ell}\bigr).

As long as
k+\ell\leq-1
, the right-hand side is going to zero as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
; because
Q(v,w)
is constant, it follows that
Q(v,w)=0
.

145
.

We can use the basic estimate (in
1
) to show that

N(W_{\bullet})\subseteq W_{\bullet-2}
; recall that this is one of the two
conditions that characterize the monodromy weight filtration.

Lemma 23
.

We have
N(W_{k})\subseteq W_{k-2}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
4
. In fact, we proved that
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\lVert Nv\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq\frac{C}{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\quad\text{for $v\in V$ and $\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1$,}

and so
v\in W_{k}
implies that
Nv\in W_{k-2}
.
∎

146
.

The monodromy weight filtration has the property that
\ker N^{k+1}\subseteq M_{k}
.
So far, we only know (from
8
) that
\ker N\subseteq W_{0}
. The
following lemma generalizes this to arbitrary powers of
N
.

Lemma 24
.

We have
\ker N^{k+1}\subseteq W_{k}
for
k\geq 0
.

Proof.

Together with the triangle inequality,
8
implies that
\ker N\subseteq W_{0}
. Now consider any vector
v\in\ker N^{k+1}
. As in
95
, let

S_{k}
denote the irreducible representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
with
\dim S_{k}=k+1
. Define a linear mapping
f\colon S_{k}\to V
by setting

f(v_{j})=\frac{j!(k-j)!}{k!}(-N)^{j}v\quad\text{for $j=0,1,\dotsc,k$;}

then
f(v_{0})=v
and
f\circ\mathsf{Y}=-N\circ f
. According to

Section

5.4
,
S_{k}
is the space of multi-valued flat sections of a
polarized variation of Hodge structure of weight
k
, whose monodromy operator is
T=e^{-2\pi i\mathsf{Y}}
; we also know from
112
that the Hodge norm of
any vector in
S_{k}
can
grow at most like
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}
. Consequently,
\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{k},V)
is the space
of multi-valued flat sections of a polarized variation of Hodge structure of weight

n-k
, and
f\in\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}(S_{k},V)
satisfies
Nf=0
. By
8
,
the Hodge norm of
f
is bounded; since the Hodge norm is an upper bound for the
operator norm, it follows that
\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=O\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}\bigr)
.
∎

147
.

We recall two facts about the monodromy weight filtration; as we are only sketching
the proof, we omit the details. First,

M_{k}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}N^{j}\bigl(\ker N^{k+2j+1}\bigr)\quad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

Second, since
N^{{\dagger}}=N
is self-adjoint with respect to the hermitian pairing

Q
, one has

M_{k}^{\perp}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in M_{k}$}\thinspace\bigr\}=M_{-k-1}\quad\text{for $k\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

We can use this to prove that
W_{k}=M_{k}
. By
24
, we have

M_{k}=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}N^{j}\bigl(\ker N^{k+2j+1}\bigr)\subseteq\sum_{j\in\mathbb{N}}N^{j}\bigl(W_{k+2j}\bigr)\subseteq W_{k},

where the last inclusion is due to
23
. Dually, we have

W_{k}\subseteq W_{-k-1}^{\perp}\subseteq M_{-k-1}^{\perp}=M_{k},

where we used (
144.1
) for the first inclusion. It follows that
W_{k}=M_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and so the filtration by order of growth of the Hodge
metric is indeed equal to the monodromy weight filtration.

7
The nilpotent orbit theorem

148
.

This chapter is devoted to the proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem. The theorem has
several parts, and it is going to take us some time to prove all of them. Our first
goal is to prove that the “untwisted period mapping”

\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\check{D},\quad\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z),

extends holomorphically to the entire disk
\Delta
. The general idea is to construct
a holomorphic extension of the Higgs field
\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
with the
help of Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates, and then to use this extension to prove that

\Psi_{S}
is meromorphic at
t=0
. This is enough because
\check{D}
is a projective complex
manifold.

7.1
Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates

149
.

We need only the most basic version of Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates for vector
bundles, in one complex dimension. The technique, in the special case of the
trivial bundle, is explained very nicely in
[
Berndtsson
, Lecture 1]
.
We include an elementary proof for arbitrary vector bundles here, both in order to
keep the paper self-contained, and to show the reader that the
L^{2}
-estimates in one
dimension are not difficult – in fact, most of the argument is just integration by
parts.

150
.

Let
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}
be a domain in
\mathbb{C}
, with the usual Euclidean metric
\omega=\frac{i}{2}\mathit{dz}\wedge d\bar{z}
. Let
E
be a smooth vector bundle on
X
with a
hermitian metric
h
, and suppose that
E
has the structure of a holomorphic vector
bundle; this corresponds to a connection
d^{\prime\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{0,1}(X,E)
of
type
(0,1)
; the integrability condition
(d^{\prime\prime})^{2}=0
is automatically satisfied in
dimension one. One technique for constructing holomorphic sections of
E
is to solve
the
\bar{\partial}
-equation

d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}

for a given section
f\in A^{0}(X,E)
. The general idea is that this can be done,
provided
h
has positive curvature. Let us write the Chern connection
in the form
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
, where
\delta^{\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1,0}(X,E)
is a
connection of type
(1,0)
. Let

\Theta=(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime})^{2}\in A^{1,1}\bigl(X,\End(E)\bigr)

be the curvature operator of the metric
h
. The main result is as follows

[
Demailly
, VIII.§6]
.

Theorem 15

(Hörmander)
.

Suppose that there is a positive function
\rho\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
such
that

\int_{X}h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,\alpha,\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu\geq\int_{X}\rho^{2}h(\alpha,\alpha)\,d\mu\quad\text{for all $\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)$.}

Let
f\in A^{0}(X,E)
be an arbitrary smooth section. Then the equation
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
has a solution
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
that moreover satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate

\int_{X}h(u,u)\,d\mu\leq\int_{X}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}h(f,f)\,d\mu,

provided that the integral on the right-hand side is finite.

151
.

The proof uses one nontrivial (but well-known) fact, namely the regularity of the

\bar{\partial}
-equation in one complex dimension. Let
f\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be a smooth
function. Suppose that a measurable function
u\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
is a
weak
solution
to the equation

\frac{\partial u}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}=f,

(151.1)

meaning that for every compactly supported smooth function
\varphi\in A_{c}^{0}(X)
, one has

\int_{X}f\varphi\,d\mu=-\int_{X}u\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,d\mu.

Then after modifying
u
on a set of measure zero, if necessary, the function
u
is
smooth and solves (
151.1
) in the usual sense.

152
.

Now we turn to the proof of
15
. We start by using
integration by parts to relate the two operators
d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}
and

\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}
.

Lemma 25
.

Let
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
be a smooth section, and let
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
be a smooth
section with compact support. Then

\int_{X}h\bigl(d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}u,\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}h\bigl(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu.

Proof.

Since
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
is a metric connection, we have

\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}h(u,\alpha)=h\bigl(d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}u,\alpha\bigr)+h\bigl(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr).

Now integrate over
X
and use Stokes’ theorem to get the result. ∎

153
.

The general idea, which should be familiar from the proof of Hodge’s theorem about
harmonic forms, is to construct a “weak solution” to the equation by Hilbert space
techniques. We let
L^{2}(X,E)
be the Hilbert space of all measurable
sections of the bundle
E
with finite
L^{2}
-norm

\lVert u\rVert_{h}^{2}=\int_{X}h(u,u)\,d\mu.

We say that
u\in L^{2}(X,E)
is a
weak solution
of the equation
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
if

\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}h(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha)\,d\mu

for every smooth section
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
with compact support. The
regularity theory of the
\bar{\partial}
-operator implies that weak solutions are
actually smooth.

Proposition 10
.

Let
f\in A^{0}(X,E)
. If
u\in L^{2}(X,E)
is a weak solution to the equation

d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
, then
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
and
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
in the usual sense.

Proof.

This is a local problem; after replacing
X
by an open neighborhood of a
given point, we may assume that
E
is a trivial holomorphic bundle of rank
r
. Let

s_{1},\dotsc,s_{r}\in A^{0}(X,E)
be a holomorphic frame, and define the smooth
functions

h_{i,j}=h(s_{i},s_{j}).

The
r\times r
-matrix with entries
h_{i,j}
is hermitian and positive definite;
let
h^{i,j}
be the entries of the inverse matrix. A simple calculation shows
that

\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}s_{i}=\sum_{j,k}\frac{\partial h_{i,j}}{\partial z}h^{j,k}s_{k}

Now let
\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}s_{i}\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
be an arbitrary smooth
section of
E
with compact support. Since
\delta^{\prime}
is a connection of type

(1,0)
, we have

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\frac{\partial\alpha_{i}}{\partial z}s_{i}+\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}s_{i}=\sum_{i,j,k}\Bigl(\frac{\partial\alpha_{i}}{\partial z}h_{i,j}h^{j,k}+\alpha_{i}\frac{\partial h_{i,j}}{\partial z}h^{j,k}\Bigr)s_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k}\frac{\partial(\alpha_{i}h_{i,j})}{\partial z}h^{j,k}s_{k}.

Write the given section
u\in L^{2}(X,E)
as
u=\sum_{i}u_{i}s_{i}
, with
measurable functions
u_{i}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
. Since
h
is
conjugate-linear in the second argument, we get

h(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha)=\sum_{i,j,k,\ell}u_{\ell}h_{\ell,k}\frac{\partial(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{i}h_{j,i})}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}h^{k,j}=\sum_{i,j}u_{j}\frac{\partial(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{i}h_{j,i})}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}.

Because
u
is a weak solution to
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
, we therefore have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\sum_{i,j}h_{j,i}f_{j}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{i}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu

\displaystyle=-\int_{X}h\bigl(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}\sum_{i,j}u_{j}\frac{\partial(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{i}h_{j,i})}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,d\mu,

where
f=\sum_{j}f_{j}s_{j}
. Now let
\varphi_{1},\dotsc,\varphi_{r}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be
arbitrary compactly supported smooth functions, and set

\alpha=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}s_{i}=\sum_{i,j}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{j}h^{j,i}s_{i};

with this choice, we have
\varphi_{j}=\sum_{i}h_{j,i}\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}_{i}
, and
so the identity from above becomes

\int_{X}\sum_{j}f_{j}\varphi_{j}\,d\mu=-\int_{X}\sum_{j}u_{j}\frac{\partial\varphi_{j}}{\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,d\mu.

This shows that each coefficient function
u_{j}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
by itself is a
weak solution of the ordinary
\bar{\partial}
-equation
\partial u_{j}/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}=f_{j}
. By standard regularity theory, we can modify each
u_{j}
on a set of measure
zero and make it smooth; then
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
. We can now use integration by
parts, as in
25
, and deduce that

\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}h\bigl(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu=\int_{X}h\bigl(d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}u,\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu

for every
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
. But this means exactly that
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
.
∎

154
.

To find the desired weak solution, we use the Riesz representation theorem. The image
of the linear mapping

\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\colon A_{c}^{0}(X,E)\to L^{2}(X,E)

(154.1)

is a linear subspace of
L^{2}(X,E)
, in general not closed. On this subspace, we can
define a conjugate-linear functional by the formula

\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\mapsto-\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu.

The following lemma shows that this is well-defined and bounded.

Lemma 26
.

For every
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
, we have

\left\lvert\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu\right\rvert\leq\left(\int_{X}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}h(f,f)\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{X}h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu\right)^{1/2},

where
\rho\colon X\to\mathbb{R}
is the positive function from
15
.

Proof.

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives

\left\lvert\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu\right\rvert\leq\left(\int_{X}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}h(f,f)\,d\mu\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{X}\rho^{2}h(\alpha,\alpha)\,d\mu\right)^{1/2},

and in view of the inequality in
15
, it is therefore
enough to prove that

\int_{X}h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,\alpha,\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu\leq\int_{X}h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu.

The curvature operator is
\Theta=\delta^{\prime}d^{\prime\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}\delta^{\prime}
, and so

h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,\alpha,\alpha\bigr)=h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\alpha,\alpha\bigr)-h\bigl(d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha,\alpha\bigr).

If we integrate over
X
and apply
25
, we get

\int_{X}h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,\alpha,\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu=\int_{X}h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu-\int_{X}h\bigl(d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\alpha,d^{\prime\prime}_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu.

Since the second term is negative, this gives us the inequality we need.
∎

155
.

The inequality in
26
says that the conjugate-linear functional

\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\mapsto-\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu

is well-defined, bounded, and of norm at most
\lVert f/\rho\rVert_{h}
. By continuity, it
extends uniquely to a conjugate-linear functional

L^{2}(X,E)\to\mathbb{C}

that is identically zero on the orthogonal complement of the image of
(
154.1
); its norm is of course still at most

\lVert f/\rho\rVert_{h}
. By the Riesz representation theorem, this conjugate-linear
functional is in turn represented by a unique element
u\in L^{2}(X,E)
. By
construction, we have

\int_{X}h\bigl(u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}\alpha\bigr)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}h(f,\alpha)\,d\mu

for every
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
, and so
u
is a weak solution to the equation

d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
. This weak solution also satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate because

\int_{X}h(u,u)\,d\mu=\lVert u\rVert_{h}^{2}\leq\lVert f/\rho\rVert_{h}^{2}=\int_{X}\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}h(f,f)\,d\mu.

Now an application of
10
finishes the proof of

15
.

156
.

If
v\in L^{2}(X,E)
is orthogonal to the image of
(
154.1
), then

\int_{X}h(d_{\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}^{\prime\prime}v,\alpha)\,d\mu=-\int_{X}h(v,\delta_{\partial/\partial z}^{\prime}\alpha)\,d\mu=0

for every
\alpha\in A_{c}^{0}(X,E)
, and so
v\in A^{0}(X,E)
is smooth and
satisfies
d^{\prime\prime}v=0
, hence is holomorphic. This means that the solution
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
constructed above is orthogonal to the space of holomorphic sections of

E
, and so it is the (unique) solution of the equation
d^{\prime\prime}u=fd\bar{z}
with
minimal
L^{2}
-norm.

7.2
Hodge bundles and metrics with positive curvature

157
.

Now let
E
be a polarized variation of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
. In order to apply
Hörmander’s theory to the Hodge bundles, we need a metric with positive curvature.
According to
2
, the curvature operator of the Hodge
metric
h
on the holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}^{p,q}
is

\Theta=-(\theta\theta^{\ast}+\theta^{\ast}\theta).

For any smooth section
u\in A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})
, we therefore have

h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\bigr)=h\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u,\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u\bigr)-h\bigl(\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u,\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u\bigr).

The right-hand side is unfortunately not positive, but at least we know that the
troublesome second term can be no bigger than

h\bigl(\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u,\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u\bigr)\leq h\bigl(\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}},\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\bigr)h(u,u)\leq\frac{1}{4}\binom{r+1}{3}\frac{1}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}h(u,u),

using the universal bound for the Higgs field in
1
. (Here
r=\rk E
.)

158
.

We can try to fix this problem by multiplying the Hodge metric by a factor of the
form
e^{-\varphi}
, where
\varphi\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
is a suitable weight function.
Let us denote the new hermitian metric temporarily by the symbol
h^{\varphi}=he^{-\varphi}
. Since

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}h^{\varphi}(v,w)

\displaystyle=\bar{\partial}h(v,w)\cdot e^{-\varphi}+h(v,w)e^{-\varphi}(-\bar{\partial}\varphi)

\displaystyle=h^{\varphi}(d^{\prime\prime}v,w)+h^{\varphi}(u,\delta^{\prime}v)-h^{\varphi}(u,\partial\varphi),

the new Chern connection is
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}-\partial\varphi
, and
so the new curvature operator is

\Theta^{\varphi}=(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}-\partial\varphi)^{2}=\Theta+\partial\bar{\partial}\varphi.

Our inequality from above now changes into

h^{\varphi}\bigl(\Theta^{\varphi}_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\bigr)=h^{\varphi}\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u,\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u\bigr)-h^{\varphi}\bigl(\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u,\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}}u\bigr)+\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}\cdot h^{\varphi}(u,u).

The right-hand side is greater or equal to

h^{\varphi}(u,u)\cdot\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}-\frac{1}{4}\binom{r+1}{3}\frac{1}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}\right),

and as long as the term in parentheses is positive, the metric
h^{\varphi}
has positive
curvature. The following lemma gives us a family of suitable weight functions.

Lemma 27
.

For
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}
, the function
e^{-\varphi}=\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}
satisfies

\frac{\partial^{2}\varphi}{\partial t\partial\bar{t}}=\frac{b}{4\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}

Proof.

A small calculation, which can be made easier by pulling back to
\mathbb{H}
.
∎

159
.

This gives us a nice family of hermitian metrics on the Hodge bundles to which we can
apply Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates.

Corollary 6
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
, subject to the condition
b\geq\binom{r+1}{3}+1
, where
r=\rk E
. Let
f\in A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})
be a smooth
section with the property that

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(f,f)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu<+\infty.

Then there is a smooth section
u\in A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})
that solves the

\bar{\partial}
-equation
\bar{\partial}u=fd\bar{t}
and also satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(u,u)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq 4\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(f,f)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu.

Proof.

We apply
15
to the Hodge bundle
E^{p,q}
,
with the holomorphic structure defined by the operator
\bar{\partial}
and with the
hermitian metric

h^{\varphi}=h\cdot\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}

For
b\geq\binom{r+1}{3}+1
, this metric has positive curvature; in fact, we
now have

h^{\varphi}\Bigl(\Theta^{\varphi}_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\Bigr)\geq h^{\varphi}(u,u)\cdot\frac{1}{4\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}

(159.1)

for every smooth section
u\in A^{0}(\Delta^{\ast},E^{p,q})
. The assumptions of the theorem
are therefore met with
1/\rho^{2}=4\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}
.
∎

160
.

In what follows, we are actually going to apply the
L^{2}
-estimates to the induced
variation of Hodge structure on the bundle
\End(E)
. Because of the better bound in

2
, the result takes the following form.

Corollary 7
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
, subject to the condition
b\geq 2r\binom{r+1}{3}+1
. Let
f\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)^{p,q}\bigr)
be a smooth
section with the property that

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h_{\End(E)}(f,f)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu<+\infty.

Then there is a smooth section
u\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)^{p,q}\bigr)
that
solves the equation
\bar{\partial}u=fd\bar{t}
and also satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h_{\End(E)}(u,u)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq 4\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h_{\End(E)}(f,f)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu.

7.3
Constructing a holomorphic lifting of the Higgs field

161
.

After these preliminaries, we now turn to the actual proof of the nilpotent orbit
theorem. Fix a half-open interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
of length
1
, and let
S\in\End(V)
be the unique semisimple operator such that
T_{s}=e^{2\pi iS}
and such
that the eigenvalues of
S
are real and contained in
I
. If we denote by

P_{\lambda}\in\End(V)
the projection to the eigenspace
E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
, we can
write
S
compactly as

S=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\alpha P_{e^{2\pi i\alpha}}.

The differential of the holomorphic mapping
z\mapsto\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z)
at the point
z\in\mathbb{H}
is equal to

e^{-z(S+N)}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}e^{z(S+N)}-(S+N)\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}.

The differential is holomorphic, modulo the indicated subspace, but the operator on
the left-hand side is
not
holomorphic as a section of the bundle
\End(V)
.
The idea of the proof is to lift this operator to a holomorphic section of the bundle

\End(V)
with the help of Hörmander’s
L^{2}
-estimates, and then to use the
properties of this lifting to show that
\Psi_{S}
extends.

162
.

Let us begin by looking at the Higgs field itself. By construction, the operator

\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\colon\mathbb{H}\to\End(V)
is the pullback of
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
, and as such, it satisfies

\theta_{\partial/\partial z}(z+2\pi i)=T\,\theta_{\partial/\partial z}(z)\,T^{-1}.

Recall that we have an induced variation of Hodge structure of
weight
0
on the bundle
\End(E)
. The Higgs field
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}

is a smooth section of the Hodge bundle
F^{-1}\End(E)
on
\Delta^{\ast}
. Now the
Hodge bundle is a holomorphic vector bundle, whose holomorphic structure is defined
by the operator
d^{\prime\prime}_{\End(E)}=[d^{\prime\prime},-]
, but the Higgs field is not a
holomorphic section.
Instead, because of the identity
\bar{\partial}\theta+\theta\bar{\partial}=0
, only its projection
to the quotient bundle

\End(E)^{-1,1}\cong F^{-1}\End(E)/F^{0}\End(E)

is holomorphic, for the holomorphic structure defined by
\bar{\partial}_{\End(E)}=[\bar{\partial},-]
.

163
.

The
L^{2}
-estimates allow us to lift
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
to a
holomorphic section of the bundle
F^{-1}\End(E)
in a controlled manner. We will
make a specific choice of the two parameters
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
