b\in\mathbb{Z}
a little later.

Proposition 11
.

Set
r=\rk E
. For every
a>-2
and every
b\geq 2r\binom{r+1}{3}+1
, there
is a holomorphic section
\vartheta
of the Hodge bundle
F^{-1}\End(E)
such that

\vartheta\equiv t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\mod F^{0}\End(E),

and which also satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h_{\End(E)}(\vartheta,\vartheta)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq C,

with a constant
C>0
whose exact value only depends on
r
,
a
, and
b
.

Proof.

Since the Higgs field is trivial when
r=1
, we may assume without loss of
generality that
r\geq 2
. Using the identities in
2
,
we compute that

d^{\prime\prime}_{\End(E)}\bigl(t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)=\bar{\partial}_{\End(E)}\bigl(t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)+\theta^{\ast}_{\End(E)}\bigl(t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)=t[\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}},\theta_{\partial/\partial t}]d\bar{t}.

Temporarily define
f_{0}=t[\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}},\theta_{\partial/\partial t}]
; this is a smooth section of the Hodge bundle

\End(E)^{0,0}
. The universal bound on the Higgs field in

1
gives

h(f_{0},f_{0})\leq 2\lvert t\rvert^{2}\cdot h\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t},\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)^{2}\leq\frac{2C_{0}^{4}}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{4}}.

Provided that
a>-2
and
b\geq 2
, the integral

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(f_{0},f_{0})\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu\leq 2C_{0}^{4}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\lvert t\rvert^{a}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b-2}\,d\mu

is finite. Once
b\geq 2r\binom{r+1}{3}+1
, the version of the
L^{2}
-estimates
in
6
gives us a solution
u_{0}\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)^{0,0}\bigr)
to the equation
\bar{\partial}_{\End(E)}u_{0}+f_{0}d\bar{t}=0
that satisfies the
L^{2}
-estimate

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(u_{0},u_{0})\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq 8C_{0}^{4}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b-2}\,d\mu

Continuing in this way, we produce smooth sections
u_{k},f_{k}\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)^{k,-k}\bigr)
, indexed by
k\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

f_{k+1}=[\theta^{\ast}_{\partial/\partial\bar{t}},u_{k}]\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{\partial}_{\End(E)}u_{k+1}+f_{k+1}d\bar{t}=0,

subject to the
L^{2}
-estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(u_{k+1},u_{k+1})\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq 4\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(f_{k+1},f_{k+1})\,\lvert t\rvert^{a+2}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b+2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq 8C_{0}^{2}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(u_{k},u_{k})\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu

Let
p\in\mathbb{N}
be the biggest integer such that
\End(E)^{p,-p}\neq 0
. By
construction,

\vartheta=t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}+\sum_{k=0}^{p}u_{k}\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},F^{-1}\End(E)\bigr)

satisfies
d_{\End(E)}^{\prime\prime}(\vartheta)=0
, hence is a holomorphic section
of the Hodge bundle
F^{-1}\End(E)
, with the same image in
\End(E)^{-1,1}\cong F^{-1}\End(E)/F^{0}\End(E)
as the Higgs field
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
.
We also get another valuable piece of information about
\vartheta
from the

L^{2}
-estimates for the individual solutions
u_{k}
. On the one hand,

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(u_{k},u_{k})\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq(8C_{0}^{2})^{k+1}\cdot C_{0}^{2}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b-2}\,d\mu

by combining the inequalities above. On the other hand,

h\bigl(t\theta_{\partial/\partial t},t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)\leq\frac{C_{0}^{2}}{(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}},

and so we have a similar inequality

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h\bigl(t\theta_{\partial/\partial t},t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}\bigr)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu\leq C_{0}^{2}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b-2}\,d\mu.

If we put everything together and remember that the Hodge decomposition on
\End(E)
is
orthogonal with respect to the Hodge metric, the result is that

\displaystyle\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}h(\vartheta,\vartheta)\,\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}(8C_{0}^{2})^{k}\cdot C_{0}^{2}\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b-2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{p+1}(8C_{0}^{2})^{k}\cdot\frac{2\pi(b-2)!}{(a+2)^{b-1}},

after evaluating the integral.
∎

164
.

Recall that the pullback of the flat bundle
(E,d)
along
\exp\colon\mathbb{H}\to\Delta^{\ast}

has a canonical trivialization by the space
V
of multi-valued flat sections. Let us
use the same letter

\vartheta\colon\mathbb{H}\to\End(V)

also for the pullback of
\vartheta\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},F^{-1}\End(E)\bigr)
to
the half-plane
\mathbb{H}
; this is now a holomorphic mapping with the property that

\vartheta(z)\in F^{-1}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}\quad\text{and}\quad\vartheta(z)-\theta_{\partial/\partial z}\in F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)}

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
. As a pullback, it transforms according to the rule

\vartheta(z+2\pi i)=T\vartheta(z)T^{-1}.

Recall that
T=e^{2\pi i(S+N)}
, where
S\in\End(V)
is semisimple with real
eigenvalues contained in a half-open interval of length
1
. The expression
e^{-z(S+N)}\vartheta(z)e^{z(S+N)}
is again invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
,
and therefore

e^{-z(S+N)}\,\vartheta(z)\,e^{z(S+N)}=B(e^{z})

for a well-defined holomorphic mapping
B\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\End(V)
.

165
.

We can use our integral estimate for
\vartheta
to prove that
B
extends
holomorphically across the origin. To do that, we first need to know how much
conjugation by the operator
e^{-z(S+N)}
affects the norm of an endomorphism. Define

\delta(T)=\min\bigl\{\thinspace\delta>0\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$T$ has two eigenvalues of the form
$\lambda$ and $\lambda\cdot e^{2\pi i\delta}$}\thinspace\bigr\};

concretely, this is the minimal distance between consecutive eigenvalues of
T
(on
the unit circle), divided by
2\pi
. In the extreme case where all eigenvalues of

T
are equal to each other, we have
\delta(T)=1
. Also define

m(N)=\min\bigl\{\thinspace m\in\mathbb{N}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace N^{m}\neq 0\thinspace\bigr\}

as the index of nilpotency of the operator
N=\log T_{u}
.

Lemma 28
.

There is a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\bigl\lVert e^{-z(S+N)}

\displaystyle Ae^{z(S+N)}\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{(1-\delta(T))\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m(N)+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}\Bigr)\lVert A\rVert_{\Phi(z)}

for every
A\in\End(V)
, uniformly on the horizontal strip

\bigl\{\thinspace z\in\mathbb{H}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert\leq\pi\thinspace\bigr\}.

Here
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
, and the exact value of the constant

C
only depends on
r=\rk E
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
.

Proof.

In order to simplify the notation, we denote by
\lVert v\rVert=\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}

the Hodge norm at the point
-1\in\mathbb{H}
. Let us first consider the dominant
term
e^{-zS}
. Let
P_{\lambda}\colon V\to E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
denote the
projection to the
\lambda
-eigenspace of
T_{s}
. Then

S=\sum_{\alpha\in I}\alpha P_{e^{2\pi i\alpha}}.

Let
A\in\End(V)
be an arbitrary endomorphism. We have

e^{-zS}Ae^{zS}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}e^{(\alpha-\beta)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}e^{-i(\alpha-\beta)\operatorname{Im}z}P_{e^{2\pi i\alpha}}AP_{e^{2\pi i\beta}}

and so the triangle inequality and submultiplicativity of the
L^{2}
-norm give

\bigl\lVert e^{-zS}Ae^{zS}\bigr\rVert\leq e^{(1-\delta)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\biggl(\sum_{\lambda}\lVert P_{\lambda}\rVert\biggr)^{2}\lVert A\rVert,

(165.1)

where
\delta=\delta(T)
, due to the fact that
\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}-\alpha_{\mathrm{min}}\leq 1-\delta
. The term
e^{-zN}
is easier to analyze because
N
is nilpotent. Set
m=m(N)
. Then

e^{-zN}Ae^{zN}=\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\frac{(-1)^{i}z^{i+j}}{i!\,j!}N^{i}AN^{j}

and therefore

\bigl\lVert e^{-zN}Ae^{zN}\bigr\rVert\leq\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\frac{\lvert z\rvert^{i+i}}{i!\,j!}\lVert N\rVert^{i+j}\lVert A\rVert=\sum_{k=0}^{m}\frac{\lvert 2z\rvert^{k}}{k!}\lVert N\rVert^{k}\lVert A\rVert.

Now we consider
A\in\End(V)
as a multi-valued flat section of the induced
variation of Hodge structure on
\End(E)
. Remembering the improved bound for the
Higgs field in
2
, we get from

7
the inequality

\lVert A\rVert\leq e^{2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}\pi}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}\Bigr)\lVert A\rVert_{\Phi(z)}

Putting everything together, we arrive at

\bigl\lVert e^{-z(S+N)}Ae^{z(S+N)}\bigr\rVert\leq Ce^{(1-\delta)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}\Bigr)\lVert A\rVert_{\Phi(z)}

with a constant
C>0
that only depends on the two integers
r=\rk E

and
m=m(N)
, and on the Hodge norms
\lVert P_{\lambda}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}
and

\lVert N\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}
. The latter quantities are in turn bounded, in

4
, by a constant whose value only depends on

r
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
. This gives the desired
result.
∎

166
.

We are now ready to show that
B\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\End(V)
extends holomorphically
across the origin. We choose

a=-2+2\delta(T)>-2\quad\text{and}\quad b=2r\binom{r+1}{3}+1,

and then construct
\vartheta
and
B
according to the procedure described above.
Since
b=8rC_{0}^{2}+1\geq m(N)+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}
,
28
gives

\bigl\lVert B(e^{z})\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}\leq C^{2}e^{-a\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\max\Bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{b},\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-b}\Bigr)\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2};

the restriction on
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert
becomes irrelevant here, because
B(e^{z})
is
of course invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
. Now
\lvert t\rvert=e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
and
-\log\lvert t\rvert=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
, and so we can rewrite
this inequality in the form

\min\Bigl(1,(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2b}\Bigr)\cdot\lVert B(t)\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}\leq C^{2}h(\vartheta,\vartheta)\lvert t\rvert^{a}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{b}.

The
L^{2}
-estimate for
\vartheta
in
11
now gives us

\int_{\Delta^{\ast}}\min\Bigl(1,(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2b}\Bigr)\cdot\lVert B(t)\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}\,d\mu\leq C^{\prime},

(166.1)

with a constant
C^{\prime}>0
whose value has the same dependence on parameters as in

28
. Since
B\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\End(V)
is holomorphic, this is enough to conclude that
B

extends holomorphically across the origin.

167
.

As an aside, we note that the bound on the integral also gives us some control over
the pointwise norm of
B(t)
. To simplify the calculations, we use the inequality

\min\Bigl(1,(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2b}\Bigr)\geq(1-\lvert t\rvert)^{2b},

which follows from the fact that
\lvert\log x\rvert\geq 1-x
for
0<x\leq 1
. The following result is a simple modification of the formula
for the Bergman kernel on the unit disk.

Lemma 29
.

For any holomorphic function
f\colon\Delta\to\mathbb{C}
, one has

\lvert f(t)\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{2^{b}(b+1)}{(1-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{b+2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta}(1-\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\,d\mu.

Proof.

Consider the power series expansion

f=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}a_{n}t^{n}.

An easy computation shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta}(1-\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{2}\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\Delta}(1-\lvert t\rvert)^{b}\lvert t\rvert^{2n}\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{2}\int_{0}^{1}2(1-r)^{b}r^{2n+1}\mathit{dr}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{2}\frac{2(2n+1)!b!}{(2n+2+b)!}.

We can now apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to get

\lvert f(t)\rvert^{2}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert t\rvert^{2n}\frac{(2n+2+b)!}{2(2n+1)!b!}\cdot\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert a_{n}\rvert^{2}\frac{2(2n+1)!b!}{(2n+2+b)!}.

To estimate the first series, we set
x=\lvert t\rvert^{2}
and compute as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\lvert t\rvert^{2n}\frac{(2n+2+b)!}{2(2n+1)!b!}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2b!}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+2)(2n+3)\dotsm(2n+2+b)x^{n}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2b!}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(2n+2)(2n+4)\dotsm(2n+2+2b)x^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{b}}{b!}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(n+1)\dotsm(n+1+b)x^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{b}}{b!}\left(\frac{d}{dx}\right)^{b+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x^{n}=\frac{2^{b}(b+1)}{(1-x)^{b+2}}=\frac{2^{b}(b+1)}{(1-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{b+2}}.

The desired result follows.
∎

168
.

If we apply this result to the integral bound for
B(t)
in
(
166.1
), for example by choosing an orthonormal basis of

\End(V)
relative to the inner product coming from the Hodge structure
\Phi(-1)
,
and then writing
B(t)
as a matrix, we get

\lVert B(t)\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\leq\frac{C}{(1-\lvert t\rvert^{2})^{2b+2}},

(168.1)

where
b=2r\binom{r+1}{3}+1
, and where
C>0
is a constant that again depends
only on
r=\rk E
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
.
The interesting thing is that
B(t)
still behaves rather
nicely near the outer boundary of the unit disk, even though all we have is a bound
on its
L^{2}
-norm.

7.4
Holomorphic extension of the untwisted period mapping

169
.

We return to our analysis of the untwisted period mapping

\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z).

Recall that the differential of the mapping
z\mapsto\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is given by

e^{-z(S+N)}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}e^{z(S+N)}-(S+N)\equiv B(e^{z})-(S+N)\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}

Here we are using the fact that
B(e^{z})-e^{-z(S+N)}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}e^{z(S+N)}
lies in
F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}
. The point is that
the operator on the right-hand side is holomorphic.

170
.

Now suppose that we have a holomorphic mapping

g\colon\mathbb{H}\to\GL(V)

that solves the ordinary differential equation

g^{\prime}(z)=\Bigl(B(e^{z})-(S+N)\Bigr)g(z),

(170.1)

subject to the initial condition
g(-1)=\id
. A short calculation with derivatives
shows that the mapping
g(z)^{-1}\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is then constant, which means that

\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=g(z)\cdot\Psi_{S}(e^{-1})=g(z)\cdot e^{(S+N)}\Phi(-1).

The key to the remainder of the proof is that the differential equation in
(
170.1
) has a
regular singular point
at
t=0
.

171
.

Let us first prove that (
170.1
) has a solution with the desired
properties. For every
v\in V
, the differential equation

f^{\prime}(z)=\Bigl(B(e^{z})-(S+N)\Bigr)f(z)

has a unique solution
f\colon\mathbb{H}\to V
that is holomorphic and satisfies the
initial condition
f(-1)=v
; this is Cauchy’s theorem. Let
v_{1},\dotsc,v_{r}\in V

be a basis, and let
f_{1},\dotsc,f_{r}\colon\mathbb{H}\to V
be the corresponding
solutions. By uniqueness, the values
f_{1}(z),\dotsc,f_{r}(z)\in V
must be linearly
independent for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
. We can therefore define a holomorphic mapping

g\colon\mathbb{H}\to\GL(V)

by requiring that
g(z)v_{i}=f_{i}(z)
for
i=1,\dotsc,r
. It follows that
g(-1)=\id
, and so
g
is the desired solution to (
170.1
).

172
.

Clearly,
g(z+2\pi i)g(-1+2\pi i)^{-1}
is also a solution, and so by uniqueness,

g(z+2\pi i)=g(z)g(-1+2\pi i).

Let
A\in\End(V)
be the unique operator such that
g(-1+2\pi i)=e^{2\pi iA}
and such that all eigenvalues of
A
have their real part contained in the
interval
[0,1)
. Then
g(z)e^{-zA}
is invariant under
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
, and
so

g(z)=M(e^{z})e^{zA}

for a unique holomorphic mapping
M\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\GL(V)
. Since (
170.1
)
has a regular singular point at
t=0
, the basic theory of Fuchsian differential
equations implies that
M(t)
is meromorphic at
t=0
.

173
.

Let us briefly recall how this is proved. We use the shorthand
\lVert v\rVert=\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}
for the Hodge norm in the Hodge structure
\Phi(-1)
. Since

B\colon\Delta\to\End(V)
is holomorphic, there is a constant
C>0

such that

\bigl\lVert B(e^{z})-(S+N)\bigr\rVert\leq C\quad\text{for $\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1$.}

Let
z\in\mathbb{H}
be any point with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1
. Consider the auxiliary function

\varphi\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\quad\varphi(x)=\bigl\lVert g\bigl(-1+x(z+1)\bigr)\bigr\rVert^{2}.

The derivative of
\varphi
is given by the formula

\varphi^{\prime}(x)=2\operatorname{Re}\Bigl\langle(z+1)g^{\prime}\bigl(-1+x(z+1)\bigr),\,g\bigl(-1+x(z+1)\bigr)\Bigr\rangle,

and so the differential equation in (
170.1
), together with the
Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the bound on
B(e^{z})-(S+N)
, leads to
the simple inequality

\lvert\varphi^{\prime}(x)\rvert\leq 2C\lvert z+1\rvert\cdot\varphi(x).

This is easily integrated, with the result that

\lVert g(z)\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert g(-1)\rVert^{2}e^{2C\lvert z+1\rvert}\leq re^{2C\lvert z+1\rvert},

remembering that
g(-1)=\id
and
r=\dim V
. Since the
L^{2}
-norm is
submultiplicative, we then get

\lVert M(e^{z})\rVert\leq\lVert g(z)\rVert\cdot\lVert e^{-zA}\rVert\leq\sqrt{r}e^{C\lvert z+1\rvert}\cdot e^{\lvert z\rvert\lVert A\rVert}\leq C^{\prime}e^{d\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for some
C^{\prime}>0
and some
d\in\mathbb{N}
; note that we may assume that
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert\leq\pi
. But this says exactly that
M(t)
has a pole of order at most
d
at
t=0
.

174
.

We can now finish the proof of the first half of the nilpotent orbit theorem, still
under the assumption that the eigenvalues of
S
lie in a fixed half-open interval

I
of length
1
. By construction, we have

\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=g(z)\cdot e^{S+N}\Phi(-1)=M(e^{z})\cdot e^{zA}\cdot e^{S+N}\Phi(-1)

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
. The left-hand side is invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
, and so the operator
e^{2\pi iA}\in\GL(V)
has to leave the point
e^{S+N}\Phi(-1)\in\check{D}
fixed. It follows that

A
itself must leave the point fixed, and so we can erase the factor
e^{zA}
from
the identity above. The result is that

\Psi_{S}(t)=M(t)\cdot e^{S+N}\Phi(-1)

for
t\in\Delta^{\ast}
. Because
M(t)
is meromorphic at
t=0
, and
\check{D}
is projective,
this is enough to conclude that
\Psi_{S}
extends holomorphically across the origin.

175
.

Removing the restriction on the eigenvalues of
S
is now an easy matter. Suppose
that
S\in\End(V)
is
any
operator such that
T_{s}=e^{2\pi iS}
; of
course,
S
is then automatically semisimple with real eigenvalues. Let
S(I)\in\End(V)
be the choice we were using above, with eigenvalues contained in the given
interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Then
S(I)-S
has integer eigenvalues, and so

\Psi_{S}(t)=e^{z(S(I)-S)}\Psi_{S(I)}(t)=t^{S(I)-S}\Psi_{S(I)}(t)

is obtained from the holomorphic maping
\Psi_{S(I)}(t)
by acting by a semisimple
operator whose eigenvalues are powers of
t
. It follows that
\Psi_{S}(t)
is also
meromorphic, and therefore extends holomorphically to
\Delta
for the same reason as
before.

176
.

In particular, we now have a well-defined limit
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
. Let us write

F^{\bullet}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)}
for the induced filtration on
\End(V)
. It remains
to show that

S+N\in F^{-1}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)}.

Recall that the derivative of the holomorphic mapping
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is equal to

B(e^{z})-(S+N)\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})},

and that the operator
B(e^{z})
is holomorphic and belongs to
F^{-1}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}
. Since
\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta\to\check{D}
is holomorphic, the
chain rule shows that the derivative of
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
goes to zero like
\lvert t\rvert=e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
. Passing to the limit, we get

B(0)-(S+N)\in F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)},

and therefore
S+N\in F^{-1}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)}
, as claimed. This completes the
proof of
5
, which contained the convergence
assertions in the nilpotent orbit theorem.

177
.

The nilpotent orbit theorem has the following more geometric interpretation, in terms
of certain bundles on the unit disk. Recall that the operator
S\in\End(V)
in

T=e^{2\pi i(S+N)}
depended on a choice of half-open interval
I
of length
1
.
Let
\mathscr{E}_{S}
denote the canonical extension
[
Deligne-eq
, Prop. 5.2]
of the
holomorphic vector bundle
\mathscr{E}
, such that the connection extends to a logarithmic
connection
\nabla\colon\mathscr{E}_{S}\to\Omega_{\Delta}^{1}(\log 0)\otimes\mathscr{E}_{S}
whose
residue
\Res_{0}\nabla
has eigenvalues in the same interval
I
. The bundle
\mathscr{E}_{S}
has a
unique trivialization

V\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathscr{O}_{\Delta}\cong\mathscr{E}_{S}

such that the logarithmic connection becomes
\nabla(v\otimes 1)=(S+N)v\otimes\mathit{dt}
; therefore
S+N
is exactly the Jordan decomposition of the residue
\Res_{0}\nabla
. Under this trivialization, the Hodge bundle
F^{p}\mathscr{E}
goes to the subbundle

\bigl\{\thinspace\bigl(t,\Psi_{S}^{p}(t)\bigr)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace t\in\Delta^{\ast}\thinspace\bigr\}\subseteq\Delta^{\ast}\times V

of the trivial bundle;
5
is
therefore saying exactly that the Hodge bundles extend to holomorphic subbundles
F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{S}
of the canonical extension.

178
.

Now if we introduce the quotient bundles

\mathscr{E}_{S}^{p,q}=F^{p}\mathscr{E}_{S}/F^{p+1}\mathscr{E}_{S},

then the operator
B(t)
that we constructed above gives a holomorphic extension of
the Higgs field to a morphism of holomorphic vector bundles

\mathscr{E}_{S}^{p,q}\to\Omega_{\Delta}^{1}(\log 0)\otimes\mathscr{E}_{S}^{p-1,q+1}

whose residue at
t=0
is exactly the operator
S+N\in\End(V)
. This gives us a
sort of canonical extension of the Higgs bundle
\bigoplus\mathscr{E}^{p,q}
to a
logarithmic Higgs bundle
\bigoplus\mathscr{E}_{S}^{p,q}
, on which the residue
\Res_{0}\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
of the Higgs field has eigenvalues in the interval
I
.
The fiber at
t=0
is isomorphic to the graded vector space

\bigoplus_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\Psi_{S}^{p}(0)/\Psi_{S}^{p+1}(0),

and we have
\Res_{0}\theta_{\partial/\partial t}=S+N
.

7.5
The limiting Hodge filtration

179
.

From the untwisted period mapping, we get a sort of “limit” filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
, but it depends on the choice of
S\in\End(V)
, and therefore has
little intrinsic meaning. In this section, we introduce an additional filtration

F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in\check{D}
, called the “limiting Hodge filtration”, that only depends on the
period mapping itself. The limiting Hodge filtration is important in two ways: it
allows one to approximate the original period mapping by a nilpotent orbit; and it
shows up in the limiting mixed Hodge structure.

180
.

For technical reasons, we again have to restrict to the case where the eigenvalues of

S\in\End(V)
are contained in a half-open interval of length
1
.

Proposition 12
.

As long as
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert
remains bounded, the limit

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{-zN}\Phi(z)=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}

exists and is called the
limiting Hodge filtration
. It satisfies

N(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet-1}\quad\text{and}\quad T_{s}(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}.

181
.

From the formula for the untwisted period mapping, it is clear that

e^{-zN}\Phi(z)=e^{zS}\Psi_{S}(e^{z}).

Since
\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta\to\check{D}
is holomorphic, there is a constant
C>0

such that

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(\Psi_{S}(t),\Psi_{S}(0)\bigr)\leq C\lvert t\rvert

(181.1)

for small values of
\lvert t\rvert
. According to (
79.1
), translation
by the operator
e^{zS}\in\GL(V)
distorts distances in the compact dual
\check{D}
by
at most the operator norm of
\Ad e^{zS}
. We can estimate this using the following
variant of
28
.

Lemma 30
.

Let
S\in\End(V)
be a semisimple with real eigenvalues. Then

\lVert\Ad e^{zS}\rVert\leq Ce^{(\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}-\alpha_{\mathrm{min}})\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

where
\alpha_{\mathrm{min}}
and
\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}
are the smallest and largest eigenvalue of
S
.

Proof.

Let
A\in\End(V)
be an arbitrary endomorphism. We have

e^{zS}Ae^{-zS}=\sum_{\alpha,\beta}e^{(\beta-\alpha)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}e^{i\operatorname{Im}z(\alpha-\beta)}P_{\alpha}AP_{\beta},

where
P_{\alpha}\colon V\to E_{\alpha}(S)
are the projections to the eigenspaces
of
S
. Since the
L^{2}
-norm is submultiplicative, we get

\lVert e^{zS}Ae^{-zS}\rVert\leq e^{(\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}-\alpha_{\mathrm{min}})\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\biggl(\sum_{\alpha}\lVert P_{\alpha}\rVert\biggr)^{2}\lVert A\rVert,

and therefore the desired upper bound on the operator norm of
\Ad e^{zS}
.
∎

182
.

Applying this lemma to (
181.1
), we find that

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(e^{-zN}\Phi(z),e^{zS}\Psi_{S}(0)\bigr)\leq C^{\prime}e^{-(1-\rho)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

(182.1)

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
, where
\rho=\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}-\alpha_{\mathrm{min}}<1
is the
difference between the largest and smallest eigenvalue of
S
. It remains to
understand the effect of the exponential factor
e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert S}
on the
filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)
.

183
.

The following lemma does this in slightly greater generality; we will use it again
(in
209
) when we prove the convergence of the rescaled
period mapping.

Lemma 31
.

Let
S\in\End(V)
be a semisimple operator with real eigenvalues. For any
filtration
F\in\check{D}
, the limit

\hat{F}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xS}F

exists in
\check{D}
, and the filtration
\hat{F}
is compatible with the eigenspace
decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{\alpha}E_{\alpha}(S)
. Moreover, there is a constant
C\geq 0
such that

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(\hat{F},e^{xS}F\bigr)\leq Ce^{-\delta x},

where
\delta>0
is the smallest distance between consecutive eigenvalues of
S
.

Proof.

Since a filtration is just a collection of subspaces, it suffices to prove that
for any subspace
W\subseteq V
of dimension
d
, the limit

\hat{W}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xS}W

exists (in the Grassmannian of
d
-dimensional subspaces of
V
), and satisfies

\hat{W}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}E_{\alpha}(S)\cap\hat{W}.

To make it clear what is going on, let us first do the case where
W=\mathbb{C}v
is
one-dimensional. Write
v=\sum_{\alpha}v_{\alpha}
, where
Sv_{\alpha}=\alpha v_{\alpha}
. Then

e^{xS}v=\sum_{\alpha}e^{\alpha x}v_{\alpha}.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
be the largest number such that
v_{\beta}\neq 0
. From

e^{xS}(\mathbb{C}v)=\mathbb{C}\biggl(v_{\beta}+\sum_{\alpha<\beta}e^{-(\beta-\alpha)x}v_{\alpha}\biggr),

we see that
\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xS}(\mathbb{C}v)
exists and equals
\mathbb{C}v_{\beta}
.
So the effect of the limit is to extract the component of
v
for
the largest possible eigenvalue. Moreover, the rate of convergence is evidently

e^{-(\beta-\beta^{\prime})x}
, where
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
is the next largest eigenvalue of
S
.

To deal with the general case, let
\alpha_{1}<\alpha_{2}<\dotsb<\alpha_{n}
be the
eigenvalues of
S
in increasing order, and consider the filtration
G_{\bullet}

by increasing eigenvalues, with terms

G_{j}=E_{\alpha_{1}}(S)\oplus\dotsb\oplus E_{\alpha_{j}}(S)\subseteq V.

Set
W_{1}=G_{1}\cap W
; choose a subspace
W_{2}\subseteq W
such that

W_{1}\oplus W_{2}=G_{2}\cap W
; and so on. Continuing in this way, we obtain a
collection of subspaces
W_{1},\dotsc,W_{n}\subseteq W
, possibly zero-dimensional,
with the property that

G_{j}\cap W=W_{1}\oplus\dotsb\oplus W_{j}.

By construction, any nonzero vector
v\in W_{j}
must have a nontrivial component

v_{\alpha_{j}}\in E_{\alpha_{j}}(S)
, and as we have seen above,
e^{xS}(\mathbb{C}v)

therefore converges to
\mathbb{C}v_{\alpha_{j}}
at a rate of
e^{-(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{j-1})x}
. It follows that the subspace
e^{xS}W_{j}
converges, at the
same rate, to a subspace
\hat{W}_{j}\subseteq E_{\alpha_{j}}(S)
, which consists of
the
E_{\alpha_{j}}(S)
-components of all the vectors in
W_{j}
. Putting everything
together, we find that

\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xS}W=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}\hat{W}_{j}.

The rate of convergence is evidently
e^{-\delta x}
, where
\delta>0
is the
smallest distance between consecutive eigenvalues of
S
.
∎

184
.

Here is an equivalent way for describing the limit filtration
\hat{F}
in terms of
the filtration
G_{\bullet}
by increasing eigenvalues of
S
. Projecting
W\subseteq V
to the subquotient
G_{j}/G_{j-1}
yields the subspace

(G_{j}\cap W+G_{j-1})/G_{j-1}\subseteq G_{j}/G_{j-1}.

Since
G_{j}\cap W+G_{j-1}=W_{j}+G_{j-1}
, the subspace
\hat{W}_{j}\subseteq E_{\alpha}(S)
that we used during the proof is exactly the preimage of the above
subspace under the isomorphism
E_{\alpha_{j}}(S)\cong G_{j}/G_{j-1}
. So the effect
of the limit

\hat{F}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xS}F

is to make the filtration
F
compatible with the eigenspace decomposition of
S
,
by projecting to the subquotients of the filtration by increasing eigenvalues.

185
.

The lemma shows that the limit

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}

exists, and that the resulting filtration satisfies
S(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}
. On account of (
182.1
), it follows that the
limit

\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{-zN}\Phi(z)=\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{i\operatorname{Im}z\,S}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert S}\Phi(z)=F_{\mathrm{lim}}

also exists, provided that
\lvert\operatorname{Im}z\rvert
remains bounded in the process. In fact, the
limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
is obtained from
\Psi_{S}(0)
by projecting to the
subquotients of the filtration by
decreasing
eigenvalues of
S
. This is due
to the minus sign in the exponential factor
e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert S}
.

186
.

We already know that
T_{s}(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}
.
To complete the proof of
12
, we have to analyze how the
nilpotent operator
N
acts on the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. Recall that we
have

(S+N)\Psi_{S}^{p}(0)\subseteq\Psi_{S}^{p-1}(0)\quad\text{for all $p\in\mathbb{Z}$.}

From the definition of
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, we now get

NF_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}=(S+N)F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}(S+N)\Psi_{S}^{p}(0)\subseteq\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}^{p-1}(0)=F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p-1},

as desired.

187
.

It turns out that there is another way to relate the limiting Hodge
filtration to untwisted period mappings. This is useful for the proof of the
nilpotent orbit theorem in higher dimensions, which we plan to discuss in
a sequel to this paper. The idea, which already occurs in
[
Schmid
, §8]
, is to
go to a finite covering of
\Delta^{\ast}
of sufficiently high degree.

188
.

For any
m\geq 1
, we can pull back
E
along the finite covering space

\Delta^{\ast}\to\Delta^{\ast},\quad t\mapsto t^{m}.

The result is another variation of Hodge structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
. It has
the same space of multi-valued flat sections, but the monodromy operator changes to

T^{m}
, and the period mapping changes to
\Phi(mz)
. This is convenient, because it
means that we do not have to introduce any new notation. Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be any
half-open interval of length
1
. Let
S_{m}\in\End(V)
be the unique semisimple
operator with

T_{s}^{m}=e^{2\pi imS_{m}},

such that
mS_{m}
has eigenvalues in
I
; the eigenvalues of
S_{m}
itself are
contained in
\frac{1}{m}I
. Because
T_{s}=e^{2\pi iS}
, the
eigenvalues of the operator
e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}
are
m
-th roots of unity.

Lemma 32
.

On each eigenspace of
T_{s}
, the operator
e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}
acts as
multiplication by an
m
-th root of unity; these roots of unity are distinct if

m\delta(T)\geq 1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
I=[\alpha,\alpha+1)
; the remaining case is similar. List the
eigenvalues of
S
in increasing order as

\alpha\leq\alpha_{1}<\dotsb<\alpha_{n}<\alpha+1;

the distance between adjacent eigenvalues is at least
\delta(T)
. Write
m\alpha_{j}=k_{j}+\beta_{j}
, where
k_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\beta_{j}\in I
; then the
eigenvalues of
mS_{m}
are exactly
\beta_{1},\dotsc,\beta_{n}
. Now

k_{j}=\lfloor m\alpha_{j}-a\rfloor,

and since
m\alpha_{j+1}-m\alpha_{j}\geq m\delta(T)\geq 1
, it follows that

k_{j+1}>k_{j}
; similarly, the fact that
m\alpha_{r}-m\alpha_{1}<m
implies
that
k_{n}-k_{1}<m
. The eigenvalues of
e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}
are therefore the
complex numbers
e^{2\pi ik_{j}/m}
, which are distinct
m
-th roots of unity.
∎

189
.

Now we consider a variant of the untwisted period mapping. The expression

e^{-mz(S_{m}+N)}\Phi(mz)

is invariant under the substitution
z\mapsto z+2\pi i
, and so it again
descends to a holomorphic mapping

\Psi_{m}\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\check{D},\quad\Psi_{m}(e^{z})=e^{-mz(S_{m}+N)}\Phi(mz).

This in of course just the untwisted period mapping
\Psi_{mS_{m}}
for the pullback of

E
along
t\mapsto t^{m}
. A brief calculation shows that

\Psi_{m}(e^{2\pi i/m}\cdot t)=e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}\cdot\Psi_{m}(t).

This identity has the following nice consequence.

Proposition 13
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
m\delta(T)\geq 1
, and choose
S_{m}\in\End(V)
as above. The holomorphic mapping

\Psi_{m}\colon\Delta^{\ast}\to\check{D},\quad\Psi_{m}(e^{z})=e^{-mz(S_{m}+N)}\Phi(mz),

extends holomorphically across the origin, and
\Psi_{m}(0)=F_{\mathrm{lim}}
.

Proof.

We already know from the proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem that
\Psi_{m}
extends
holomorphically across the origin. It remains to show that the filtration
\Psi_{m}(0)\in\check{D}
agrees with the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. From the identity

\Psi_{m}(e^{2\pi i/m}\cdot t)=e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}\cdot\Psi_{m}(0),

we conclude that the filtration
\Psi_{m}(0)\in\check{D}
is preserved by the operator

e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}
. By
32
, the eigenvalues of this
operator are
m
-th roots of unity, with a different root of unity
\zeta_{\lambda}

on each eigenspace
E_{\lambda}(T_{s})
. Consequently,

P_{\lambda}=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\bigl(\zeta_{\lambda}^{-1}e^{2\pi i(S-S_{m})}\bigr)^{k},

and one deduces easily that
T_{s}\cdot\Psi_{m}(0)=\Psi_{m}(0)
. For
\lvert t\rvert

sufficiently small, we have

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(\Psi_{m}(t),\Psi_{m}(0)\bigr)\leq C\lvert t\rvert,

which translates into

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{-z(S_{m}+N)}\Phi(z),\Psi_{m}(0)\Bigr)\leq Ce^{-\frac{1}{m}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

After applying (
79.1
) and (
165.1
) to control
the distortion caused by translating by the operator
e^{zS_{m}}\in\GL(V)
, we get

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{-zN}\Phi(z),e^{zS_{m}}\Psi_{m}(0)\Bigr)\leq C\biggl(\sum_{\lambda}\lVert P_{\lambda}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\biggr)^{2}e^{-\frac{1-\rho}{m}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

Since
e^{zS_{m}}\Psi_{m}(0)=\Psi_{m}(0)
, we conclude that

\Psi_{m}(0)=\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{-zN}\Phi(z)=F_{\mathrm{lim}}

is indeed equal to the limiting Hodge filtration.
∎

190
.

Here is a small example that shows the difference between the filration
\Psi_{S}(0)
and
the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
.

Example 6
.

Let
\alpha>0
be a real number. Using the notation from

3
, consider the polarized variation of Hodge structure
\Phi\colon\mathbb{H}\to D
with

V_{\Phi(z)}^{1,0}=\mathbb{C}\bigl(1,e^{\alpha z}\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad V_{\Phi(z)}^{0,1}=\mathbb{C}\bigl(e^{\alpha\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}},1\bigr).

Here
N=0
and so
F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{1}=\mathbb{C}(1,0)
, which is the Hodge filtration of a
polarized Hodge structure. On the other hand, if we use

S=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\alpha\end{pmatrix},

then
\Psi_{S}^{1}(0)=\mathbb{C}(1,1)
, and this is
not
the Hodge filtration of a
polarized Hodge structure.

7.6
Effective estimates for the rate of convergence

191
.

What is missing from the above proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem are good
estimates for the rate of convergence of the untwisted period mapping. The purpose of
this section is to obtain such estimates, by using the curvature properties of the
Hodge metric and the maximum principle.

192
.

Let us first see what kind of estimates we can derive from the fact that the
untwisted period mapping
\Psi_{S}
extends across the origin.
Recall that the differential of the holomorphic mapping
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is equal to

e^{-z(S+N)}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}e^{z(S+N)}-(S+N)\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})}.

At the same time, for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small, we can certainly find a holomorphic
mapping
g\colon\Delta_{r}\to\GL(V)
with
g(0)=\id
such that
\Psi_{S}(t)=g(t)\cdot\Psi_{S}(0)
. By the chain rule, the differential of
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
is therefore
also equal to

e^{z}\cdot g^{\prime}(e^{z})g(e^{z})^{-1}\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(e^{z})},

provided that
\operatorname{Re}z<\log\varepsilon
. Putting both things together, we get

\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-(S+N)\equiv e^{z}\cdot e^{z(S+N)}g^{\prime}(e^{z})g(e^{z})^{-1}e^{-z(S+N)}\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{\Phi(z)},

at least when
\operatorname{Re}z<\log\varepsilon
. Arguing as in the proof of

28
to estimate the effect of conjugating by
e^{z(S+N)}
, the
Hodge norm of the operator on the right-hand side is bounded, for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
, by a constant multiple of

\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

where
m=m(N)+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}
; the exact value of the constant depends of course
on
g(t)
and hence on
\Psi_{S}(t)
. We remind the reader that
0<\delta(T)\leq 1
is
the minimal distance between consecutive eigenvalues of
T
on the unit circle,
divided by
2\pi
. If we denote by

N=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}N^{k,-k}\quad\text{and}\quad S=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{k,-k}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{\lambda}=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}

the Hodge decompositions, then it follows that the quantity

\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-N^{-1,1}-S^{-1,1}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq-2}\lVert N^{k,-k}+S^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

is bounded, for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
, by a constant multiple of
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
.

193
.

We can improve the estimates from above by changing the interval containing the eigenvalues
of
S
. Recall that
T_{s}=e^{2\pi iS}
, and that the eigenvalues of
S
are
supposed to lie in a half-open interval of length
1
. Write the eigenvalues of
T_{s}

in the form
\lambda_{j}=e^{2\pi i\alpha_{j}}
, say with
0\leq\alpha_{1}<\dotsb<\alpha_{n}<1
. If we gradually slide the interval
[0,1)
over to the right, we
obtain the following
n
choices for the operator
S
, namely

S=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\alpha_{j}P_{\lambda_{j}}+(P_{\lambda_{1}}+\dotsb+P_{\lambda_{k}}),

for
k=0,1,\dotsc,n-1
. If we apply the argument in the preceding paragraph to
each choice of
S
, and then take a suitable linear combination of the resulting
inequalities, we find that there are two constants
C>0
and
b\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-N^{-1,1}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq-2}\lVert N^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq-1}\lVert P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
. Here
m=m(N)+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}
, but we cannot say anything
about the value of the constant
C
, or about how big
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert
has to be for the
inequality to hold; these are going to depend, in some unspecified way, on the
variation of Hodge structure
E
. The problem is that these estimates are not

effective
.

Note
.

In fact, the argument above gives a somewhat better estimate for
P_{\lambda}
.
Instead of
\delta(T)
, the optimal exponent is the minimal distance, on the unit
circle, from
\lambda
to the two immediately adjacent eigenvalues of
T
.

194
.

We can make the above estimates effective – and independent of the specific
variation of Hodge structure! – by exploiting once more the curvature properties of
the Hodge metric. The precise result we are going to prove is the following.

Theorem 16
.

Given
x<0
, there are constants
C>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-N^{-1,1}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq-2}\lVert N^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2b}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

\displaystyle\sum_{k\leq-1}\lVert P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2b}e^{-2\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x
. The exact value of
C
only
depends on
x
, on
r=\rk E
, and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
; the
exact value of
b
only depends on
r
.

195
.

We need another basic fact about metrics with negative curvature on
holomorphic vector bundles. Let
E
be a smooth vector bundle with a hermitian metric

h
, defined on a domain
X\subseteq\mathbb{C}
, and suppose that
E

has the structure of a holomorphic vector bundle, given by a connection
d^{\prime\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{0,1}(X,E)
of type
(0,1)
. As before, we write the Chern
connection as
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
, where
\delta^{\prime}\colon A^{0}(X,E)\to A^{1,0}(X,E)
,
and denote by

\Theta=(\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime})^{2}\in A^{1,1}\bigl(X,\End(E)\bigr)

the curvature operator of the metric.

Lemma 33
.

Suppose that the hermitian metric
h
has semi-negative curvature, in the sense
that for every
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
, one has

h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,u,u\bigr)\leq 0.

Then for every nontrivial holomorphic section
u\in A^{0}(X,E)
with
d^{\prime\prime}u=0
,
the function
\log h(u,u)
is subharmonic on
X
.

Proof.

Since
\delta^{\prime}+d^{\prime\prime}
is a metric connection, we have
\bar{\partial}h(u,u)=h(u,\delta^{\prime}u)
, hence

\partial\bar{\partial}h(u,u)=h(\delta^{\prime}u,\delta^{\prime}u)+h(u,d^{\prime\prime}\delta^{\prime}u)=h(\delta^{\prime}u,\delta^{\prime}u)+h(u,\Theta u).

After evaluating this on
\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
, we
get

\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}h(u,u)=h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}u\bigr)-h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial z\wedge\partial/\partial\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}}\,u,u\bigr)\geq h\bigl(\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}u,\delta^{\prime}_{\partial/\partial z}u\bigr).

At all points of
X
where
h(u,u)>0
, we now get
\Delta\log h(u,u)\geq 0

from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, by the same argument as in the proof of

6
. Since
\log h(u,u)
is locally bounded from above,
it follows that
\log h(u,u)
is a well-defined subharmonic function with values
in
[-\infty,\infty)
.
∎

196
.

We return to our usual setting where
E
is a polarized variation of Hodge
structure on
\Delta^{\ast}
. The result above has the following implication for
\End(E)
.

Proposition 14
.

Let
u\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)\bigr)
be a smooth section with the property
that
d^{\prime\prime}u\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},F^{0}\End(E)\bigr)
. Write the Hodge
decomposition of
u
as

u=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}u^{k,-k},

where
u^{k,-k}\in A^{0}\bigl(\Delta^{\ast},\End(E)^{k,-k}\bigr)
. Then for every
b\geq 4r\binom{r+1}{3}
, the function

\log\left((-\log t)^{-b}\sum_{k\leq-1}h\bigl(u^{k,-k},u^{k,-k}\bigr)\right)

is subharmonic on
\Delta^{\ast}
.

Proof.

We apply the result from above to the quotient bundle
\End(E)/F^{0}\End(E)
, with
the hermitian metric
h
induced by the Hodge metric on
\End(E)
. According to

2
, the curvature tensor of this metric satisfies

h\bigl(\Theta_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\bigr)\leq 2h\bigl(\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u,\theta_{\partial/\partial t}u\bigr)\leq r\binom{r+1}{3}\frac{1}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}h(u,u),

using the improved bound for the Higgs field of
\End(E)
in

2
. Just as in
158
, we now consider the
modified hermitian metric

h^{\varphi}=h\cdot e^{-\varphi}=h\cdot(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{-b},

for
b\in\mathbb{N}
. Its curvature tensor satisfies

h^{\varphi}\bigl(\Theta^{\varphi}_{\partial/\partial t\wedge\partial/\partial\bar{t}}\,u,u\bigr)\leq\left(r\binom{r+1}{3}-\frac{b}{4}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{\lvert t\rvert^{2}(\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2}}h^{\varphi}(u,u)\leq 0,

provided we choose
b\geq 4r\binom{r+1}{3}
. By assumption,
u
gives a holomorphic
section of the quotient bundle
\End(E)/F^{0}\End(E)
, and so the result now
follows from
33
.
∎

197
.

We can now use the
maximum principle
to make the estimates in

192
effective. Since the operator
P_{\lambda}\in\End(V)
commutes with
T
, its defines a flat section of the bundle
\End(E)
on

\Delta^{\ast}
. Define

f=\sum_{k\leq-1}h\bigl(P_{\lambda}^{k,-k},P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}\bigr),

which is a smooth function on
\Delta^{\ast}
. According to

14
, the function

\varphi=\log\bigl(f\cdot(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{-b}\bigr)

is subharmonic on
\Delta^{\ast}
, where
b=4r\binom{r+1}{3}
. We also know from

192
that, in a small neighborhood of the origin,
f

is bounded from above by

C^{\prime}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2m(N)+4\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}\lvert t\rvert^{2\delta(T)}.

Since
b=16rC_{0}^{2}\geq 2m(N)+4\sqrt{2r}C_{0}
, it follows that

\varphi-2\delta(T)\log\lvert t\rvert\leq\log C^{\prime}

for
\lvert t\rvert
sufficiently small. In particular, this means that the subharmonic
function
\varphi-2\delta(T)\log\lvert t\rvert
is bounded from above on every compact
subset of
\Delta
. Now the maximum principle for subharmonic functions implies that

\varphi-2\delta(T)\log\lvert t\rvert\leq\max_{\lvert t\rvert=R}\Bigl(\varphi(t)-2\delta(T)\log R\Bigr)

for every
0<R<1
. Since
P_{\lambda}\in\End(V)
is a flat section, we can
estimate the right-hand side with the help of
7
.
After exponentiating, the conclusion is that

\sum_{k\leq-1}h\bigl(P_{\lambda}^{k,-k},P_{\lambda}^{k,-k}\bigr)\leq C(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{2b}\lvert t\rvert^{2\delta(T)}\quad\text{for $\lvert t\rvert\leq R$,}

where
b=4r\binom{r+1}{3}
and, assuming without loss of generality that
R>e^{-1}
,

C=R^{-2\delta(T)}e^{4\sqrt{2r}C_{0}\pi}(-\log R)^{-2(b+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0})}\lVert P_{\lambda}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{2}.

Since
4
contains an upper bound on
\lVert P_{\lambda}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}
,
with a constant that only depends on
r=\dim V
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
, this gives us the first inequality in
16
,
just with different notation.

198
.

To prove the other inequality, we apply the same argument to
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}-N
, viewed as a holomorphic section of the quotient
bundle
\End(E)/F^{0}\End(E)
on
\Delta^{\ast}
. Everything works out because the Hodge norm
of
t\theta_{\partial/\partial t}
is bounded from above by
C_{0}(-\log\lvert t\rvert)^{-1}
,
according to
1
, and because
\lVert N\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}
is bounded by
virtue of
4
. This finishes the proof of the effective
estimates in
16
.

7.7
Approximation by nilpotent orbits

199
.

In this section, we prove the second half of the nilpotent orbit theorem. Roughly
speaking, the result is that the original period mapping
\Phi(z)
can be
approximated very well by the nilpotent orbit
\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)=e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. We fix an
arbitrary base point
o\in D
, and for simplicity, we denote by
\lVert v\rVert=\lVert v\rVert_{o}
the
resulting norm on the vector space
V
. We use this norm to define the metric, and
hence the distance function
d_{\check{D}}
, on the compact dual
\check{D}
.

Theorem 17
.

There are constants
C>0
,
x_{0}<0
, and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)=e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in D\quad\text{and}\quad d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(z),\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(z)\bigr)\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
. The constants
C,x_{0}
only depend on
the base point
o\in D
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
; the integer
m
only depends on
r=\rk E
.

200
.

We first note that, due to the
G
-invariance of the metric on
D
, it is
enough to prove the theorem under the additional assumption that
\Phi(-1)=o
.
Here is why. Choose an element
g\in G
such that
g\cdot\Phi(-1)=o
,
and consider the modified period mapping
\Phi^{\prime}(z)=g\cdot\Phi(z)
. The monodromy
transformation changes to
T^{\prime}=gTg^{-1}
, which has the same minimal polynomial;
the limiting Hodge filtration changes to
F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\prime}=gF_{\mathrm{lim}}
. If the theorem is known
for the modified period mapping
\Phi^{\prime}
, then

g\cdot e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{zN^{\prime}}F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\prime}\in D\quad\text{for $\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}$},

with a constant
x_{0}
that depends on
o\in D
and on the minimal polynomial of

T^{\prime}
; but then clearly
e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in D
in the same range. In the same
way, the distance estimate for
\Phi^{\prime}
implies that for
\Phi
itself.

201
.

We assume from now on that
\Phi(-1)=o
. The following lemma tells us how close a
point of
\check{D}
has to be to
\Phi(z)
in order to belong to
D
.

Lemma 34
.

There is a constant
\varepsilon>0
such that

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr)\leq\varepsilon\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-4C_{0}}

implies both that
p\in D
and that

d_{D}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr)\leq 2ne^{4C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{4C_{0}}\cdot d_{\check{D}}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr).

The exact value of
\varepsilon
only depends on the choice of base point
o\in D
.

Proof.

Since
D
is open in
\check{D}
, there is a constant
\delta>0
such that

\bigl\{\thinspace p\in\check{D}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace d_{\check{D}}(p,o)<\delta\thinspace\bigr\}\subseteq D;

because
d_{D}
and
d_{\check{D}}
are continuous, we can arrange moreover that

d_{D}(p,o)\leq 2d_{\check{D}}(p,o)

on the open ball in question. Since we are using the Hodge norm at the base point

o\in D
to define the metric on
\check{D}
, this second condition means that

\delta
depends on the choice of
o\in D
. Fix a point
z\in\mathbb{H}
with

\operatorname{Re}z\leq-1
, and choose
g\in G
such that
\Phi(z)=g\cdot\Phi(-1)
. For any
v\in V
, we have

\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}=\lVert v\rVert_{g\cdot\Phi(-1)}=\lVert g^{-1}v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}.

According to
7
,

e^{-2C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-2C_{0}}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\leq\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}\leq e^{2C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2C_{0}}\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(-1)},

where
C_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\binom{r+1}{3}}
and
r=\rk E
. Putting both
things together, we get an upper bound on the operator norms of
g
and
g^{-1}
,
and therefore

\max\bigl(\lVert g\rVert_{\Phi(-1)},\lVert g^{-1}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\bigr)\leq\sqrt{r}e^{2C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2C_{0}}

This gives us a bound on the operator norm of
\Ad g\colon\End(V)\to\End(V)
;
indeed,

\lVert gAg^{-1}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\leq\lVert g\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\lVert A\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\lVert g^{-1}\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}\leq re^{4C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{4C_{0}}\lVert A\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}.

According to the discussion in
79
, for any
p\in\check{D}
, we have

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(g^{-1}p,o\bigr)\leq re^{4C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{4C_{0}}\cdot d_{\check{D}}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr)

Define
\varepsilon=e^{-4C_{0}\pi}\cdot\delta/r
. As long as
d_{\check{D}}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr)<\varepsilon\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-4C_{0}}
, we can conclude from this that
g^{-1}p\in D
and, therefore,
p\in D
; we also get the distance estimate

d_{D}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr)=d_{D}\bigl(g^{-1}p,o\bigr)\leq 2d_{\check{D}}\bigl(g^{-1}p,o\bigr)\leq 2re^{4C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{4C_{0}}\cdot d_{\check{D}}\bigl(p,\Phi(z)\bigr).

This is what we wanted to show.
∎

202
.

Fix a point
z\in\mathbb{H}
, and consider the curve

[0,\infty)\to\check{D},\quad x\mapsto e^{xN}\Phi(z-x).

At
x=0
, this starts at the point
\Phi(z)\in D
; as
x\to\infty
, it converges
to the point

\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xN}\Phi(z-x)=e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in\check{D}.

The derivative at a given point
x>0
is easily seen to be

N-e^{xN}\theta_{\partial/\partial z}(z-x)e^{-xN}\mod F^{0}\End(V)_{e^{xN}\Phi(z-x)}.

We know from the effective estimates in
16
that

\lVert\theta_{\partial/\partial z}-N^{-1,1}\rVert_{\Phi(z-x)}^{2}+\sum_{k\leq-2}\lVert N^{k,-k}\rVert_{\Phi(z-x)}^{2}\leq C^{2}\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert+x\bigr)^{2b}e^{-2\delta(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert+x)}.

where
\delta=\delta(T)
, and where the value of
C
depends on
r=\rk E
and
on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
. Arguing as in the proof of

28
, it follows that the length of the derivative, measured using
the metric on
\check{D}
, is bounded from above by

C^{\prime}\bigl(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert+x\bigr)^{b+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}e^{-\delta(\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert+x)}\sum_{k=0}^{m(N)}\frac{(2x)^{k}}{k!}\lVert N\rVert_{\Phi(-1)}^{k}.

After integrating this expression over the interval
[0,\infty)
, we get a bound
for the distance between
\Phi(z)
and
e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
that looks like

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(\Phi(z),e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\bigr)\leq C^{\prime\prime}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{b+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}e^{-\delta\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

with a constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
whose exact value is somewhat complicated, but depends only
on the two integers
m(N)
and
r=\rk E
and on the minimal polynomial of
T\in\GL(V)
.

203
.

Now let us choose
x_{0}<0
in such a way that
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
implies

C^{\prime\prime}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{b+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}e^{-\delta\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\leq\varepsilon\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-4C_{0}},

where
\varepsilon
is the constant from
34
. We can then conclude
that
e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in D
; we also get the distance estimate

d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(z),e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\bigr)\leq 2re^{4C_{0}\pi}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{4C_{0}}\cdot C^{\prime\prime}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{b+2\sqrt{2r}C_{0}}e^{-\delta\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

Since all the constants on the right-hand side have the correct dependence on
parameters, this completes the proof of the second half of the nilpotent orbit theorem.

204
.

We close this chapter with two remarks about the nilpotent orbit theorem:

1.

The nilpotent orbit theorem guarantees that
e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in D
for
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
, where the constant
x_{0}<0
only depends on
\rk E
and on the
minimal polynomial of
T
. One may wonder whether this actually holds for every

z\in\mathbb{H}
. We do not know the answer to this question, and we were not able
to find any relevant examples in the literature.

2.

One can easily prove a variant of the nilpotent orbit theorem for the
filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)
. The statement is that there are constants
C>0
and
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that

e^{z(S+N)}\Psi_{S}(0)\in D\quad\text{and}\quad d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(z),e^{z(S+N)}\Psi_{S}(0)\bigr)\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

provided that
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
. In fact,
31
gives us the
distance estimate

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(0),F_{\mathrm{lim}}\bigr)\leq Ce^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

and so the desired result follows from
17

and its proof.

8
Convergence of the rescaled period mapping

205
.

In this chapter, we use the nilpotent orbit theorem and its consequences to show
that the
rescaled period mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}\colon\mathbb{H}\to D,\quad\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z),

has a well-defined limit in
D
as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
. Recall from

139
that the operator
H\in\End(V)
is a splitting for the
monodromy weight filtration
W_{\bullet}
.

Theorem 18
.

Let
H\in\End(V)
be as in
9
. Then the limit

e^{-N}F_{H}=\lim_{\operatorname{Re}z\to-\infty}\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)\in D

exists in the period domain. The resulting filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}
satisfies

T_{s}(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\quad H(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\quad N(F_{H}^{\bullet})\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet-1}.

We remind the reader that the two operators
H
and
i(S+N)
both belong to the Lie
algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of the real group
G=\Aut(V,Q)
, which means that

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\in G.

This is why the rescaled period mapping stays in
D
. Also note that the rescaled
period mapping depends both on
S
and on
H
; we will see during the proof that the
filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}
only depends on
H
, justifying the notation.

8.1
Proof of convergence in the compact dual

206
.

Let us first rewrite everything in terms of
\Psi_{S}
. We have

\displaystyle e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{z(S+N)}\Psi_{S}(e^{z})

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert(S+N)}\Psi_{S}(e^{z}).

The relation
[H,N]=-2N
implies that

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}Ne^{-\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}=e^{-\log x}\cdot N=\frac{N}{x},

(206.1)

and therefore gives us the useful identity

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}e^{-xN}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}=e^{-N}.

Putting everything together, we arrive at

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z)=e^{-N}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(e^{z}),

(206.2)

due to the fact that
[S,N]=[S,H]=0
(by
9
).

207
.

We know from the nilpotent orbit theorem that
\Psi_{S}(e^{z})
converges to
\Psi_{S}(0)

at a rate proportional to
\lvert e^{z}\rvert=e^{-\operatorname{Re}z}
. The effect of the two exponential
factors is controlled by
31
. For the exponential factor

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}
, the filtration by increasing eigenvalues of
H
is
exactly the monodromy weight filtration
W_{\bullet}
;
moreover, the rate of convergence is
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, since the eigenvalues of
H
will generally be consecutive integers. For
the other exponential factor
e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}=e^{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert(-S)}
, the relevant
filtration is by
decreasing
eigenvalues of
S
(because of the minus sign);
the rate of convergence is
e^{-\delta\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
, where
\delta>0
is the
minimal distance among consecutive eigenvalues of
S
.

208
.

Let us now prove the convergence of the rescaled period mapping. Since
\Psi_{S}\colon\Delta\to\check{D}
is holomorphic, there is a constant
C>0
such that

d_{\check{D}}\bigl(\Psi_{S}(e^{z}),\Psi_{S}(0)\bigr)\leq Ce^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}\quad\text{for $\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0$.}

According to
30
, the operator norm of
\Ad e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}
is
bounded by a constant multiple of
e^{(\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}-\alpha_{\mathrm{min}})\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
. From
(
79.1
), we therefore get

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(e^{z}),\,e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(0)\Bigr)\leq C^{\prime}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

using the fact that
(1+\alpha_{\mathrm{min}})-\alpha_{\mathrm{max}}\geq\delta(T)
is greater or
equal to the minimal distance between adjacent eigenvalues of
T
.

209
.

Recall that the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in\check{D}
satisfies

F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)

and that
31
gives us a distance estimate of the form

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(0),F_{\mathrm{lim}}\Bigr)\leq C^{\prime\prime}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

Because of the triangle inequality, we then get

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(e^{z}),F_{\mathrm{lim}}\Bigr)\leq(C^{\prime}+C^{\prime\prime})e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

(209.1)

210
.

Next, we have to analyze the effect of the second exponential factor
e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}
. On the one hand, we have

d_{\check{D}}\Bigl(e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(t),\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\Bigr)\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m(N)}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

due to the fact that the operator norm of
\Ad e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}
is bounded
by a constant multiple of
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{2m}
by
30
; here
m(N)

is the largest integer such that
N^{m}\neq 0
. On the other hand, the limit

F_{H}=\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in\check{D}

exists by
31
. Putting everything together, we find that the
limit

\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,S}\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=F_{H}

exists in
\check{D}
. It follows that the Hodge filtrations of the rescaled period
mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z)\in D

converge, in the compact dual
\check{D}
, to the filtration
e^{-N}F_{H}
.

211
.

The filtration
F_{H}
can be described concretely as follows. First, we take the
filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)
from the nilpotent orbit theorem and make it compatible with
the eigenspace decomposition of
S
, by projecting to the subquotients of the
filtration by
decreasing
eigenvalues of
S
. By construction,

T_{s}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}=F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}.

Similarly, the filtration
F_{H}
is obtained by starting from
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
,
and making it compatible with the eigenspace decomposition of
H
by projecting to
the subquotients of the monodromy weight filtration
W_{\bullet}
(which is the
filtration by
increasing
eigenvalues of
H
). This gives us the two relations

T_{s}\cdot F_{H}^{\bullet}=F_{H}^{\bullet}\quad\text{and}\quad H\cdot F_{H}^{\bullet}=F_{H}^{\bullet}.

We already know from the nilpotent orbit theorem that
N\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet}\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{\bullet-1}
. From this, we can easily deduce that

N\cdot F_{H}^{\bullet}\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet-1}.

Indeed, the filtration
F_{H}\in\check{D}
was defined in such a way that

F_{H}^{p}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xH}F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}.

From (
206.1
), we have
e^{-xH}Ne^{xH}=e^{2x}N
, which gives

N\cdot F_{H}^{p}=\lim_{x\to\infty}Ne^{xH}F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xH}NF_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}\\
\subseteq\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xH}F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p-1}=F_{H}^{p-1}.

This is what we wanted to prove.

8.2
Proof that the limit belongs to the period domain

212
.

It remains to argue that
e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
, and hence that

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z)

actually converges to a polarized Hodge structure on
V
. From the Hodge norm
estimates, we know that the corresponding inner products

\bigl\langle v,\,w\bigr\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)}=\Bigl\langle e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v,\,e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}w\Bigr\rangle_{\Phi(z)}

remain
bounded
as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
, for every
v,w\in V
.
Equivalently, if we fix a norm
\lVert-\rVert
on the vector space
V
, we have an upper
bound

\bigl\lVert v\bigr\rVert_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)}^{2}=\bigl\lVert e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\leq C\lVert v\rVert^{2}

for some constant
C>0
. To prove the convergence, we need a lower bound. This can
easily be obtained with the help of the following trick.

213
.

Fix a basis
v_{1},\dotsc,v_{r}\in V
, and consider the
r\times r
-matrix
M(z)
with
entries

\Bigl\langle e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v_{i},\,e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v_{j}\Bigr\rangle_{\Phi(z)}^{2}

We need to bound the inverse matrix
M(z)^{-1}
, and since we know that all entries
of
M(z)
stay bounded as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
, we only need to control the
function
\det M(z)
. But the determinant can be computed in a different way.
The wedge product

v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r}\in\det V

is a multi-valued flat section of the variation of Hodge structure on
\det E
. Since

\rk(\det E)=1
, we have, with the appropriate notation,

\displaystyle H(v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r})

\displaystyle=N(v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r})=0,

\displaystyle S(v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r})

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r})

for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Therefore the exponential factors act trivially and

\det M(z)=\lVert v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}.

But now the variation of Hodge structure on
\det E
can only have a single Hodge
type
(p,q)
, with
p+q=kr
. Therefore the Hodge norm of
v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r}
is equal to

\lVert v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r}\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}=(-1)^{q}Q(v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r},v_{1}\wedge\dotsb\wedge v_{r}),

which is
(-1)^{q}
times the determinant of the
r\times r
-matrix with entries

Q(v_{i},v_{j})
. Anyway, the conclusion is that
\det M(z)
is a nonzero constant. This
implies that the inverse matrix
M(z)^{-1}
is also bounded, and hence that

\frac{1}{C}\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq\bigl\lVert e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}v\bigr\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\leq C\lVert v\rVert^{2}

for a suitable constant
C>0
and all
v\in V
.

214
.

Now we get the result that we want from the following lemma. Pretty much the same
argument also appears in
[
Kashiwara
, Lem 4.2.1]
.

Lemma 35
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{N}\to D
be a sequence of points of
D
such that:

1.

The limit
\lim_{m\to\infty}f(m)
exists in
\check{D}
.

2.

There is a constant
C>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}\lVert v\rVert^{2}\leq\lVert v\rVert_{f(m)}^{2}\leq C\lVert v\rVert^{2}

for all vectors
v\in V
.

Then
\lim_{m\to\infty}f(m)\in D
.

Proof.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we have the Hodge decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V_{f(m)}^{p,q}.

After passing to a subsequence, we can assume that each limit

W^{p,q}=\lim_{m\to\infty}V_{f(m)}^{p,q}

exists (by compactness of the Grassmannian). We have to prove that

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}W^{p,q},

and that this Hodge structure of weight
n
is polarized by the pairing
Q
. Since
the different subspaces
W^{p,q}
are obviously orthogonal to each other under

Q
, it suffices to show that
(-1)^{q}Q
is positive definite on
W^{p,q}
. By hypothesis,

(-1)^{q}Q(v,v)=\lVert v\rVert_{f(m)}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{C}\lVert v\rVert^{2}

for all
v\in V_{f(m)}^{p,q}
. After passing to the limit, the same is then
true for
v\in W^{p,q}
, which means that
(-1)^{q}Q
is positive definite. It is easy
to deduce from this that

W^{p,q}\cap W^{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}=\{0\}

whenever
(p,q)\neq(p^{\prime},q^{\prime})
, and so we do get a polarized Hodge structure.
∎

215
.

We can use the effective estimates from the proof of the nilpotent orbit theorem to
get a better bound for the distance between

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z)\in D

and its limit
e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
. According to

17
, we have

d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(z),e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\bigr)\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert},

for every
z\in\mathbb{H}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\leq x_{0}
, with constants
C>0
and
x_{0}<0

that are basically independent of the period mapping in question. Since the
exponential factors in the definition of
\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)
belong to the real Lie
group
G
, it follows that

d_{D}\Bigl(\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z),\,e^{-N}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\Bigr)\leq C\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}.

Here we used (
206.1
) to simplify the second argument. Because
H
has
integer eigenvalues,
e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
converges to
F_{H}
at a
rate proportional to
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. This means that there is a constant
C^{\prime}>0
, whose value depends on
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, such that

d_{D}\bigl(\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z),e^{-N}F_{H}\bigr)\leq C^{\prime}\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Schmid’s
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem gives much more precise information about the
convergence; we are going to prove a weak form of this result later, in

Section

11
.

9
Results about mixed Hodge structures

216
.

In this chapter, we review some basic facts about complex mixed Hodge structures. We
also discuss Deligne’s functorial splitting for the weight filtration; we are going
to need it during the proof of the cheap
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem in

Section

11
. Everything in this chapter is just (somewhat tedious) linear
algebra, but the results are important for the study of degenerating variations of
Hodge structure, especially in higher dimensions.

9.1
Complex mixed Hodge structures

217
.

Unlike in the real case, we need
three
filtrations to describe a mixed Hodge
structure, because the Hodge decomposition in a complex Hodge structure is not
determined by the Hodge filtration alone.

Definition 5
.

A
mixed Hodge structure
on a finite-dimensional complex vector space
V

consists of an increasing filtration
W_{\bullet}
with
W_{n}=0
for
n\ll 0

and
W_{n}=V
for
n\gg 0
, and two decreasing filtrations

F^{\bullet}
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{\bullet}
, such that each subquotient

\gr_{n}^{W}=W_{n}/W_{n-1}

has a Hodge structure of weight
n
, given by the two induced filtrations

\displaystyle F^{\bullet}\gr_{n}^{W}

\displaystyle=(F^{\bullet}\cap W_{n}+W_{n-1})/W_{n-1}

\displaystyle\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{\bullet}\gr_{n}^{W}

\displaystyle=(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{\bullet}\cap W_{n}+W_{n-1})/W_{n-1}.

The filtration
W_{\bullet}
is called the
weight filtration
.

218
.

To save space, we are going to use the shorthand notation

F^{p}W_{n}=F^{p}\cap W_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\cap W_{n}

from now on. The
(p,q)
-subspace in the Hodge decomposition of
\gr_{n}^{W}
is

F^{p}\gr_{n}^{W}\cap\,\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\gr_{n}^{W}=\frac{(F^{p}W_{n}+W_{n-1})\cap(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}+W_{n-1})}{W_{n-1}}.

In order to have a mixed Hodge structure on
V
, the direct sum of these subspaces
(over
p+q=n
) must equal
\gr_{n}^{W}
, which means concretely that

W_{n}=\sum_{p+q=n}(F^{p}W_{n}+W_{n-1})\cap(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}+W_{n-1})

and that, whenever
p+q>n
, one has

(F^{p}W_{n}+W_{n-1})\cap(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}+W_{n-1})=W_{n-1}.

From this, one deduces, by induction on the length of the weight filtration, that

F^{p}W_{n}\cap\Bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-1}W_{n-1}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-2}W_{n-2}+\dotsb\Bigr)=0

(218.1)

whenever
p+q>n
. Either way, this is a fairly complicated set of conditions.

Example 7
.

A mixed Hodge structure is called
real
if
V=V_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}
for an

\mathbb{R}
-vector space
V_{\mathbb{R}}
, and if
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}=\sigma(F)
and
W=\sigma(W)
, where
\sigma\in\End_{\mathbb{R}}(V)
is the conjugation operator
\sigma(v\otimes z)=v\otimes\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
.
Since the weight filtration is defined over
\mathbb{R}
, each subquotient
\gr_{n}^{W}
is a
real Hodge structure of weight
n
.

219
.

Here are some general operations on mixed Hodge structures:

1.

Let
V
be a mixed Hodge structure. For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, we obtain a new mixed
Hodge structure
V(k)
on the same underlying vector space by setting

W_{n}V(k)=W_{n+2k}V,\quad F^{p}V(k)=F^{p-k}V,\quad\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}V(k)=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-k}V

for
n,p,q\in\mathbb{Z}
. This operation is called the
Tate twist
.

2.

Let
V
be a mixed Hodge structure. The
dual
vector space
\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})
inherits a mixed Hodge structure, with

\displaystyle W_{n}\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(W_{-n-1})=0\thinspace\bigr\},

\displaystyle F^{p}\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(F^{-p+1})=0\thinspace\bigr\},

\displaystyle\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to\mathbb{C}\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-q+1})=0\thinspace\bigr\}.

It is easy to see that
\gr_{n}^{W}\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})\cong\Hom\bigl(\gr_{-n}^{W}V,\mathbb{C}\bigr)
are isomorphic Hodge structures of weight
n
.

3.

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be mixed Hodge structures. Their
tensor product

V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
is again a mixed Hodge structure, with

\displaystyle W_{n}(V_{1}\otimes V_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}=n}W_{n^{\prime}}V_{1}\otimes W_{n^{\prime\prime}}V_{2},

\displaystyle F^{p}(V_{1}\otimes V_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{p^{\prime}+p^{\prime\prime}=p}F^{p^{\prime}}V_{1}\otimes F^{p^{\prime\prime}}V_{2},

\displaystyle\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}(V_{1}\otimes V_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{q^{\prime}+q^{\prime\prime}=q}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q^{\prime}}V_{1}\otimes\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q^{\prime\prime}}V_{2}.

As expected, one has an isomorphism of Hodge structures

\gr_{n}^{W}(V_{1}\otimes V_{2})\cong\bigoplus_{n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}=n}\gr_{n^{\prime}}^{W}V_{1}\otimes\gr_{n^{\prime\prime}}^{W}V_{2}.

4.

An important special case is the
endomorphism algebra

\End(V)
of the
vector space
V
underlying a mixed Hodge structure. It has a mixed Hodge structure with

\displaystyle W_{n}\End(V)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(W_{\bullet})\subseteq W_{\bullet+n}\thinspace\bigr\},

\displaystyle F^{p}\End(V)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(F^{\bullet})\subseteq F^{\bullet+p}\thinspace\bigr\},

\displaystyle\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\End(V)

\displaystyle=\bigl\{\thinspace f\colon V\to V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace f(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{\bullet})\subseteq\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{\bullet+q}\thinspace\bigr\}.

With these definitions, the multiplication map
\End(V)\otimes\End(V)\to\End(V)
becomes a morphism of mixed Hodge structures.

9.2
Deligne’s splitting construction

220
.

A mixed Hodge structure is called
split
if it is a direct sum of Hodge
structures of different weights, with the obvious weight filtration. For example,
any
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
