-Hodge structure is a split mixed Hodge structure. Being
split is equivalent to having a decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{i,j}

with the property that

W_{n}=\bigoplus_{i+j\leq n}V^{i,j},\quad F^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}V^{i,j},\quad\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigoplus_{j\geq q,i}V^{i,j}.

If
V
is a mixed Hodge structure, then the associated graded

\gr_{\bullet}^{W}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr_{n}^{W}

becomes in a natural way a split mixed Hodge structure.

221
.

A general mixed Hodge structure is of course not split. Nevertheless, Deligne

[
Deligne
, Lem. 1.2.11]
showed that there is a functorial decomposition

V=\bigoplus_{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}}I^{i,j}

into subspaces, with the property that

W_{n}=\bigoplus_{i+j\leq n}I^{i,j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}I^{i,j},

(221.1)

and such that
I^{i,j}
maps isomorphically to the
(i,j)
-subspace in the Hodge
decomposition of
\gr_{i+j}^{W}
, under the projection from
W_{i+j}
to

\gr_{i+j}^{W}
. The subspaces in question are defined by the formula

I^{i,j}=F^{i}\cap W_{i+j}\cap\bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j}\cap W_{i+j}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j-1}\cap W_{i+j-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j-2}\cap W_{i+j-3}+\dotsb\bigr).

(221.2)

The decomposition is functorial in the following sense. Suppose that
f\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures, which means that

f(W_{n}V_{1})\subseteq W_{n}V_{2},\quad f(F^{p}V_{1})\subseteq F^{p}V_{2},\quad f(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}V_{1})\subseteq\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}V_{2}

for all
p,q,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then with the obvious notation, we have
f(I_{1}^{i,j})\subseteq I_{2}^{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. This is an immediate consequence of the
formula for
I^{i,j}
.

Example 8
.

When the mixed Hodge structure is split, one has
I^{i,j}=F^{i}\cap W_{i+j}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j}
and

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigoplus_{j\geq q,i}I^{i,j}.

In the case of a non-split mixed Hodge structure, there is no simple formula for

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
in terms of the subspaces
I^{i,j}
.

222
.

Let us try to understand Deligne’s construction from a slightly different point of
view. To get a decomposition of
V
, all we need is a
splitting

H\in\End(V)
for the weight filtration; by this we mean that
H
is semisimple with integer
eigenvalues, and that
W_{n}=E_{n}(H)\oplus W_{n-1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Such a splitting induces an isomorphism

V=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}(H)\,\cong\,\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr_{n}^{W},

and the desired decomposition of
V
is then simply the image of the Hodge
decomposition in the direct sum of Hodge structures on the right-hand side.

223
.

Any splitting preserves the weight filtration, and acts on
\gr_{n}^{W}
as multiplication
by
n
. This condition actually characterizes splittings, as the following lemma
shows.

Lemma 36
.

Let
V
be a mixed Hodge structure.

(a)

Suppose that
H\in W_{0}\End(V)
acts on each subquotient
\gr_{n}^{W}
as
multiplication by
n
. Then
H
is a splitting of the weight filtration.

(b)

If
H
and
H^{\prime}
are two splittings of the weight filtration, then there is a
unique element
g\in\GL(V)
such that
g-\id\in W_{-1}\End(V)
and
H^{\prime}=gHg^{-1}
.

Proof.

We first prove (a). For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
(H-n\id)(W_{n})\subseteq W_{n-1}
because of the assumptions on
H
. If
W_{n_{0}-1}=0
and
W_{n_{1}}=V
, we get

\prod_{n=n_{0}}^{n_{1}}(H-n\id)=0,

and so
H
is semisimple with integer eigenvalues. It also follows that
W_{n}=E_{n}(H)\oplus W_{n-1}
, and so
H
is indeed a splitting.

Next, we prove (b). There is a unique element
g\in\GL(V)
that maps each
eigenspace
E_{n}(H)
into the eigenspace
E_{n}(H^{\prime})
and makes all the following diagrams
commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}(H^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}(H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gr_{n}^{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

Clearly then
H^{\prime}=gHg^{-1}
, and also
g-\id\in W_{-1}\End(V)
, as claimed.
∎

224
.

Now we return to Deligne’s construction. Using the mixed Hodge structure on

\End(V)
, we can express the condition in (
221.1
) as

H\in F^{0}W_{0}\End(V)=F^{0}\End(V)\cap W_{0}\End(V).

Deligne’s result then takes the following form.

Proposition 15
.

Let
V
be a mixed Hodge structure. There is a unique splitting
H\in\End(V)
for the weight filtration with the property that

H\in F^{0}W_{0}\End(V)\cap\Bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{0}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\Bigr).

Moreover, if
v\in F^{p}\cap W_{p+q}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}
, then one has
Hv=(p+q)v
.

Proof.

We first prove existence. Consider the endomorphism
h
of

\gr_{\bullet}^{W}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr_{n}^{W}

that acts as multiplication by
n
on the summand
\gr_{n}^{W}
. In terms of
the mixed Hodge structure on
\End(V)
, this endomorphism is an element of

\gr_{0}^{W}\End(V)
, of Hodge type
(0,0)
. We start by lifting
h
in an arbitrary
way to an element
H\in F^{0}W_{0}\End(V)
; according to
36
, this
is a splitting of the weight filtration. We are now going to adjust this
initial choice.

We can also lift
h
to an element
\bar{H}_{0}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{0}\End(V)
. Then
H-\bar{H}_{0}\in W_{-1}\End(V)
, and we consider its image in
\gr_{-1}^{W}\End(V)
, which has a
Hodge structure of weight
-1
. As such,

\gr_{-1}^{W}\End(V)=F^{0}\gr_{-1}^{W}\End(V)\,\oplus\,\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}\gr_{-1}^{W}\End(V),

and so we can find
H_{-1}\in F^{0}W_{-1}\End(V)
and
\bar{H}_{-1}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{-1}\End(V)
such that
H-\bar{H}_{0}
and
H_{-1}+\bar{H}_{-1}
have the
same image in
\gr_{-1}^{W}\End(V)
. If we replace
H
by
H-H_{-1}
, and

\bar{H}_{0}
by
\bar{H}_{0}+\bar{H}_{-1}
, we can arrange that
H-\bar{H}_{0}\in W_{-2}\End(V)
.
Next, we project into

\gr_{-2}^{W}\End(V)=F^{0}\gr_{-2}^{W}\End(V)\,\oplus\,\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}\gr_{-2}^{W}\End(V).

As before, we can find
H_{-2}\in F^{0}W_{-2}\End(V)
and
\bar{H}_{-2}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)
such that
H-\bar{H}_{0}
and
H_{-2}+\bar{H}_{-2}
have
the same image in
\gr_{-2}^{W}\End(V)
; after replacing
H
by
H-\bar{H}_{0}
, we get

H-\bar{H}_{0}-\bar{H}_{-2}\in W_{-3}\End(V)
. Continuing in this manner, we obtain

H=\bar{H}_{0}+\bar{H}_{-2}+\bar{H}_{-3}+\dotsb,

where
\bar{H}_{k}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{k+1}W_{k}\End(V)
for
k\leq-2
. This is the desired
splitting. It is clear from this argument that the splitting
H
is optimal among
all splittings that preserve
F
, in the sense that the subspace of
\End(V)
in
the definition is as small as possible.

Uniqueness of
H
follows in the same way. If we have two splittings
H,H^{\prime}\in\End(V)
with the stated properties, then their difference
H-H^{\prime}
is an element of

F^{0}W_{-1}\End(V)\cap\Bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{0}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\Bigr).

But since
\End(V)
is a mixed Hodge structure, this intersection is trivial by
(
218.1
).

Finally, let us suppose that we have a vector
v\in F^{p}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\cap W_{n}
,
where
n=p+q
. Then

Hv-nv\in F^{p}\cap W_{n-1}\cap\Bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n-1}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-1}W_{n-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-2}W_{n-3}+\dotsb\Bigr),

and as the intersection on the right-hand side is again trivial, it follows that
Hv=nv
.
∎

225
.

We are going to denote the splitting in
15
by the symbol
H(W,F,\overline{F}\vphantom{F})
if we want to emphasize its dependence on the mixed Hodge structure.

226
.

It is now an easy matter to deduce Deligne’s formula for the subspaces
I^{i,j}
in
(
221.2
). Let
n=p+q
, and suppose that we have a vector of Hodge type

(p,q)
in
\gr_{n}^{W}
. We can lift it to
v\in F^{p}W_{n}
and also to
\bar{v}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}W_{n}
. Let
P_{n}\colon V\to E_{n}(H)
denote the projection to the
n
-eigenspace of

H
; since
H
is semisimple with integer eigenvalues, this equals

P_{n}=\prod_{k\neq n}\frac{1}{n-k}(H-k\id),

where the product runs over all eigenvalues of
H
different from
n
. As
v-\bar{v}\in W_{n-1}
, we have
P_{n}v=P_{n}\bar{v}
. Now
v\in F^{p}\cap W_{n}
, and therefore

P_{n}v\in F^{p}\cap W_{n}.

On the other hand, we have
\bar{v}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\cap W_{n}
, and therefore

P_{n}\bar{v}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\cap W_{n}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-1}\cap W_{n-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q-2}\cap W_{n-3}+\dotsb,

because of the defining properties of
H
in
15
. This proves that

P_{n}v\in I^{p,q}
. The conclusion is that, in the decomposition of
V
induced by
the splitting
H
, each subspace is contained in some
I^{p,q}
.

227
.

Conversely, let us show that
H
acts as multiplication by
i+j
on the subspace

I^{i,j}
. Set
n=i+j
. For any vector
v\in I^{i,j}
, we have
v\in F^{i}\cap W_{n}
, hence

Hv-nv\in F^{i}\cap W_{n-1}.

At the same time, the formula in (
221.2
) and the properties of
H
imply
that

Hv-nv\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j}W_{n-1}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j-1}W_{n-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j-2}W_{n-3}+\dotsb

Since the intersection between these two subspaces is trivial by
(
218.1
), we get
Hv=nv
, as claimed. This is enough to conclude
that, under the isomorphism

V=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}E_{n}(H)\,\cong\,\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\gr_{n}^{W},

the subspace of type
(i,j)
in the Hodge decomposition on the right-hand side
corresponds exactly to the subspace
I^{i,j}
.

228
.

Let us also check that Deligne’s construction respects the basic operations on mixed
Hodge structures; this point is not covered very well in the literature

[
Morgan
, Prop. 1.9]
.

Proposition 16
.

The splitting in
15
is functorial; it is also compatible with
duals and tensor products.

Proof.

Let us first consider tensor products. Suppose that
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are two mixed
Hodge structures, and set
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
. Denote the three splittings
coming from
15
by
H_{1}\in\End(V_{1})
,
H_{2}\in\End(V_{2})
, and

H\in\End(V)
. Then the claim is
that
H=H_{1}\otimes\id+\id\otimes H_{2}
. Now the operator
H_{1}\otimes\id+\id\otimes H_{2}\in\End(V)
acts on

\gr_{n}^{W}V\cong\bigoplus_{n^{\prime}+n^{\prime\prime}=n}\gr_{n^{\prime}}^{W}V_{1}\otimes\gr_{n^{\prime\prime}}^{W}V_{2}

as multiplication by
n
; since it is also semisimple with integer eigenvalues, it
is a splitting of the weight filtration on
V
. From the definition of the mixed
Hodge structure on
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
, it is easy to see that both
H_{1}\otimes\id
and
\id\otimes H_{2}
belong to

F^{0}W_{0}\End(V)\cap\Bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{0}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\Bigr).

The result we want now follows from the uniqueness statement in

15
.

The same kind of argument proves that if
V
is a mixed Hodge structure, and if

H\in\End(V)
denotes the splitting in
15
, then the splitting
of the mixed Hodge structure on
\Hom(V,\mathbb{C})
is given by the formula
f\mapsto-f\circ H
.

Lastly, let us prove the functoriality of the splitting. Suppose that
f\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures. Let
H_{1}\in\End(V_{1})
and

H_{2}\in\End(V_{2})
denote the two splittings. By the above, the splitting of the
mixed Hodge structure on
\Hom(V_{1},V_{2})
is then given by
Hf=H_{2}\circ f-f\circ H_{1}
. Since
f\in\Hom(V_{1},V_{2})
is a morphism, it satisfies

f\in F^{0}\Hom(V_{1},V_{2})\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}\Hom(V_{1},V_{2})\cap W_{0}\Hom(V_{1},V_{2}).

By
15
, it follows that
Hf=0
, which translates into
H_{2}\circ f=f\circ H_{1}
.
∎

229
.

What about complex conjugation? If we swap the role of the two filtrations
F
and

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
, we obtain another splitting
\bar{H}=H(W,\overline{F}\vphantom{F},F)
, whose characteristic property
is that

\bar{H}\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{0}\End(V)\cap\Bigl(F^{0}W_{0}\End(V)+F^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+F^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\Bigr).

The corresponding subspaces in the decomposition of
V
are then

\bar{I}^{i,j}=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{j}\cap W_{i+j}\cap\bigl(F^{i}\cap W_{i+j}+F^{i-1}\cap W_{i+j-2}+F^{i-2}\cap W_{i+j-3}+\dotsb\bigr).

We have
H=\bar{H}
if and only if
H\in\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}\End(V)
if and only if the mixed Hodge
structure is split; we leave the proof of this assertion as an easy exercise.

230
.

One can also understand the result in
15
by comparing the two
splittings
H
and
\bar{H}
. We have
\bar{H}-H\in W_{-2}\End(V)
, and therefore

\displaystyle\bar{H}-H\in

\displaystyle\bigl(F^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+F^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\bigr)\cap

\displaystyle\bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}\End(V)+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}\End(V)+\dotsb\bigr).

This element functions as a sort of “extension class” for the mixed Hodge structure
(because
V
is split if and only if
\bar{H}-H=0
).

231
.

Given a mixed Hodge structure, let us consider the subspace

R(W_{-2})=\bigl(F^{-1}W_{-2}+F^{-2}W_{-3}+\dotsb\bigr)\cap\bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}+\dotsb\bigr).

The next proposition describes its properties.

Proposition 17
.

In any mixed Hodge structure, one has a decomposition

W_{-1}=F^{0}W_{-1}\oplus\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{-1}\oplus R(W_{-2}).

The subspace
R(W_{-2})
is a sub-mixed Hodge structure, and

R(W_{-2})=\bigoplus_{i,j\leq-1}I^{i,j}=\bigoplus_{i,j\leq-1}\bar{I}^{i,j}.

Proof.

The formula in (
221.2
) shows that
I^{i,j}\subseteq R(W_{-2})
for

i,j\leq-1
; by symmetry, it follows that
\bar{I}^{i,j}\subseteq R(W_{-2})
as
well. Now remember that
I^{i,j}
maps isomorphically to the subspace of type

(i,j)
in the Hodge decomposition of
\gr_{i+j}^{W}
. It follows readily that

W_{n}=F^{0}W_{n}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{n}+W_{n}\cap R(W_{-2})

for every
n\leq-1
. Indeed, the subspace on the right-hand side maps onto

\gr_{n}^{W}
under the projection
W_{n}\to\gr_{n}^{W}
, because its image contains
the subspace of type
(i,j)
in the Hodge decomposition as long as either
i\geq 0
, or
j\geq 0
, or
i,j\leq-1
. At the same time, it
is easy to see that the three subspaces are linearly independent. Indeed, if we
have
v+\bar{v}+w=0
with
v\in F^{0}W_{-1}
,
\bar{v}\in F^{0}W_{-1}
, and
w\in R(W_{-2})
, then

v=-(\bar{v}+w)\in F^{0}W_{-1}\cap\bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{-1}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-1}W_{-2}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{-2}W_{-3}+\dotsb\bigr),

and this intersection is trivial by (
218.1
). By symmetry, we get
v=\bar{v}=w=0
. Since

W_{-1}=\bigoplus_{i+j\leq-1}I^{i,j}=F^{0}W_{-1}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq-1\\
i\leq-1\end{subarray}}I^{i,j}=\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{0}W_{-1}\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i+j\leq-1\\
i\leq-1\end{subarray}}\bar{I}^{i,j},

this also proves the nice identity

R(W_{-2})=\bigoplus_{i,j\leq-1}I^{i,j}=\bigoplus_{i,j\leq-1}\bar{I}^{i,j}.

Because of the formulas for
F
and
W
in (
221.1
), and a
similar formula for
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
in terms of the subspaces
\bar{I}^{i,j}
, this is enough to
conclude that
R(W_{-2})
is a sub-mixed Hodge structure.
∎

232
.

With this notation, we have
\bar{H}-H\in R\bigl(W_{-2}\End(V)\bigr)
. According
to
36
, there is a unique element
g\in\GL(V)
such that

\bar{H}=gHg^{-1}\quad\text{and}\quad g-\id\in R\bigl(W_{-2}\End(V)\bigr).

(232.1)

This gives another way to think about the characteristic property of Deligne’s splitting
H
.

233
.

In a Hodge structure of weight
n
, one has
V=F^{0}\oplus\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{n+1}
. It turns
out that a mixed Hodge structure still determines a canonical complement for the
subspace
F^{0}
.

Proposition 18
.

In any mixed Hodge structure, one has

V=F^{0}\oplus\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{n+1}W_{n}.

In terms of Deligne’s decomposition, one has

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{n+1}W_{n}=\bigoplus_{i\leq-1,j}I^{i,j}.

Proof.

From the formula for
I^{i,j}
in (
221.2
), it is clear that
I^{i,j}\subseteq\sum_{n}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{n+1}W_{n}
as long as
i\leq-1
. Since we already know from
(
221.1
) that

V=F^{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\leq-1,j}I^{i,j},

it is therefore enough to prove that
F^{0}\cap\sum_{n}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{n+1}W_{n}=0
. This is
easily checked by projecting to the subquotients
\gr_{n}^{W}
in descending order.
∎

9.3
Mixed Hodge structures and hermitian pairings

234
.

Let us briefly discuss what happens when the vector space
V
comes equipped with a
nondegenerate hermitian pairing
Q\colon V\otimes\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}\to\mathbb{C}
. The pairing induces
an isomorphism

Q\colon V\to\Hom(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}},\mathbb{C}),\quad v\mapsto Q(v,-),

between
V
and the conjugate dual vector space. We make the assumption that

Q\colon V\to\Hom(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}},\mathbb{C})(-n)

is actually an isomorphism of mixed Hodge structures (for a certain integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}
).
Since the role of the two filtrations
F
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
get swapped in the mixed Hodge
structure on
\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}
, this amounts concretely to the following two conditions:

1.

We have
W_{k}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in W_{2n-k-1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}
.

2.

We have
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in F^{n-q+1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}
.

They imply that, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the hermitian pairing
Q
induces an
isomorphism of Hodge structures between
\gr_{k}^{W}
and the conjugate dual of

\gr_{2n-k}^{W}
(with a Tate twist by
n
).

235
.

Just as in
71
, the presence of the pairing turns
\End(V)
into a real
mixed Hodge structure. The real structure is given by

\mathfrak{g}=\bigl\{\thinspace A\in\End(V)\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace A^{\dagger}=-A\thinspace\bigr\},

the Lie algebra of the real Lie group
G
. The “complex conjugate” of an
endomorphism
A\in\End(V)
is again
\sigma(A)=-A^{\dagger}
, where the dagger
means the adjoint with respect to
Q
. The two conditions in the previous paragraph
are saying that
\sigma\bigl(W_{k}\End(V)\bigr)=W_{k}\End(V)
and that
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}^{q}\End(V)=\sigma\bigl(F^{q}\End(V)\bigr)
, and so
\End(V)
is indeed a real mixed
Hodge structure.

236
.

From
15
, we get a splitting
H\in\End(V)
of the mixed Hodge
structure. By swapping the role of the two filtrations
F
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}
, we get another
splitting
\bar{H}\in\End(V)
.

Lemma 37
.

We have
\bar{H}=2n\id-H^{\dagger}
, where the dagger means the adjoint with respect to

Q
.

Proof.

Since the splitting in
15
is functorial, this follows from the
fact that
Q\colon V\to\Hom(\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}},\mathbb{C})(-n)
is an isomorphism of mixed Hodge
structures.
∎

237
.

By averaging the two splittings
H
and
\bar{H}=2n\id-H^{\dagger}
, we can create a
splitting that is “real”, meaning compatible with the hermitian pairing
Q
.

Proposition 19
.

The operator
H_{\mathbb{R}}=\frac{1}{2}(H-H^{\dagger})\in\mathfrak{g}
is the unique real
splitting of the shifted weight filtration
W_{n+\bullet}
with the property
that

H_{\mathbb{R}}-(H-n\id)\in R\bigl(W_{-2}\End(V)\bigr).

Proof.

The operator
\frac{1}{2}(H+\bar{H})-n\id=\frac{1}{2}(H-H^{\dagger})\in\mathfrak{g}
preserves
the weight filtration, and acts on
\gr_{n+k}^{W}
as multiplication by
k
; it is
therefore a splitting of the shifted weight filtration
W_{n+\bullet}
. Recall
from
17
that
\bar{H}-H\in R\bigl(W_{-2}\End(V)\bigr)
.
The proof of uniqueness is the same as in
15
.
∎

10
The limiting mixed Hodge structure

238
.

The purpose of this chapter is show that the vector space
V
of multi-valued flat
sections has a mixed Hodge structure on it; following Schmid, we shall call this the

limiting mixed Hodge structure
. Before stating the result, we briefly recall
the definition of a (complex) mixed Hodge structure.

10.1
The limiting
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure

239
.

We are going to derive the existence of the limiting mixed Hodge structure from a more
precise result. Recall that, after having chosen a splitting
H\in\End(V)
for the
monodromy weight filtration, we get a unique representation

\rho\colon\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})\to\End(V)

with the property that
\rho(\mathsf{H})=H
and
\rho(\mathsf{Y})=-N
. It turns out that this
representation is part of a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure. The “total” Hodge
filtration of this
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure is obtained from the rescaled period
mapping

\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}\colon\mathbb{H}\to D,\quad\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert H}e^{-\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}\Phi(z).

In
Section

8
, we showed that
\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}
converges to a well-defined limit

e^{-N}F_{H}=\lim_{\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty}\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)\in D,

which is the Hodge filtration of a polarized Hodge structure of weight
n
on the
vector space
V
(with polarization
Q
). The filtration
F_{H}
has the property that

HF_{H}^{\bullet}\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\qquad T_{s}F_{H}^{\bullet}\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet},\qquad NF_{H}^{\bullet}\subseteq F_{H}^{\bullet-1}.

(239.1)

Since
e^{\mathsf{Y}}F_{H}
is the Hodge filtration of a polarized Hodge structure of weight

n
, the criterion in
12
shows that
F_{H}
is the
total Hodge filtration of a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
,
polarized by the hermitian pairing
Q
. Moreover, by

4
, the semisimple part
T_{s}
is necessarily an
endomorphism of this polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure.

240
.

We observed earlier that the conjugate Hodge filtration is
e^{-\mathsf{Y}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}
, where

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,x)=0$ for all $x\in F^{n-q+1}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

It follows that the two “total” Hodge filtrations of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure
are
F_{H}
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}
, and so the Hodge structure on the weight space
V_{k}
is given
by

V_{k}^{i,j}=V_{k}\cap F_{H}^{i}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}^{j}.

In particular, this says that
\mathsf{Y}(V_{k}^{i,j})\subseteq V_{k-2}^{i-1,j-1}
and

\mathsf{X}(V_{k}^{i,j})\subseteq V_{k+2}^{i+1,j+1}
; especially the second inclusion is not
at all obvious from the conditions in (
239.1
).

10.2
The limiting mixed Hodge structure

241
.

In terms of the limiting Hodge filtrations
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}
coming from the
nilpotent orbit theorem, one can describe
F_{H}
and its conjugate as

F_{H}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xH}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{xH}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}.

The effect of the limit is that, under the isomorphism
E_{k}(H)\cong W_{k}/W_{k-1}
, one has

F_{H}^{p}\cap E_{k}(H)\cong\bigl(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}\cap W_{k}+W_{k-1}\bigr)/W_{k-1},

and similarly for the conjugate filtration
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}
. This means that the passage from

(F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})
to
(F_{H},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H})
is exactly the passage from a mixed Hodge
structure to its associated graded.

Corollary 8
.

The three filtrations
W_{\bullet-n}(N)
,
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}
determine a mixed
Hodge structure on the vector space
V
. The associated graded of this mixed Hodge
structure is a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
. The operators

N\colon V\to V(-1)\quad\text{and}\quad T_{s}\colon V\to V

are morphisms of mixed Hodge structures.

Proof.

Set
W_{k}=W_{k}(N)
. The first assertion is clear because each weight space

V_{k}=E_{k}(H)\cong W_{k}/W_{k-1}

has a Hodge structure of weight
n+k
. The second assertion follows because we
have
N(W_{k})\subseteq W_{k-2}
and
T_{s}(W_{k})\subseteq W_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
and also
N(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p-1}
and
T_{s}(F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p})\subseteq F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}

for all
p\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

10.3
A formula for the period mapping

242
.

It is sometimes useful to have a more explicit description of the period mapping. At
least on a small neighborhood of the origin, we can write down a fairly concrete
formula for
\Phi(z)
with the help of the limiting mixed Hodge structure. For
variations of Hodge structure with unipotent monodromy, this result can be found in

[
CK
, (2.5)]
.

243
.

The nilpotent orbit theorem (in
5
) tell us that

\Psi_{S}(e^{z})=e^{-z(S+N)}\Phi(z)

converges to
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
. Since
\Psi_{S}(t)
is
holomorphic, we should therefore be able to express
\Psi_{S}(t)
in terms of

\Psi_{S}(0)
, using functions that are holomorphic on a neighborhood of the origin. To
do this, recall the formula for the holomorphic tangent space

T_{\Psi_{S}(0)}^{1,0}\check{D}\cong\End(V)/F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)}.

We can use the limiting mixed Hodge structure to find a subalgebra
\mathfrak{q}\subseteq\End(V)
such that

\End(V)=\mathfrak{q}\oplus F^{0}\End(V)_{\Psi_{S}(0)}.

(243.1)

Recall that
V
has a mixed Hodge structure with Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and weight
filtration
W_{\bullet-n}
, and that the semisimple operator
T_{s}
is an endomorphism
of this mixed Hodge structure. Consider the induced mixed Hodge structure on

\End(V)
. According to the general result in
18
, the subspace

\mathfrak{q}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}^{n+1}W_{n}\End(V)

is a vector space complement of
F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{0}\End(V)
, and hence

\End(V)=\mathfrak{q}\oplus F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{0}\End(V).

(243.2)

It is also clearly a subalgebra, and we have
N\in\mathfrak{q}
because
N\colon V\to V(-1)
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures. Moreover, since
S
is an
endomorphism of the mixed Hodge structure, the operator
\ad S
preserves
\mathfrak{q}
;
this gives us a decomposition

\mathfrak{q}=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha},

where
\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha}=\mathfrak{q}\cap E_{\alpha}(\ad S)
. This decomposition is
compatible with the Lie algebra structure on
\End(V)
, in the sense that

[\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha},\mathfrak{q}_{\beta}]\subseteq\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha+\beta}
by
the Jacobi identity.

244
.

Now (
243.1
) follows from (
243.2
) by a
formal argument. Let
\alpha_{1}>\dotsb>\alpha_{m}
be the distinct
eigenvalues of
\ad S
on
\End(V)
, in decreasing order. Setting
\lambda_{k}=e^{2\pi i\alpha_{k}}
, we have

\frac{E_{\alpha_{1}}(\ad S)\oplus\dotsb\oplus E_{\alpha_{k-1}}(\ad S)\oplus E_{\alpha_{k}}(\ad S)}{E_{\alpha_{1}}(\ad S)\oplus\dotsb\oplus E_{\alpha_{k-1}}(\ad S)}\cong E_{\lambda_{k}}(\Ad T_{s}),

and under this isomorphism, the filtration induced by
\Psi_{S}(0)
on the left-hand
side goes to the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. We can then derive
(
243.1
) by induction on
k=1,\dotsc,m
.

245
.

Since
T_{\Psi_{S}(0)}^{1,0}\check{D}\cong\mathfrak{q}
, the exponential mapping

\exp\colon\mathfrak{q}\to\check{D},\quad A\mapsto e^{A}\cdot\Psi_{S}(0),

restricts to a biholomorphism between a small neighborhood of
0\in\mathfrak{q}
and a
small neighborhood of the point
\Psi_{S}(0)\in\check{D}
. Consequently, there is a
constant
\varepsilon>0
, whose exact value depends on the period mapping under
consideration, and a unique holomorphic mapping

\tilde{\Gamma}\colon\Delta_{\varepsilon}\to\mathfrak{q}

such that
\tilde{\Gamma}(0)=0
and
\Psi_{S}(t)=e^{\tilde{\Gamma}(t)}\cdot\Psi_{S}(0)
for

\lvert t\rvert<\varepsilon
. As long as
\operatorname{Re}z<\log\varepsilon
, this gives us a (preliminary)
formula for the period mapping:

\Phi(z)=e^{z(S+N)}e^{\tilde{\Gamma}(e^{z})}\cdot\Psi_{S}(0)

(245.1)

Note that, unlike in
[
CK
, (2.5)]
, the relevant filtration here is
not
the
limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, but the filtration
\Psi_{S}(0)
coming from the
nilpotent orbit theorem.

246
.

Now we would like to derive from (
245.1
) a formula for
\Phi(z)

that only involves the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. The key is the following lemma.

Lemma 38
.

One has
\Psi_{S}(0)=e^{A}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
for a unique element
A\in\mathfrak{q}
.
Decomposing
A=\sum_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}
, with
A_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha}
,
one has
A_{\alpha}=0
for
\alpha\leq 0
.

Proof.

As before, the exponential mapping
A\mapsto e^{A}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}
gives a biholomorphism
between a neighborhood of
0\in\mathfrak{q}
and a neighborhood of the point

F_{\mathrm{lim}}\in\check{D}
. Recall that
F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)
. For

x\gg 0
, it follows that there is an element
B(x)\in\mathfrak{q}

such that
e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)=e^{B(x)}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. Since
S
preserves the filtration

F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, we can rewrite this as

\Psi_{S}(0)=e^{xS}e^{B(x)}e^{-xS}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{A}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}},

where
A=e^{x\ad(S)}B(x)\in\mathfrak{q}
is necessarily constant. Now we decompose
this constant endomorphism as
A=\sum_{\alpha}A_{\alpha}
, with
A_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{q}\cap E_{\alpha}(\ad S)
. Then

e^{-xS}\Psi_{S}(0)=e^{-xS}e^{A}e^{xS}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\exp\left(\sum_{\alpha}e^{-\alpha x}A_{\alpha}\right)\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

As
x\to\infty
, this can only converge to
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
if
A_{\alpha}=0
for

\alpha\leq 0
.
∎

247
.

Putting the lemma and (
245.1
) together, we find that

\Phi(z)=e^{z(S+N)}e^{\tilde{\Gamma}(e^{z})}e^{A}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{z(S+N)}e^{\Gamma(e^{z})}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}},

where
\Gamma\colon\Delta_{\varepsilon}\to\mathfrak{q}
is holomorphic and satisfies
\Gamma(0)=A
. We can do even better by decomposing
\Gamma(t)=\sum_{\alpha}\Gamma_{\alpha}(t)
; each individual function
\Gamma_{\alpha}\colon\Delta_{\varepsilon}\to\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha}
is holomorphic, and
\Gamma_{\alpha}(0)=0
for
\alpha\leq 0
.
Since
S
preserves the filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, we finally get

\Phi(z)=e^{zN}\cdot e^{zS}e^{\Gamma(e^{z})}e^{-zS}\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{zN}\cdot\exp\left(\sum_{\alpha}e^{\alpha z}\,\Gamma_{\alpha}(e^{z})\right)\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

To summarize, we have proved the following result.

Proposition 20
.

There is a small positive number
\varepsilon>0
, and a collection of holomorphic
functions
\Gamma_{\alpha}\colon\Delta_{\varepsilon}\to\mathfrak{q}_{\alpha}
with

\Gamma_{\alpha}(0)=0
for
\alpha\leq 0
, such that the formula

\Phi(z)=e^{zN}\cdot\exp\left(\sum_{\alpha}e^{\alpha z}\,\Gamma_{\alpha}(e^{z})\right)\cdot F_{\mathrm{lim}}

describes the period mapping on the half-plane
\operatorname{Re}z<\log\varepsilon
.

Note that each term in the sum vanishes as
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\to\infty
. The rate at
which this happens is controlled by the smallest positive eigenvalue of
\ad S
; but
this is the smallest distance among consecutive eigenvalues of
S
, which we had
earlier denoted by
\delta(T)
. This shows one more time that
e^{-zN}\Phi(z)
approaches
its limit
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
at a rate proportional to
e^{-\delta(T)\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert}
.

11
Asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric and the

\SL(2)
-orbit theorem

248
.

In this chapter, we study the rate of convergence of the rescaled period mapping, and
prove a cheap version of Schmid’s famous
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem

[
Schmid
, Thm. 5.13]
.

249
.

On the vector space
V
, we have the limiting Hodge filtration
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
, as well as
the conjugate filtration
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}
, defined by

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,w)=0$ for all $w\in F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{n+1-q}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

These put a mixed Hodge structure on
V
, with weight filtration

W_{\bullet-n}=W_{\bullet-n}(N)
; moreover,
N\colon V\to V(-1)
is a morphism of
mixed Hodge structures. At the same time,
V
also has a polarized
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge
structure of weight
n
, whose two Hodge filtrations are
F_{H}
and
\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}
,
where

\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}^{q}=\bigl\{\thinspace v\in V\thinspace\thinspace\big|\thinspace\thinspace\text{$Q(v,w)=0$ for all $w\in F_{H}^{n+1-q}$}\thinspace\bigr\}.

250
.

We begin by comparing the two filtrations
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and
F_{H}
.

Lemma 39
.

There is an element
h\in\GL(V)
with
h-\id\in W_{-1}\End(V)
and
hNh^{-1}=N
, such that
F_{\mathrm{lim}}=hF_{H}
.

Proof.

Let
H(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})
denote Deligne’s splitting of
the limiting mixed Hodge structure (as in
15
). The corresponding
subspaces

I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}=F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i}\cap W_{i+j-n}\cap\bigl(\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}^{j}\cap W_{i+j-n}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}^{j-1}\cap W_{i+j-2-n}+\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}^{j-2}\cap W_{i+j-3-n}+\dotsb\bigr)

decompose
V
into a direct sum, in such a way that

W_{k}=\bigoplus_{i+j\leq n+k}I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}=\bigoplus_{i\geq p,j}I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j};

moreover, one has
N(I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j})\subseteq I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i-1,j-1}
, due to the fact that

N\colon V\to V(-1)
is a morphism of mixed Hodge structures. In terms of the
splitting,
[H(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}}),N]=-2N
.

Recall that in an
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
n
, one has

V_{k}^{i,j}=F_{H}^{i}\cap V_{k}\cap\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{H}^{j},

whenever
i+j=n+k
. Under the projection from
W_{i+j-n}
to
\gr_{i+j-n}^{W}
, the
two subspaces
I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}
and
V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}
both map isomorphically to the

(i,j)
-subspace in the Hodge decomposition of
\gr_{i+j-n}^{W}
, and so there is a
unique isomorphism
h^{i,j}
making the following diagram commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigl(\gr_{i+j-n}^{W}\bigr)^{i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Consequently, there is a unique automorphism
h\in\GL(V)
such that
h\big|_{V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}}=h^{i,j}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. It follows that

h(F_{H}^{p})=\bigoplus_{i\geq p}h\bigl(V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}\bigr)=\bigoplus_{i\geq p}I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}=F_{\mathrm{lim}}^{p}.

By construction,
h
preserves the weight filtration
W
and acts as the identity
on each
\gr_{k}^{W}
; therefore
h-\id\in W_{-1}\End(V)
. Moreover, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i+j-n}^{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i,j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i+j-2-n}^{i-1,j-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{\mathrm{lim}}^{i-1,j-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i,j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{i-1,j-1}}

is commutative, and this implies that
hN=Nh
. In terms of the two splittings,
we have

H(W_{\bullet-n},\overline{F}\vphantom{F},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})=h\bigl(H+n\id\bigr)h^{-1},

which is consistent with
36
.
∎

251
.

Note that, in the lemma above, the splitting
H
was arbitrary, subject only to the
conditions in
9
. If we pick a splitting that is adapted to the
limiting mixed Hodge structure, we can get a much better result. Indeed, suppose we
use instead the splitting
H_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})\in\mathfrak{g}

for the filtration
W_{\bullet}
, constructed in
19
, which
we got by averaging the two splittings
H(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})
and

H(W_{\bullet-n},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}},F_{\mathrm{lim}})
. This also has all the properties required by

9
. Then

H_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})-\bigl(H(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})-n\id\bigr)\in R\bigl(W_{-2}\End(V)\bigr),

and so the element
h\in\GL(V)
in
39
even satisfies
h-\id\in W_{-2}\End(V)
.

252
.

We return to our investigation of the two filtrations
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and
F_{H}
. Let us
write the element
h\in\GL(V)
as a finite sum

h=\id+h_{-1}+h_{-2}+\dotsb,

where each
h_{-k}\in E_{-k}(\ad H)\cap\ker(\ad N)
. Recall from
210

that

F_{H}=\lim_{x\to\infty}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}.

We can analyze the convergence more precisely as follows. For
x>0
, one has

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}hF_{H}=e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}he^{-\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}F_{H},

due to the fact that
H
preserves the filtration
F_{H}
. Now

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}he^{-\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}=\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x^{-k/2}h_{-k},

and since
[N,h_{-k}]=0
, we finally obtain

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}e^{-xN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{-N}e^{\frac{1}{2}\log x\,H}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}x^{-k/2}h_{-k}\right)\cdot F_{\sharp},

where
F_{\sharp}=e^{-N}F_{H}\in D
. Note that the filtration on the right-hand side
belongs to
D
when
x\gg 0
. With the substitution
u=x^{-1/2}
, we can rewrite
this as

\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}h_{-k}\right)\cdot F_{\sharp},

as long as
u
is sufficiently close to
0
.

253
.

To understand the behavior of the Hodge metric, we have to express this in terms of
the real group
G
. For simplicity, let us denote by

V=\bigoplus_{p+q=n}V^{p,q}

the Hodge structure with Hodge filtration
F_{\sharp}=e^{-N}F_{H}
, and by

\End(V)=\bigoplus_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\End(V)^{j,-j}

the induced Hodge structure of weight
0
on
\End(V)
. Recall that this is an
\mathbb{R}
-Hodge
structure; the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
of the real group
G
gives the real structure. Setting

\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{g}\cap\bigoplus_{j\neq 0}\End(V)^{j,-j}

we then have
\End(V)=\mathfrak{m}\oplus F^{0}\End(V)
; compare
(
76.2
). In concrete terms, this decomposition works as
follows. For an endomorphism
A\in\End(V)
, denote by
A_{j}\in\End(V)^{j,-j}
the
components in the Hodge decomposition; then

\sum_{j<0}\bigl(A_{j}-(A_{j})^{\dagger}\bigr)\in\mathfrak{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{j\geq 0}A_{j}+\sum_{j<0}(A_{j})^{\dagger}\in F^{0}\End(V)

254
.

Recall that the subspace
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\End(V)
maps isomorphically to the tangent
space of
D
at the point
F_{\sharp}
. The exponential mapping

\End(V)\to D,\quad A\mapsto e^{A}\cdot F_{\sharp},

is real analytic, and therefore restricts to a real-analytic diffeomorphism between a
neighborhood of the origin in
\mathfrak{m}
and a neighborhood of the point
F_{\sharp}\in D
,
it follows that there is a small positive number
\varepsilon>0
and two unique real
analytic functions

B\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathfrak{m}\quad\text{and}\quad C\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to F^{0}\End(V)

with the property that
B(0)=C(0)=0
and

\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}h_{-k}\right)e^{C(u)}=e^{B(u)}.

Let us expand
B
and
C
into convergent power series of the form

B(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}B_{-k}\quad\text{and}\quad C(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}C_{-k},

where
B_{-k}\in\mathfrak{m}
and
C_{-k}\in F^{0}\End(V)
.

255
.

From the power series expansion of

e^{B(u)}=\id+uB_{-1}+u^{2}\Bigl(B_{-2}+\frac{1}{2}B_{-1}^{2}\Bigr)+\dotsb,

we see that there are universal non-commutative polynomials
P_{-k}
in the variables

B_{-1},B_{-2},\dotsc
, such that

e^{B(u)}=\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}P_{-k}(B_{-1},\dotsc,B_{-k}).

The first few terms are easily computed to be

\displaystyle P_{-1}(B_{-1})

\displaystyle=B_{-1},

\displaystyle P_{-2}(B_{-1},B_{-2})

\displaystyle=B_{-2}+\frac{1}{2}B_{-1}^{2}

\displaystyle P_{-3}(B_{-1},B_{-2},B_{-3})

\displaystyle=B_{-3}+\frac{1}{2}B_{-2}B_{-1}+\frac{1}{2}B_{-1}B_{-2}+\frac{1}{6}B_{-1}^{3}.

In general, the coefficients of
k!P_{-k}
are positive integers; moreover,
P_{-k}

is homogeneous of degree
k
, if we declare that the degree of
B_{-j}
is
j
. It
is easy to see that the variable
B_{-k}
always appears in
P_{-k}
with coefficient
1
.

256
.

From the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure on
V
, the vector space
\End(V)
inherits an

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}
-Hodge structure of weight
0
. In particular, the Lie algebra

\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
\End(V)
, and therefore induces a decomposition

\End(V)\cong\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{N}}S_{m}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Hom_{\mathbb{C}}\bigl(S_{m},\End(V)\bigr)^{\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})}.

The summand with
S_{m}
is called the
\boldsymbol{S_{m}}
-isotypical
component
. According
to the discussion in
104
, the isotypical components are sub-Hodge
structures of the Hodge structure on
\End(V)
. They are also stable under taking
adjoints; therefore the decomposition

\End(V)=\mathfrak{m}\oplus F^{0}\End(V)

respects the isotypical components.

257
.

Since each operator
h_{-k}\in E_{-k}(\ad H)\cap\ker(\ad N)
is primitive, it
obviously belongs to the
S_{k}
-isotypical component of the representation. This has
the following consequence for the operators
B_{-k},C_{-k}\in\End(V)
.

Proposition 21
.

For each
k\geq 1
, both
B_{-k}
and
C_{-k}
belong to the sum of the

S_{j}
-isotypical components with
j=k,k-2,k-4,\dotsc
.

Proof.

Expanding the relation

\displaystyle\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}P_{-k}(B_{-1},\dotsc,B_{-k})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}h_{-k}\right)

\displaystyle\left(\id+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}P_{-k}(C_{-1},\dotsc,C_{-k})\right)

from above now gives us the following series of identities:

P_{-k}(B_{-1},\dotsc,B_{-k})=h_{-k}+P_{-k}(C_{-1},\dotsc,C_{-k})+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}h_{-k+j}\cdot P_{-j}(C_{-1},\dotsc,C_{-j})

For
k=1
, this reads
B_{-1}=h_{-1}+C_{-1}
; here
B_{-1}\in\mathfrak{m}
and

C_{-1}\in F^{0}\End(V)
. Remembering that
\End(V)=\mathfrak{m}\oplus F^{0}\End(V)
,
we see again that this equation uniquely determines
B_{-1}
and
C_{-1}
. Since

h_{-1}
belongs to the
S_{1}
-isotypical
component of the representation, and since this is a sub-Hodge structure, it
follows that
B_{-1}
and
C_{-1}
also belong to the
S_{1}
-isotypical component.

We can now prove the assertion by induction on
k\geq 1
. Rearranging the
identity from above, we obtain an identity of the form

B_{-k}-C_{-k}=P(B_{-1},\dotsc,B_{-k+1},C_{-1},\dotsc,C_{-k+1},h_{-1},\dotsc,h_{-k})

for a certain non-commutative polynomial
P
, homogeneous of degree
k
.
Now each term on the right-hand side is a product of operators of the form
B_{-j}
,

C_{-j}
or
h_{-j}
, and by induction, each such operator is in the direct
sum of the
S_{j-2i}
-isotypical components for
i\geq 0
. Since

S_{k}\otimes S_{\ell}\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{\min(k,\ell)}S_{k+\ell-2i},

and since the degree of each term is
-k
, this gives the result.
∎

258
.

This has the following nice consequence for the nilpotent orbit
e^{zN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}
. The
result is of course less precise than the
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem

[
Schmid
, Thm. 5.13]
, but in return, the proof is
much
easier, and we
believe that the version below is actually sufficient for all practical purposes.

Theorem 19
.

There is a constant
\varepsilon>0
, whose value depends on
F_{\mathrm{lim}}
and
N
, and a
real-analytic function
B\colon(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathfrak{g}
, such that

\Phi_{\mathrm{nil}}(x+iy)=e^{(x+iy)N}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{iyN}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,H}e^{B(\lvert x\rvert^{-1/2})}\cdot e^{-N}F_{H}

as long as
\lvert x\rvert>1/\varepsilon^{2}
. In the power series expansion

B(u)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}u^{k}B_{-k},

each coefficient
B_{-k}\in\mathfrak{g}
belongs to the direct sum of the

S_{m}
-isotypical components of the
\mathfrak{sl}_{2}(\mathbb{C})
-representation with
m=k,k-2,k-4,\dotsc
.

The point is that all the operators on the right-hand side of this formula belong
to the real group
G
. This makes it possible to describe the asymptotic behavior of
the Hodge metric or the Weil operator in any nilpotent orbit.

Note
.

Instead of an arbitrary splitting
H\in\End(V)
, we can use the splitting

H_{\mathbb{R}}(W_{\bullet-n},F_{\mathrm{lim}},\overline{F}\vphantom{F}_{\mathrm{lim}})\in\mathfrak{g}

given by
19
. Then
h-\id\in W_{-2}\End(V)
, and an
analysis of the proof shows that
B_{-1}=0
. With this choice, the statement of

19
becomes closer to the full
\SL(2)
-orbit theorem in

[
Schmid
, Thm. 5.13]
.

259
.

We can use
19
to get more precise information about the
asymptotic behavior of the Hodge metric. The Hodge norm estimates tell us the exact
rate of growth of
\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}
for any
v\in V
, but so far, we have not
said anything about the behavior of the inner products
\langle v,w\rangle_{\Phi(z)}

when
v,w\in V
are two different multi-valued flat sections.

260
.

Let us denote by
\langle v,w\rangle
the inner product coming from the polarized Hodge
structure
F_{\sharp}=e^{-N}F_{H}
. Set
z=x+iy
. According to

17
, we have

d_{D}\bigl(\Phi(x+iy),e^{(x+iy)N}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\bigr)\leq C\lvert x\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert x\rvert}

as long as
x\leq x_{0}
, with constants
C>0
and
x_{0}<0

that are basically independent of the period mapping in question. Since the
exponential factors in the definition of
\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}
belong to the real Lie
group
G
, it follows that

d_{D}\Bigl(\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(x+iy),\,e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,H}e^{xN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}\Bigr)\leq C\lvert x\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert x\rvert}.

We now restrict our attention to
\lvert x\rvert>1/\varepsilon^{2}
, and set
u=\lvert x\rvert^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Then

e^{\frac{1}{2}\log\lvert x\rvert\,H}e^{xN}F_{\mathrm{lim}}=e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp},

and so our distance estimate becomes

d_{D}\Bigl(\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(x+iy),\,e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp}\Bigr)\leq C\lvert x\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert x\rvert}.

13
allows us to conclude that, as long as

\lvert x\rvert
is sufficiently large,

\Bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(x+iy)}-\langle v,w\rangle_{e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp}}\Bigr\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\lVert v\rVert_{e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp}}\lVert w\rVert_{e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp}}\cdot\lvert x\rvert^{m}e^{-\delta(T)\lvert x\rvert}

for some constant
C^{\prime}>0
that is independent of
v,w\in V
.

261
.

Now suppose that
v\in E_{k}(H)
and
w\in E_{\ell}(H)
belong to possibly different weight
spaces, say with
k\leq\ell
. The behavior of the inner product

\langle v,w\rangle_{e^{B(u)}F_{\sharp}}=\langle e^{-B(u)}v,e^{-B(u)}w\rangle

is very easy to understand, given what we know about the coefficients of the series

B(u)
. The whole expression is a convergent power series in
u
. Since different
eigenspaces of
H
are orthogonal under the inner product
\langle v,w\rangle
, the leading
term in this series is divisible by
u^{\ell-k}
. It follows that there is a constant

C>0
such that for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
, one has

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)}\bigr\rvert\leq C\lVert v\rVert\lVert w\rVert\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-(\ell-k)/2}.

(261.1)

From the definition of the rescaled period mapping in (
21.1
), we get

\bigl\langle v,\,w\bigr\rangle_{\hat{\Phi}_{S,H}(z)}=\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{-(k+\ell)/2}\cdot\bigl\langle e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}v,\,e^{\frac{1}{2}(z-\mathchoice{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}}{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}})(S+N)}w\bigr\rangle_{\Phi(z)},

and as long as
\operatorname{Im}z
remains bounded, it follows that

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{\Phi(z)}\bigr\rvert\leq C^{\prime}\lVert v\rVert\lVert w\rVert\cdot\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}

(261.2)

for a different constant
C^{\prime}>0
and
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
.

262
.

We know from the Hodge norm estimates that
\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}
and

\lVert w\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}
are of order
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{k}
and
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert^{\ell}
,
respectively. Consequently, (
261.2
) is equivalent to the statement that,
for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
, one has

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{\Phi(z)}\bigr\rvert\leq C^{\prime\prime}\min\bigl(\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2},\lVert w\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\bigr).

Because of the triangle inequality, we deduce that for any two multi-valued flat
sections
v,w\in V
, there is a constant
C(v,w)>0
such that

\bigl\lvert\langle v,w\rangle_{\Phi(z)}\bigr\rvert\leq C(v,w)\cdot\min\Bigl(\lVert v\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2},\lVert w\rVert_{\Phi(z)}^{2}\Bigr)

for
\lvert\operatorname{Re}z\rvert\gg 0
. This finishes the proof of
9
from
the introduction.
